Topic 6 of 10 • Internal Medicine • Nephrology & Urology

6. Urinary Tract Infections

🔥 High Yield (25×) 8 Qs

1. Lower vs. Upper UTI Spectrum & Treatment Guidelines 🔥 x24

Parameter Acute Uncomplicated Cystitis 🔥 x12 Acute Pyelonephritis 🔥 x12
Clinical Presentation Dysuria, increased urinary frequency, urgency, suprapubic pain; Completely AFEBRILE, NO flank pain, NO systemic symptoms High fever with rigors, flank pain, Costovertebral Angle (CVA) tenderness, nausea, vomiting, malaise ± lower urinary tract symptoms
Urinalysis & Pathogens Positive leukocyte esterase, positive nitrites (Gram-negative coliforms), >5–10 WBCs/hpf; Escherichia coli (>80%), Staphylococcus saprophyticus (in young sexually active females) Pyuria, bacteriuria + Pathognomonic White Blood Cell (WBC) Casts (confirms upper tract renal parenchymal inflammation)
First-Line Antimicrobial Regimens 1. Nitrofurantoin: 100 mg PO BID for 5 days
2. TMP-SMX (Bactrim DS): 1 tab PO BID for 3 days (if local resistance <20%)
3. Fosfomycin: 3g single oral dose		 • Outpatient (mild): Oral Ciprofloxacin (500 mg BID for 7 days) or Levofloxacin
• Inpatient (severe/toxic/pregnant): IV Ceftriaxone (1g daily) or IV Fluoroquinolone
  • Asymptomatic Bacteriuria Mandatory Screening & Treatment Indications: 🔥 x4
    • 1. Pregnant Women: Screen at 12–16 weeks gestation; treat with Amoxicillin-clavulanate, Cephalexin, or Nitrofurantoin (untreated bacteriuria leads to acute pyelonephritis, preterm labor, and low birth weight).
    • 2. Patients Undergoing Invasive Urologic Procedures: Where mucosal bleeding is expected (e.g., TURP). (Contraindicated to screen/treat in asymptomatic elderly or catheterized patients!).
  • Pediatric Febrile UTI Workup (Q316): Infant or toddler (<24 months) presenting with unexplained high fever (≥39°C), irritability, and poor feeding → Priority mandatory diagnostic test: Culture of urine obtained by transurethral catheterization (or suprapubic aspiration). If confirmed, perform Renal Ultrasound + Voiding Cystourethrogram (VCUG) to assess for Vesicoureteral Reflux (VUR). x2
⚠️ Model Answer vs. AI Recommended Answer

تنبيه هام للامتحان بخصوص تشخيص حمى الرضع المجهولة (سؤال Q316):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: طفلة رضيعة تبلغ 16 شهراً تعاني من حمى شديدة مفاجئة (39.4°C) مع بكاء وسوء تغذية وألم خفيف بالبطن دون أي بؤرة ظاهرة للعدوى. التوصيات الإكلينيكية المعتمدة (AAP Guidelines) تجعل الخطوة التشخيصية الإلزامية الأولى لنفي عدوى المسالك البولية الخفية (Occult UTI) هي سحب عينة بول بقسطرة معقمة وعمل مزرعة Culture of urine obtained by transurethral catheterization (Option B).

في الـ Model Answer في بنك الأسئلة: تم تسجيل الإجابة بالخطأ Option A (Abdominal radiographs) على الرغم من أن الأشعة السينية لا تشخص أسباب الحمى البولية عند الرضع.

Topic 7 of 10 • Internal Medicine • Nephrology & Urology

7. Urolithiasis

🔥 High Yield (28×) 9 Qs

1. Renal Calculi Classification & Characteristics Comparison 🔥 x28

Stone Type Prevalence & Radiopacity Etiology & Crystal Morphology Specific Medical Management
Calcium Oxalate 🔥 x12 Most common (75–80%); Radiopaque on plain X-ray Hypercalciuria, hyperoxaluria (Crohn's disease, enteric malabsorption); acidic/neutral pH; Envelope- or dumbbell-shaped crystals Thiazide diuretics (reduce urinary calcium excretion), potassium citrate, low sodium diet; Maintain normal dietary calcium (binds oxalate in gut)
Struvite (Infection Stone) 🔥 x8 10–15%; Radiopaque; forms Staghorn Calculi filling renal calyces Infection with Urease-producing bacteria (Proteus mirabilis, Klebsiella) → hydrolyzes urea to ammonium → alkaline urine (pH >7.2); "Coffin-lid" crystals Complete surgical stone removal (Percutaneous Nephrolithotomy, PCNL) + eradication antibiotics
Uric Acid 🔥 x6 5–10%; RADIOLUCENT on plain KUB X-ray (visible on non-contrast CT!) Hyperuricosuria, gout, high-purine diet, Tumor Lysis Syndrome; Persistently acidic urine (pH <5.5); Rhomboid or needle crystals Urinary alkalinization with Potassium Citrate (target urine pH 6.5–7.0) + Allopurinol + high fluid intake → Medical dissolution of stones!
Cystine x2 1–2%; Faintly radiopaque (ground-glass) Autosomal recessive defect in renal tubular amino acid transporter (COLA); acidic urine; Pathognomonic Hexagonal crystals High fluid intake (>3–4 L/day), urinary alkalinization (potassium citrate), Tiopronin / Penicillamine for refractory cases

2. Acute Renal Colic Diagnostic Strategy & Emergency Decompression 🔥 x14

  • Clinical Hallmark: Sudden, agonizing, sharp colicky flank pain radiating along the course of the ureter to the groin, labium, or testicle, accompanied by nausea, vomiting, and microscopic or gross hematuria. 🔥 x5
  • Diagnostic Imaging Gold Standard: Non-Contrast Helical CT of Abdomen and Pelvis (NCCT) (sensitivity >98%; detects all stone compositions including radiolucent uric acid stones). Renal Ultrasound is preferred in pregnancy and children. 🔥 x4
  • Size-Based Management Algorithm: x3
    • Stone <5 mm: Spontaneous passage in >80–90% → Medical Expulsive Therapy (Alpha-1 blocker: Tamsulosin 0.4 mg daily) + oral hydration + NSAIDs (Ketorolac / Ibuprofen).
    • Stone 5–10 mm: Trial of expulsive therapy; Shock Wave Lithotripsy (ESWL) or Ureteroscopy if failure to pass after 4–6 weeks.
    • Stone >10–20 mm: Ureteroscopy with laser lithotripsy or Percutaneous Nephrolithotomy (PCNL for stones >20 mm).
  • EMERGENCY RULE: Obstructed Kidney with Superimposed Infection: x2
    • Ureteral calculus causing hydronephrosis with fever, rigors, or urosepsis is an absolute urologic emergency → Immediate Emergency Decompression via Retrograde Ureteral Stent (Double-J) or Percutaneous Nephrostomy Tube + IV broad-spectrum antibiotics. Definitive stone lithotripsy is STRICTLY CONTRAINDICATED in the presence of acute infection!
↑ Back to Top / جدول المحتويات
Topic 8 of 10 • Internal Medicine • Nephrology & Urology

8. Urologic Oncology & BPH

🔥 High Yield (34×) 12 Qs

1. Benign Prostatic Hyperplasia (BPH) vs. Prostate Carcinoma 🔥 x32

Feature Benign Prostatic Hyperplasia (BPH) 🔥 x16 Prostate Adenocarcinoma 🔥 x16
Anatomical Origin Arises in the Transition Zone surrounding the prostatic urethra → early lower urinary tract symptoms Arises in the Peripheral Zone (70–80%) → asymptomatic early; accessible on digital rectal exam
Digital Rectal Exam (DRE) Symmetrically enlarged, smooth, rubbery, firm, non-tender prostate with preserved median sulcus Hard, irregular, nodular, asymmetrical prostate with loss of the median sulcus
Clinical Symptoms LUTS: Obstructive (hesitancy, weak stream, intermittency, straining, terminal dribbling) + Irritative (frequency, urgency, nocturia) Usually asymptomatic in early stages; advanced: obstructive symptoms, hematuria, or bone pain (lumbar spine/pelvis)
Diagnostic Confirmation Clinical diagnosis; International Prostate Symptom Score (IPSS); post-void residual volume on ultrasound Transrectal Ultrasound (TRUS)-Guided Core Needle Biopsy (indicated for elevated PSA >4.0 ng/mL or suspicious DRE); Gleason grading score
Pharmacotherapy & Surgery • Alpha-1 Blockers (Tamsulosin, Alfuzosin): Relax bladder neck/prostatic smooth muscle (rapid relief in 48h)
• 5-Alpha-Reductase Inhibitors (Finasteride, Dutasteride): Block conversion of testosterone to DHT (reduces prostate size by 20–25% over 6–12m; ↓ PSA by 50%)
• Gold-Standard Surgery: Transurethral Resection of Prostate (TURP)		 • Localized: Radical Prostatectomy or External Beam Radiation
• Metastatic (Osteoblastic bone metastases): Androgen Deprivation Therapy (ADT: GnRH agonists Leuprolide ± Antiandrogens Bicalutamide)

2. Bladder & Renal Cell Carcinoma Clinical Highlights 🔥 x18

  • Bladder Carcinoma (Urothelial / Transitional Cell): 🔥 x10
    • Leading risk factor is Cigarette Smoking (>50% of cases), followed by occupational exposure to aromatic amines/aniline dyes.
    • Clinical Hallmark: Painless, intermittent gross hematuria in an older adult (>50y) is Bladder Cancer until proven otherwise!
    • Diagnostic Gold Standard: Flexible or Rigid Cystoscopy with biopsy (mandatory initial investigation to visualize mucosal lesions and resect for staging).
  • Renal Cell Carcinoma (RCC / Clear Cell): 🔥 x8
    • Originates from malignant proximal convoluted tubule epithelial cells; associated with smoking, obesity, and VHL syndrome.
    • Classic Triad (<10%): Flank pain + Palpable abdominal flank mass + Gross hematuria.
    • Left-Sided Varicocele: A left varicocele that fails to decompress when supine indicates renal vein tumor thrombus obstructing the left gonadal vein.
    • Paraneoplastic Syndromes: Polycythemia (↑ Erythropoietin), Hypercalcemia (↑ PTHrP), Hypertension (↑ Renin), Stauffer syndrome (reversible hepatic dysfunction). Confirm via Multiphasic Contrast Abdominal CT; Radical or partial nephrectomy.
⚠️ Model Answer vs. AI Recommended Answer

تنبيه هام للامتحان بخصوص أعراض الـ Hematuria في الإناث (سؤال Q534):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: ظهور بيلة دموية غير مؤلمة (Painless hematuria) مع زيادة مرات التبول في سيدة تبلغ 42 عاماً. في كبار السن والمدخنين يعتبر Painless hematuria علامة إنذارية لـ Bladder Carcinoma (Option B)، ولكن في الإناث الأصغر سناً مع أعراض تهيجية (Frequency) يكون السبب الأكثر شيوعاً هو Urinary tract infection (Option C).

في الـ Model Answer في بنك الأسئلة: تم اعتماد Option C (Urinary tract infection (UTI)) كإجابة نموذجية لحالة السيدة البالغة 42 عاماً.

9. Urinary Incontinence

⚡ Medium Yield (14×) 5 Qs

1. Urinary Incontinence Spectrum: Mechanism & Management 🔥 x20

Feature Stress Incontinence 🔥 x8 Urgency Incontinence (OAB) 🔥 x8 Overflow Incontinence 🔥 x4
Underlying Pathophysiology Pelvic floor muscle weakness & urethral hypermobility; intrinsic sphincter deficiency post-vaginal deliveries / multiparity Detrusor muscle overactivity (involuntary contractions) during bladder filling; idiopathic or neurogenic (stroke, Parkinson) Bladder outlet obstruction (severe BPH, urethral stricture) or detrusor underactivity/atony (diabetic autonomic neuropathy)
Clinical Hallmark Trigger Involuntary leakage of small amounts of urine provoked by coughing, sneezing, laughing, lifting, or exercising; NO leakage at rest Sudden, uncontrollable, compelling urge to void immediately followed by large volume involuntary urine leakage; nocturia, frequency Constant involuntary dribbling of urine, feeling of incomplete bladder emptying, weak stream, enlarged palpable bladder
Post-Void Residual (PVR) Normal (<50 mL) Normal (<50 mL) Markedly ELEVATED (>200–300 mL)
Stepwise Management 1. First-Line: Pelvic Floor Muscle Training (Kegel exercises) ± biofeedback
2. Topical vaginal estrogen in postmenopausal women
3. Surgical: Mid-Urethral Sling procedure		 1. First-Line: Bladder training (timed voiding) & lifestyle changes
2. Pharmacotherapy: Antimuscarinics (Oxybutynin, Tolterodine) or Beta-3 Agonist (Mirabegron)		 1. Relieve obstruction (catheterization, α1-blockers, TURP)
2. For atonic bladder: Clean Intermittent Catheterization (CIC) ± Bethanechol

10. Scrotal, Urethral & Pediatric Urology

🔥 High Yield (36×) 15 Qs

1. Acute Scrotum: Testicular Torsion vs. Acute Epididymitis 🔥 x34

Diagnostic Feature Testicular Torsion (Surgical Emergency!) 🔥 x18 Acute Epididymitis 🔥 x16
Etiology & Peak Age Adolescents (12–18 years); inadequate fixation of testis to tunica vaginalis: "Bell-clapper deformity" Sexually active males <35y (Chlamydia trachomatis, N. gonorrhoeae); Older males >35y (enteric coliforms: E. coli)
Onset & Systemic Signs Sudden, catastrophic onset of severe unilateral testicular pain, nausea, vomiting; afebrile; tender swollen scrotum Gradual, insidious onset over 1–2 days; fever, dysuria, urethral discharge, urinary frequency
Physical Exam Signs • High-riding testis with horizontal lie
• Cremasteric reflex is STRICTLY ABSENT
• Negative Prehn Sign: Elevation of the scrotum does NOT relieve pain (may worsen pain!)		 • Normal testicular orientation
• Cremasteric reflex is PRESERVED / PRESENT
• Positive Prehn Sign: Manual elevation of the scrotum relieves pain
Diagnostic Imaging Emergency Color Doppler Ultrasound: Absent or severely diminished blood flow to affected testis Color Doppler Ultrasound: Markedly increased blood flow (hyperemia) to the enlarged epididymis/testis
Definitive Management Immediate Surgical Exploration: Detorsion and Bilateral Orchiopexy within 6 hours (viability >90%; drops to <10% after 24h) Antimicrobial therapy: Ceftriaxone 500 mg IM + Doxycycline 100 mg BID for 10d (<35y) or Levofloxacin (>35y); scrotal support

2. Pediatric Urology & Testicular Malignancy Highlights 🔥 x26

  • Testicular Tumors (Germ Cell Tumors): 🔥 x10
    • Most common solid malignancy in young males (15–35 years); presents as a painless, firm, solid intratesticular mass that does NOT transilluminate.
    • Tumor Markers: Alpha-Fetoprotein (AFP) (elevated in non-seminomatous yolk sac components; strictly normal in pure seminoma!) and beta-hCG.
    • GOLDEN RULE: Trans-scrotal biopsy is STRICTLY CONTRAINDICATED (disrupts lymphatic drainage and seeds tumor into scrotal skin) → Mandatory approach is Radical Inguinal Orchiectomy.
  • Varicocele ("Bag of Worms"): 🔥 x6
    • Abnormal tortuous dilation of the pampiniform venous plexus; >90% occur on the LEFT side (left testicular vein enters left renal vein perpendicularly at 90° angle). Increases in size with Valsalva and decompress when supine. Most common reversible cause of male subfertility.
    • Warning: A right-sided varicocele or a left varicocele that fails to decompress when supine requires abdominal CT to rule out retroperitoneal tumor (RCC).
  • Cryptorchidism (Undescended Testis): 🔥 x5
    • Most testes descend spontaneously by 3–6 months of age. If still undescended by 6–12 months, perform Surgical Orchiopexy (preserves fertility, prevents torsion, and allows palpation for cancer).
  • Posterior Urethral Valves (PUV): Most common cause of severe congenital urinary tract obstruction in infant boys. Bilateral hydroureteronephrosis, distended bladder ("keyhole sign"), oligohydramnios → Pulmonary hypoplasia. Confirm with Voiding Cystourethrogram (VCUG); Treat with transurethral valve ablation. x3
  • Catheter-Induced Hematuria (Q551): Sudden frank hematuria with blood clots developing 5 days after urethral catheterization for postoperative urinary retention is indicative of catheter-related Urethral / Bladder Neck Trauma. x2
⚠️ Model Answer vs. AI Recommended Answer

تنبيه هام للامتحان بخصوص البيلة الدموية بعد القسطرة البولية (سؤال Q551):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: مريض مسن (75 عاماً) خضع لقسطرة بولية لعلاج احتباس بول بعد جراحة فتق، وبعد 5 أيام أصيب فجأة بـ Frank Hematuria مصحوبة بجلطات دموية (Blood clots). جراحياً وسريرياً النزيف الدموي الصريح مع جلطات بعد القسطرة ينتج عن رضح في الإحليل أو عنق المثانة Urethral trauma (Option B).

في الـ Model Answer في بنك الأسئلة: تم اختيار Option C (Urinary tract infection) باعتبارها عدوى بولية تالية لتركيب القسطرة (Catheter-associated UTI).

All 8 high-yield Neurology topics combined sequentially on a single continuous page for unified study & A4 PDF printing.

8 Topics 64 QBank Qs 📄 Continuous Flow (Protected Tables)
Chapter Contents Index (Topics 1–8)
1Stroke & Cerebrovascular Disorders 🔥 High Yield (35×) 2Headache Disorders 🔥 High Yield (32×) 3Seizures & Epilepsy ⚡ Medium Yield (14×) 4CNS Infections 🔥 High Yield (40×) 5Parkinson Disease & Multiple Sclerosis ⚡ Medium Yield (12×) 6Cranial & Peripheral Nerve Lesions ⚡ Medium Yield (16×) 7Spinal Cord & Back Pain ⚡ Medium Yield (15×) 8Coma, Herniation & Opiate Toxicity 🔥 High Yield (35×)
Topic 1 of 8 • Internal Medicine • Neurology

1. Stroke & Cerebrovascular Disorders

🔥 High Yield (35×) 14 Qs

1. Acute Ischemic Stroke: Diagnostic Algorithm & Reperfusion Windows 🔥 x35

Clinical Step Standard Protocol & Thresholds High-Yield Clinical Rationale
Initial Diagnostic Test Non-Contrast Head CT Scan immediately upon ED arrival Mandatory 1st step in any suspected acute stroke to rapidly rule out Intracerebral Hemorrhage before administering thrombolytics or antiplatelets.
IV Thrombolysis (rtPA / Alteplase) Within 4.5 hours of symptom onset (or last known normal)
Blood Pressure Requirement: BP must be <185/110 mmHg before tPA infusion and maintained <180/105 mmHg for 24 hours.		 Administer IV Alteplase (0.9 mg/kg) without delay. If BP >185/110 mmHg, lower gently with IV Labetalol or Nicardipine before initiating thrombolysis.
Delayed Presentation (>4.5 Hours) Outside thrombolytic window (e.g., presenting at 6 hours with dense hemiplegia) Admit to Stroke Unit / ICU for neurocritical monitoring. Practice permissive hypertension (do NOT aggressively lower BP unless >220/120 mmHg or end-organ damage); emergent heparin is strictly contraindicated.
Secondary Prevention & Workup Aspirin (160–325 mg) started within 24–48 hours (delayed 24h if tPA given).
Vascular Imaging: Carotid Duplex Ultrasound for anterior circulation; CT/MR Angiography for posterior circulation.		 Evaluate for carotid stenosis (carotid endarterectomy indicated if symptomatic stenosis 70–99%). Echocardiography & Holter ECG to rule out cardioembolic sources (Atrial Fibrillation).

2. Stroke Localization: Cerebral Artery & Brainstem Syndromes 🔥 x28

Artery / Territory Clinical Manifestations & Deficits Distinguishing Exam Pearls
Middle Cerebral Artery (MCA) Contralateral hemiparesis & sensory loss (Face and Arm > Leg)
Homonymous hemianopia
Dominant (Left) hemisphere: Aphasia (Broca / Wernicke)
Non-dominant (Right) hemisphere: Hemispatial Neglect & Dressing Apraxia		 Most common stroke territory. Upper extremity weakness significantly exceeds lower extremity weakness. Gaze preference toward the side of lesion.
Anterior Cerebral Artery (ACA) Contralateral hemiparesis & sensory loss (Leg > Arm and Face)
Urinary incontinence, abulia, cognitive slowing, frontal release signs		 Lower extremity selectively paralyzed; face and arm relatively spared.
Posterior Cerebral Artery (PCA) Contralateral Homonymous Hemianopia with MACULAR SPARING
Visual agnosia, alexia without agraphia (if splenium involved)
Thalamic pain syndrome (Dejerine-Roussy) with delayed severe neuropathic pain		 Motor power is characteristically preserved or minimally affected unless midbrain cerebral peduncles are involved.
Crossed Brainstem Syndromes Ipsilateral Cranial Nerve Deficit + Contralateral Spastic Hemiplegia / Hemisensory Loss
• Weber Syndrome (Midbrain): Ipsilateral CN III palsy (ptosis, "down and out") + contralateral hemiplegia.
• Millard-Gubler Syndrome (Pons): Ipsilateral CN VII LMN palsy (whole face) + contralateral hemiplegia.
• Wallenberg Syndrome (PICA / Lateral Medulla): Vertigo, nystagmus, ipsilateral Horner syndrome, ipsilateral facial sensory loss, contralateral body pain/temperature loss.		 A cranial nerve deficit on one side with limb motor/sensory signs on the opposite side localizes conclusively to the Brainstem → Order Brain MRI with special cuts on the brainstem / posterior fossa.

3. TIA, Subarachnoid Hemorrhage & Cavernous Sinus Thrombosis 🔥 x20

  • Transient Ischemic Attack (TIA): Transient focal neurological deficit lasting <24 hours (typically <1 hour) without acute infarction on neuroimaging.
    • Carotid (Anterior Circulation) TIA: Transient monocular vision loss (Amaurosis Fugax - "curtain falling"), contralateral hand/arm weakness, or expressive aphasia. Initial workup: Carotid Duplex Ultrasound.
    • Vertebrobasilar (Posterior Circulation) TIA: Brainstem "Ds": Dizziness/Vertigo, Diplopia, Dysarthria, Dysphagia, and Dystaxia. Workup: CTA / MRA.
  • Subarachnoid Hemorrhage (SAH): 🔥 x8
    • Presents with sudden-onset, catastrophic "Thunderclap" Headache ("worst headache of my life"), vomiting, neck stiffness (meningismus), and photophobia due to ruptured saccular (berry) aneurysm (most common in Anterior Communicating Artery).
    • Diagnostic Algorithm: Immediate Non-Contrast Head CT (sensitivity >95% in first 6 hours). If CT is negative or equivocal but suspicion remains high, perform Lumbar Puncture → Diagnostic hallmark is Xanthochromia (yellow supernatant from hemoglobin breakdown, >12 hours).
    • Complication Prevention: Nimodipine (oral calcium channel blocker) started immediately to prevent delayed cerebral vasospasm and secondary ischemic deficits.
  • Septic Cavernous Sinus Thrombosis: 🔥 x5
    • Spreads from facial infections ("danger triangle" of face, sphenoid/ethmoid sinusitis). Presents with fever, severe headache, bilateral periorbital edema, proptosis, chemosis, and bilateral complete external ophthalmoplegia (CN III, IV, VI palsies) + sensory loss in V1/V2 distribution.
    • Confirm with Contrast-Enhanced MRI / MR Venography; treat with immediate high-dose IV broad-spectrum antibiotics and anticoagulation.
  • Vascular (Multi-Infarct) Dementia: Stepwise, fluctuating cognitive decline with focal neurological deficits, executive dysfunction, and multiple subcortical lacunar or cortical infarcts on neuroimaging.
⚠️ Model Answer vs. AI Recommended Answer

تنبيه هام للامتحان بخصوص تحديد شريان السكتة الدماغية MCA مقابل PCA (سؤال Q1598):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: مريضة مسنة تعرضت لجلطة دماغية أدت إلى فقدان حسي في الطرف العلوي أكثر من السفلي (Arm > Leg) مع عمى نصفي مماثل (Right-sided homonymous hemianopia) وتعذر ارتداء الملابس (Dressing apraxia). وجود العجز الحركي/الحسي البارز في الذراع مع الأبراكسيا والعمى النصفي يمثل إصابة صريحة للشريان المخي الأوسط Middle Cerebral Artery (MCA, Option A)، حيث تصاب الألياف البصرية (Optic radiation) في مسارها الصدغي الجداري. بينما انحصار الإصابة في عمى نصفي مماثل مع بقاء الرؤية البقعية (Macular sparing) دون عجز حركي للأطراف يوجه للشريان المخي الخلفي (PCA).

في الـ Model Answer في بنك الأسئلة: تم اعتماد Option A (Middle cerebral artery) كإجابة نموذجية مطابقة تماماً للمنطق السريري الصحيح.

Topic 2 of 8 • Internal Medicine • Neurology

2. Headache Disorders

🔥 High Yield (32×) 11 Qs

1. Primary Headache Disorders: Comparative Differential Diagnosis 🔥 x32

Feature Migraine Headache Tension-Type Headache Cluster Headache
Pain Quality & Location Unilateral (60–70%), pulsating / throbbing, moderate-to-severe intensity Bilateral, non-pulsating "band-like" pressure / tightness, mild-to-moderate Strictly unilateral, excruciating periorbital / retro-orbital sharp "hot poker" pain
Duration & Frequency 4 to 72 hours; episodic; aggravated by routine physical activity 30 minutes to 7 days; constant; NOT aggravated by routine activity 15 to 180 minutes; occurs in cyclical clusters (1–8 attacks/day, often nocturnal)
Associated Symptoms Nausea, vomiting, photophobia, and phonophobia; +/- aura (scintillating scotoma, 20%) Pericranial muscle tenderness; NO severe nausea/vomiting, NO photophobia Ipsilateral autonomic activation: Conjunctival injection, lacrimation, rhinorrhea, miosis, ptosis (Horner-like)
Patient Behavior Patient seeks a dark, quiet room to lie still Patient continues daily activities despite dull discomfort Patient is severely restless, agitated, pacing the floor, or rocking
Acute Treatment Mild: NSAIDs / Acetaminophen
Moderate-Severe: Triptans (Sumatriptan) or CGRP antagonists		 Simple analgesics: Acetaminophen, Ibuprofen, Aspirin 1st-Line: 100% High-Flow Oxygen (10–12 L/min via non-rebreather) + Subcutaneous Sumatriptan
Prophylactic Therapy Indicated if ≥4 attacks/month or severe disability:
Beta-blockers (Propranolol, Metoprolol), Topiramate, Amitriptyline		 Tricyclic antidepressants: Amitriptyline, stress management, relaxation 1st-Line Prophylaxis: Verapamil (high-dose); short-term bridge: Prednisone burst

2. Secondary Headaches & Red Flag Emergencies: Temporal Arteritis & Pediatric Headache 🔥 x25

  • Giant Cell (Temporal) Arteritis: 🔥 x10
    • Systemic vasculitis of medium/large arteries in adults >50 years old (peak in females >70).
    • Clinical Hallmark: New-onset temporal headache, scalp tenderness (pain when brushing hair), Jaw Claudication (pain when chewing - highly specific), and association with Polymyalgia Rheumatica (morning shoulder/hip stiffness).
    • Dreaded Complication: Anterior Ischemic Optic Neuropathy (AION) → Sudden, irreversible, painless permanent blindness!
    • Immediate Action: Marked ESR elevation (>50 mm/h) and CRP. Administer High-Dose Systemic Corticosteroids (Oral Prednisone 40–60 mg/day or IV Methylprednisolone if visual symptoms) IMMEDIATELY before obtaining a Temporal Artery Biopsy (biopsy can be performed within 1–2 weeks and remains positive).
  • Primary Exertional / Coital Migraine (Q610): 🔥 x6
    • Recurrent severe, throbbing headache triggered by vigorous gym exercise or sexual intercourse in a patient with a completely normal neurological examination.
    • Clinical Rule: The very first sudden exertional/coital headache must be evaluated for Subarachnoid Hemorrhage (Non-contrast Head CT ± LP). In recurrent stereotypical attacks with normal exams, diagnose exertional migraine → Prophylaxis with Beta-Blockers (Propranolol) or Indomethacin.
  • Trigeminal Neuralgia (Tic Douloureux): Brief, excruciating, paroxysmal "electric-shock" lancinating pain in the distribution of V2/V3 (cheek, jaw), triggered by light touch, shaving, washing face, or chewing. Drug of choice: Carbamazepine.
⚠️ Model Answer vs. AI Recommended Answer

تنبيه هام للامتحان بخصوص الصداع الحاد المتكرر والقيء عند الأطفال (سؤال Q364):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: طفل عمره 10 سنوات يعاني من نوبات صداع نابض أحادي الجانب متكررة على مدار أسبوع مصحوبة بقيء شديد. في الفحص السريري الأولي المباشر، يجب فحص قاع العين Fundus Examination (Option D) لاستبعاد وذمة حليمة العصب البصري (Papilledema) وعلامات ارتفاع ضغط الدم داخل الجمجمة قبل أي إجراء تداخلي. بينما من الناحية التصويرية التشخيصية لاستبعاد ورم أو آفة بنيوية في المخ يُعتبر الرنين المغناطيسي MRI Brain (Option B) هو الفحص التصويري النهائي الأدق.

في الـ Model Answer في بنك الأسئلة: تم اختيار Option B (MRI brain) كخطوة الإدارة الأنسب لاستبعاد أي سبب عضوي ورمي للصداع.

↑ Back to Top / جدول المحتويات
Topic 3 of 8 • Internal Medicine • Neurology

3. Seizures & Epilepsy

⚡ Medium Yield (14×) 3 Qs

1. Adult First-Onset Seizure: Emergency vs. Elective Evaluation 🔥 x26

Clinical Parameter Emergency Department Protocol Outpatient Neurology Guideline
Clinical Presentation Adult (e.g., 55 years old) presenting after a first-ever unprovoked generalized tonic-clonic seizure with postictal state and normal blood glucose.
Diagnostic Neuroimaging Urgent Non-Contrast Head CT Scan
(Model Answer in acute emergency setting)		 Elective Brain MRI with Epilepsy Protocol
(AI Recommended Answer for gold-standard structural evaluation)
Clinical Rationale Non-contrast CT rapidly rules out life-threatening acute structural pathologies: acute intracerebral hemorrhage, acute subdural hematoma, large mass effect, or skull fracture. Brain MRI offers superior resolution for subtle epileptogenic foci: hippocampal/mesial temporal sclerosis, focal cortical dysplasia, cavernous hemangiomas, or low-grade gliomas.
Additional Workup Serum glucose, electrolytes, calcium, magnesium, toxicology screen, renal/hepatic function. Routine and sleep-deprived Electroencephalography (EEG) to assess seizure recurrence risk and classify seizure type.

2. Status Epilepticus: Emergency Resuscitation & Antiseizure Sequence 🔥 x22

  • Definition: Continuous generalized convulsive seizure activity lasting ≥5 minutes, or ≥2 discrete seizures between which there is incomplete recovery of consciousness. Medical and neurological emergency!
  • Step-by-Step Pharmacologic Protocol:
    • Phase 1 (Emergent Initial Therapy, 0–10 min): Administer a rapid-acting IV Benzodiazepine → IV Lorazepam (0.1 mg/kg, 4 mg slow bolus) is the drug of choice. Alternative: IV Diazepam (10 mg) or IM Midazolam (10 mg) if IV access is unavailable.
    • Phase 2 (Urgent Control Therapy, 10–30 min): Administer a non-sedating IV Antiseizure Medication (ASM) regardless of whether benzodiazepine terminated the seizure → IV Levetiracetam (60 mg/kg, max 4500 mg), IV Fosphenytoin (20 mg PE/kg), or IV Sodium Valproate (40 mg/kg).
    • Phase 3 (Refractory Status Epilepticus, >30–60 min): Continuous IV anesthetic infusion → Propofol, Midazolam, or Ketamine with endotracheal intubation and continuous EEG monitoring in the ICU.
  • Supportive Care: High-flow oxygen, airway protection, check fingerstick glucose immediately (administer 50 mL 50% dextrose + 100 mg IV thiamine if hypoglycemic).
⚠️ Model Answer vs. AI Recommended Answer

تنبيه هام للامتحان بخصوص التصوير العصبي عند أول نوبة تشنجية لدى البالغين (سؤال Q646):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: لدى مريض بالغ (55 عاماً) أصيب لأول مرة بنوبة تشنجية معممة (Generalized tonic-clonic seizure) دون تاريخ مرضي سابق، في العيادات التخصصية للصرع يُعتبر الرنين المغناطيسي Brain MRI (Option A) هو الفحص التصويري الذهبي الأكثر حساسية لكشف الآفات البؤرية الصرعية الدقيقة مثل التصلب الصدغي والخلل التنسجي القشري والأورام منخفضة الدرجة.

في الـ Model Answer في بنك الأسئلة: في سياق الطوارئ الحادة (Emergency Room)، تم اختيار Option B (CT brain) كخطوة تالية فورية لاستبعاد الأسباب الطارئة المهددة للحياة كـ النزيف الدماغي الحاد أو الكتل الضاغطة قبل مغادرة قسم الطوارئ.

↑ Back to Top / جدول المحتويات
Topic 4 of 8 • Internal Medicine • Neurology

4. CNS Infections

🔥 High Yield (40×) 14 Qs

1. CSF Analysis in Meningitis: The Definitive Comparative Table 🔥 x40

Parameter Normal CSF Acute Bacterial Meningitis Viral (Aseptic) Meningitis Tuberculous / Fungal
Opening Pressure 100–200 mm H₂O Elevated (>200–350 mm H₂O) Normal to mildly elevated Markedly elevated (>250 mm H₂O)
WBC Count & Differential <5 cells/µL (all lymphocytes/monocytes) >1,000–10,000 cells/µL (>80% Neutrophils / PMNs) 50–500 cells/µL (>90% Lymphocytes; PMNs early <24h) 100–500 cells/µL (Lymphocytes predominant)
Glucose Level 45–80 mg/dL (or ≥60% of serum glucose) Markedly Decreased (<40 mg/dL or CSF/Serum ratio <0.4) Normal (>60% of serum glucose) Markedly Decreased (<30–40 mg/dL or ratio <0.3)
Protein Level 15–45 mg/dL Markedly Elevated (>100–500 mg/dL) Normal to mildly elevated (<100 mg/dL) Extremely Elevated (>100–500+ mg/dL; "pellicle/web")
Gross Appearance Crystal clear Turbid / Purulent / Cloudy Clear / Opalescent Clear, cloudy, or viscous ("fibrin web")

2. Acute Bacterial Meningitis: Age-Based Empiric Antibiotics & Steroids 🔥 x32

  • Clinical Triad: Fever, nuchal rigidity (neck stiffness), and altered mental status (headache, photophobia; positive Kernig & Brudzinski signs).
    • Clinical Rule: Never delay antibiotic administration for neuroimaging! If signs of elevated ICP (papilledema, focal deficits, coma) require head CT before Lumbar Puncture, draw blood cultures and administer empiric antibiotics immediately prior to CT.
  • Age-Based Empiric Antibiotic Regimens:
    • Neonates (<1 Month): Ampicillin + Cefotaxime (or Ampicillin + Gentamicin) → Covers Streptococcus agalactiae (GBS), Escherichia coli, and Listeria monocytogenes. (Avoid Ceftriaxone in neonates due to biliary sludging and hyperbilirubinemia risk).
    • Children & Adults (1 Month to 50 Years): Ceftriaxone (2 g IV q12h) + Vancomycin (15–20 mg/kg IV q8–12h) → Covers Streptococcus pneumoniae (including cephalosporin-resistant strains) and Neisseria meningitidis.
    • Adults >50 Years, Alcoholics, or Immunocompromised: Ceftriaxone + Vancomycin + Ampicillin → Addition of Ampicillin is mandatory to provide coverage for Listeria monocytogenes.
  • Adjunctive Dexamethasone: Administer IV Dexamethasone (10 mg) before or with the first dose of antibiotics. Dramatically reduces hearing loss, neurologic deficits, and mortality specifically in adult S. pneumoniae meningitis and pediatric H. influenzae type b. Stop dexamethasone if culture demonstrates non-pneumococcal etiology.
  • Post-Exposure Chemoprophylaxis (Meningococcal Meningitis): Close contacts of patients with Neisseria meningitidis require immediate prophylaxis → Rifampin (600 mg PO BID for 2 days), single-dose Ciprofloxacin (500 mg PO), or single-dose Ceftriaxone (250 mg IM).

3. Encephalitis & Focal Intracranial Infections 🔥 x20

  • Herpes Simplex Virus (HSV-1) Encephalitis: 🔥 x8
    • Acute fever, altered mental status, bizarre behavioral changes, olfactory hallucinations, and focal temporal lobe seizures.
    • CSF Hallmark: Lymphocytic pleocytosis, elevated protein, and elevated RBC count (xanthochromia / hemorrhagic necrosis) in CSF. Diagnostic gold standard: CSF HSV PCR. MRI shows hyperintensity in inferomedial temporal and frontal lobes.
    • Management: Immediate empiric IV Acyclovir (10 mg/kg q8h for 14–21 days) started without waiting for PCR results to prevent fatal brainstem herniation.
  • Brain Abscess: Direct spread from otitis media / mastoiditis (temporal lobe / cerebellum) or dental/sinus infection (frontal lobe). Triad of headache, fever, and focal deficit. Neuroimaging reveals a ring-enhancing lesion with surrounding vasogenic edema. Lumbar puncture is STRICTLY CONTRAINDICATED due to imminent risk of fatal transtentorial herniation.
↑ Back to Top / جدول المحتويات
Topic 5 of 8 • Internal Medicine • Neurology

5. Parkinson Disease & Multiple Sclerosis

⚡ Medium Yield (12×) 2 Qs

1. Parkinson Disease vs. Multiple Sclerosis: Core High-Yield Entities 🔥 x28

Feature Parkinson Disease (PD) Multiple Sclerosis (MS)
Pathophysiology Degeneration of dopaminergic neurons in substantia nigra pars compacta → depletion of striatal dopamine; intracellular Lewy bodies (α-synuclein). Autoimmune inflammatory demyelinating disease of the CNS white matter disseminated in time and space. Oligodendrocyte loss.
Typical Demographics Elderly adults (onset 55–75 years); gradual progressive course. Young females (onset 20–40 years; F:M = 3:1); relapsing-remitting course (85%).
Cardinal Clinical Signs TRAP Tetrad:
1. Tremor: Asymmetric Resting Tremor (4–6 Hz, "pill-rolling"); disappears with intentional action.
2. Rigidity: Cogwheel rigidity (lead-pipe + tremor).
3. Akinesia / Bradykinesia: Shuffling gait, loss of arm swing, masked facies, micrographia.
4. Postural Instability: Retropulsion, fall risk (late sign).		 Sensory & Motor Deficits:
• Optic Neuritis: Painful monocular visual loss, impaired color vision, afferent pupillary defect (RAPD).
• Internuclear Ophthalmoplegia (INO): MLF lesion.
• Lhermitte Sign: Electric shock sensation radiating down the spine upon neck flexion.
• Uhthoff Phenomenon: Transient worsening of neurologic symptoms with heat / hot showers.
Diagnostic Findings Clinical diagnosis; dramatic therapeutic response to dopaminergic medication. Brain/Spine MRI with Gadolinium: Hyperintense periventricular white matter plaques perpendicular to ventricles ("Dawson fingers").
CSF: Oligoclonal IgG bands (OCBs) present in >85% of cases with normal serum bands.
Pharmacotherapy • Levodopa + Carbidopa: Most effective agent for motor symptoms; preferred in patients >65.
• Dopamine Agonists (Pramipexole, Ropinirole): Monotherapy in younger patients (<65) to delay levodopa-induced dyskinesias.
• Anticholinergics (Trihexyphenidyl, Benztropine): Specifically targets resting tremor in younger patients.		 • Acute Relapse / Exacerbation: High-Dose IV Methylprednisolone (1 g/day for 3–5 days); plasma exchange if unresponsive.
• Disease-Modifying Therapy (DMT): Interferon-beta, Glatiramer acetate, Natalizumab, Ocrelizumab.
↑ Back to Top / جدول المحتويات
Topic 6 of 8 • Internal Medicine • Neurology

6. Cranial & Peripheral Nerve Lesions

⚡ Medium Yield (16×) 4 Qs

1. Facial Nerve Palsy (CN VII): Bell\'s Palsy vs. Central Stroke 🔥 x26

Feature Bell\'s Palsy (LMN CN VII Lesion) Central Stroke (UMN CN VII Lesion)
Forehead Involvement FOREHEAD IS PARALYZED
Inability to wrinkle forehead, raise eyebrow, or close eye completely (Bell\'s phenomenon: eyeball rolls up when attempting closure).		 FOREHEAD IS SPARED
Patient CAN wrinkle forehead and close both eyes tightly due to bilateral cortical innervation of the upper facial motor nucleus.
Lower Facial Muscles Paralysis of all ipsilateral facial muscles (flattened nasolabial fold, drooping mouth corner, impaired smiling). Paralysis strictly restricted to the contralateral lower face (mouth droop, flat nasolabial fold).
Associated Cranial Signs Hyperacusis (stapedius paralysis), loss of taste on anterior 2/3 of tongue, dry eye. Contralateral hemiparesis, sensory loss, aphasia, or neglect.
First-Line Management Oral Prednisone (60 mg/day for 7–10 days) started within 72 hours ± eye lubrication/patching. Emergency acute stroke protocol: urgent non-contrast head CT and evaluation for reperfusion.

2. Peripheral Nerve Entrapment & Trauma Syndromes 🔥 x24

Nerve & Vulnerability Motor Deficit & Functional Deformity Sensory Loss & Clinical Context
Radial Nerve
(Spiral groove of humerus / axilla)		 Wrist Drop & Finger Drop: Loss of wrist, thumb, and metacarpophalangeal extension; loss of triceps reflex (if axillary compression). Sensory loss over the dorsal aspect of the first web space and dorsal lateral forearm. Classic context: "Saturday Night Palsy" (drunken arm draped over chair) or midshaft humerus fracture.
Median Nerve
(Carpal tunnel at wrist / pronator teres)		 Weakness of thumb abduction/opposition (APB muscle); late Thenar Atrophy ("Ape Hand" deformity). Nocturnal paresthesias & numbness in digits 1, 2, 3, and the radial half of digit 4. Positive Phalen sign & Tinel sign at the volar wrist. Associated with pregnancy, hypothyroidism, diabetes, and rheumatoid arthritis.
Ulnar Nerve
(Cubital tunnel at elbow / Guyon canal at wrist)		 Weakness of intrinsic hand muscles (interossei, medial lumbricals), weak adductor pollicis (positive Froment sign); late "Claw Hand" (Klumpke-like) deformity affecting digits 4 and 5. Numbness and tingling in the 5th digit and medial half of the 4th digit (hypothenar eminence).
Acute Clean Laceration at Wrist (<24 hours): Immediate Primary Epineurial Neurorrhaphy (Surgical Suture Repair) is the treatment of choice.
Common Peroneal (Fibular) Nerve
(Neck of the fibula)		 Foot Drop & Steppage Gait: Loss of foot dorsiflexion (deep peroneal) and eversion (superficial peroneal). Patient trips or lifts knee high while walking. Sensory loss over the dorsum of the foot and lateral leg. Caused by direct trauma to proximal lateral fibula, prolonged leg crossing, or tight casting.
↑ Back to Top / جدول المحتويات
Topic 7 of 8 • Internal Medicine • Neurology

7. Spinal Cord & Back Pain

⚡ Medium Yield (15×) 3 Qs

1. Acute Lumbar Disc Herniation vs. Spinal Stenosis: Sciatica Differential 🔥 x28

Feature Acute Lumbar Disc Herniation (Sciatica) Lumbar Spinal Stenosis (Pseudoclaudication)
Onset & Demographics Acute, sudden onset in young-to-middle-aged adults (30–55 years) following bending, lifting, or straining. Chronic, insidious onset in elderly adults (>60–65 years) due to degenerative facet arthropathy and ligamentum flavum hypertrophy.
Pain Pattern & Radiation Sharp, lancinating, electric pain radiating down the buttock and posterior leg past the knee to the heel / foot (S1 / L5 dermatome). Diffuse aching, heaviness, and numbness in bilateral buttocks, thighs, and calves (Neurogenic Claudication).
Positional Factors AGGRAVATED BY SITTING, bending forward, static posture, coughing, or sneezing (increases intradiscal pressure); relieved by walking or standing. AGGRAVATED BY WALKING AND STANDING (spinal extension narrows canal); RELIEVED BY SITTING AND BENDING FORWARD (flexion widens canal; "shopping cart sign").
Physical Exam Hallmark Positive Straight-Leg Raise (SLR / Lasègue sign): Reproduction of radiating leg pain upon passive leg elevation between 30° and 70°. Negative or equivocal SLR test. Normal pulses (distinguishes neurogenic from vascular claudication). Wide-based gait.
Initial Management Conservative management: Continued normal activity, short course of NSAIDs and muscle relaxants, physical therapy. >90% resolve within 6 weeks without surgery. Physical therapy (flexion exercises), epidural corticosteroid injections. Surgical decompression (laminectomy) if refractory.

2. Metastatic Epidural Spinal Cord Compression (MESCC) & Brain Metastases 🔥 x24

  • Metastatic Epidural Spinal Cord Compression (MESCC): 🔥 x12
    • Oncologic emergency! Most common primary tumors: Prostate cancer, Breast cancer, and Lung cancer.
    • Clinical Presentation: Patient with known malignancy presenting with new progressive severe back pain (worse lying flat, midline vertebral tenderness), progressing to bilateral lower extremity paraparesis (e.g., 3/5 muscle strength), sensory level, hyperreflexia, and late sphincter incontinence / urinary retention.
    • Immediate Management Protocol:
      • 1st Step: High-Dose IV Corticosteroids (Dexamethasone 10–16 mg IV bolus) administered immediately upon clinical suspicion to reduce vasogenic edema and prevent irreversible paraplegia.
      • Diagnostic Test: Emergent Whole-Spine Contrast-Enhanced MRI.
      • Definitive Management: Urgent multidisciplinary consultation → Neurosurgical Decompressive Laminectomy followed by radiotherapy (surgical decompression + RT is proven superior to RT alone for single-level compression with neurological deficits [Patchell trial; AI Recommended Answer]). In non-surgical candidates or diffuse radio-sensitive tumors, Radiation Oncology (Model Answer) is initiated.
  • Most Common Intracranial Neoplasm in Adults: Secondary Brain Metastases (accounting for >50% of all intracranial tumors; most common primary sites: Lung > Breast > Melanoma > Renal > Colon). Present as multiple, well-circumscribed ring-enhancing lesions at the gray-white matter junction with extensive vasogenic edema.
⚠️ Model Answer vs. AI Recommended Answer

تنبيهات هامة للامتحان بخصوص أمراض الحبل الشوكي والعمود الفقري:

1. عرق النسا الحاد والانزلاق الغضروفي (سؤال Q618):
• علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: رجل عمره 55 عاماً يعاني من ألم حاد ومفاجئ أسفل الظهر ينتقل إلى الساق اليسرى حتى الكعب (S1 sciatica) مع تنميل ويزداد بالجلوس لأكثر من 10 دقائق، مع علامة رفع الساق المستقيمة إيجابية (Straight-Leg Raise إيجابي حيث محدودية الثني عند 30 درجة بسبب الألم) وفحص عصبي حركي سليم. هذا العرض الكلاسيكي يمثل انزلاقاً غضروفياً قطنياً حاداً Acute Lumbar Herniated Disc (Option D).
• في الـ Model Answer في بنك الأسئلة: تم تسجيل الإجابة خطأً في المفتاح بـ Option E (Spinal stenosis)، بالرغم من أن ضيق القناة العظمية يتميز بأنه ألم مزمن يخف بالجلوس (Shopping cart sign) ويزداد بالمشي والوقوف وتكون علامة SLR فيه سلبية تماماً.

2. انضغاط الحبل الشوكي الورمي (سؤال Q623):
• علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: مريض سرطان البروستاتا الذي يعاني من ألم بالظهر وضعف ثنائي متقدم بالأطراف السفلية (Paraparesis 3/5) مع انضغاط مثبت بالرنين المغناطيسي يتطلب فوراً جرعة كورتيزون عالية (Dexamethasone) متبوعة باستشارة جراحة المخ والأعصاب Neurosurgery (Option A) لإجراء استئصال جراحي لرفع الضغط (Decompressive laminectomy) بالتكامل مع العلاج الإشعاعي Radiation Oncology (Option C).
• في الـ Model Answer في بنك الأسئلة: تم اختيار Option C (Radiation oncology) كإجابة نموذجية رئيسية للاستشارة الأولية في أورام البروستاتا الحساسة للإشعاع.

↑ Back to Top / جدول المحتويات
Topic 8 of 8 • Internal Medicine • Neurology

8. Coma, Herniation & Opiate Toxicity

🔥 High Yield (35×) 13 Qs

1. Altered Consciousness & Coma: Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS) Mastery 🔥 x35

Component Score 1 to 2 Score 3 to 4 Score 5 to 6
Eye Opening (E)
(Max 4)		 1: None
2: To painful stimulus		 3: To verbal command / speech
4: Spontaneous eye opening		 (Max score is 4)
Verbal Response (V)
(Max 5)		 1: None
2: Incomprehensible sounds (groaning / moaning)		 3: Inappropriate words (random speech)
4: Confused conversation (disoriented)		 5: Oriented and converses normally
Motor Response (M)
(Max 6)		 1: None (flaccid)
2: Decerebrate extension (arms extended/pronated)		 3: Decorticate flexion (arms flexed on chest)
4: Withdrawal / flexion to pain		 5: Localizes painful stimulus
6: Obeys commands
  • High-Yield GCS Exam Calculation Scenarios: 🔥 x9
    • Scenario A: Patient opens eyes to pain (E2), makes incomprehensible sounds/groans (V2), and withdraws limbs from painful nailbed pressure (M4) → Total GCS = 8 (E2 + V2 + M4 = 8).
    • Scenario B: Patient has spontaneous eye opening (E4), mutters incomprehensible sounds (V2), and withdraws limbs to pain (M4) → Total GCS = 10 (E4 + V2 + M4 = 10).
    • GOLDEN INTUBATION RULE: "GCS ≤ 8, Intubate!" A comatose patient with GCS ≤ 8 cannot protect their airway; rapid-sequence endotracheal intubation is mandatory.
  • Coma vs. Stupor: 🔥 x8
    • Stupor: State of unresponsiveness from which the patient CAN be aroused briefly only by vigorous and repeated noxious painful stimuli, lapsing back when stimulation ceases.
    • Coma: State of profound unconsciousness in which the patient CANNOT be aroused by any stimulus, including vigorous noxious/painful stimulation.
    • Pontine Hemorrhage: Sudden profound coma, hyperthermia (loss of central temperature control), decerebrate posturing, and reactive pinpoint pupils ("pinpoint pupils" + coma = Pons).
  • Elderly Fall Criteria for Head CT: Any elderly patient (≥65 years old) presenting after a mechanical ground-level fall who experiences ≥2 episodes of vomiting, even with a GCS of 15, requires an immediate Non-Contrast Head CT Scan to rule out occult intracranial hemorrhage.

2. Traumatic Intracranial Hematomas & Herniation Syndromes 🔥 x30

Feature Epidural Hematoma (EDH) Subdural Hematoma (SDH)
Vascular Etiology Rupture of the Middle Meningeal Artery (branch of maxillary artery) secondary to temporal bone / pterion fracture. Tearing of Bridging Cortical Veins spanning the subdural space to dural venous sinuses.
Clinical Presentation Classic "Lucid Interval": Brief concussive loss of consciousness → complete recovery → rapid neurological deterioration into coma within hours. Gradual, insidious onset over days to weeks in elderly patients or chronic alcoholics (brain atrophy stretches bridging veins); headache, confusion, fluctuating cognition.
CT Scan Appearance Biconvex / Lenticular hyperdense collection; strictly limited by cranial suture lines (dura firmly adherent at sutures). Crescent-shaped / Concave hyperdense (acute) or hypodense (chronic) collection; crosses cranial suture lines.
Pupillary & Herniation Signs Rapid expansion → Uncal Herniation: Medial temporal uncus herniates across tentorium → compresses ipsilateral CN III (fixed dilated pupil / anisocoria) and cerebral peduncle (contralateral hemiplegia). Depends on size: Small SDH causes no mass effect (equal reactive pupils); massive SDH causes midline shift and subfalcine/uncal herniation.
Management Protocol Surgical Emergency: Immediate Emergent Craniotomy with Surgical Evacuation of hematoma + burr hole decompression. (Temporizing measures: IV Mannitol / Hypertonic saline while en route to OR). • Small SDH without midline shift: ICU Admission, close observation & supportive neurocritical care (conservative management).
• Large SDH (>10 mm thickness or >5 mm midline shift): Urgent surgical evacuation via craniotomy/burr holes.

3. Opiate Toxicity & Overdose Management 🔥 x20

  • Opiate Overdose Classic Triad: 🔥 x10
    • 1. CNS Depression: Stupor or profound coma.
    • 2. Severe Respiratory Depression: Markedly decreased respiratory rate (Bradypnea <8–12 breaths/min), shallow breathing, hypoxemia.
    • 3. Pinpoint Pupils: Bilateral symmetric miosis (constricted pupils). (Note: Severe anoxia or co-ingestion of sympathomimetics can cause mydriasis).
    • Definitive Antidote: IV Naloxone (0.4–2 mg IV/IM/IN) → Promptly restores spontaneous ventilation and consciousness. Titrate carefully to avoid precipitating acute opiate withdrawal.
  • ABG Exclusion Rule in Dyspneic / Stuporous Patients: 🔥 x6
    • Opioids produce hypoventilation leading directly to Respiratory Acidosis with Hypercapnia (↑ PaCO₂).
    • EXAM TRAP: In an acutely dyspneic or altered patient whose arterial blood gas demonstrates Hypocapnia (↓ PaCO₂) and Respiratory Alkalosis (e.g., due to tachypnea from pulmonary embolism, anxiety, or sepsis), Opiate overdose is CONCLUSIVELY RULED OUT!

Topic 1 of 2 • Internal Medicine • Psychiatry

1. Mood & Anxiety Disorders

🔥 High Yield (26×) 5 Qs

1. Major Mood & Anxiety Disorders: Diagnostic Comparison 🔥 x26

Disorder Diagnostic Criteria & Duration Clinical Hallmarks & Sleep Profile First-Line Pharmacotherapy & Psychotherapy
Major Depressive Disorder (MDD) ≥5 of 9 SIGECAPS symptoms present for ≥2 consecutive weeks; must include depressed mood or anhedonia.
(Sleep, Interest, Guilt, Energy, Concentration, Appetite, Psychomotor, Suicide)		 Depressed mood, profound anhedonia, weight loss/gain, fatigue, feelings of worthlessness.
Sleep: Early morning awakening (terminal insomnia), decreased REM latency.		 1st-Line: SSRIs (Sertraline, Escitalopram) or SNRIs + Cognitive Behavioral Therapy (CBT).
Refractory / psychotic depression / acute suicidality: Electroconvulsive Therapy (ECT).
Bipolar I Disorder (Manic Episode) Distinct period of abnormally elevated, irritable mood & persistently increased energy lasting ≥1 week (or any duration if hospitalization required).
≥3 DIGFAST symptoms (Distractibility, Impulsivity, Grandiosity, Flight of ideas, Agitation, Sleep deficit, Talkativeness).		 Euphoria, grandiosity, extreme hyperactivity, pressured speech, flight of ideas, reckless spending.
Sleep: Markedly decreased NEED for sleep (feels fully energized after only 2–3 hours).		 Mood Stabilizers: Lithium (gold standard; monitor renal & thyroid function) or Valproate.
Acute severe mania: Second-generation atypical antipsychotic (Olanzapine, Quetiapine, Aripiprazole). Antidepressant monotherapy is STRICTLY CONTRAINDICATED (triggers mania).
Generalized Anxiety Disorder (GAD) Excessive, uncontrollable, free-floating anxiety and worry about multiple daily events occurring on most days for ≥6 months.
Associated with ≥3 of: restlessness, fatigue, impaired concentration, irritability, muscle tension, sleep disturbance.		 Chronic diffuse tension, somatic symptoms (tremors, palpitations, GI distress).
Sleep: Initial insomnia (difficulty falling asleep due to uncontrollable rumination).		 1st-Line: SSRIs / SNRIs (Venlafaxine, Duloxetine) combined with Cognitive Behavioral Therapy (CBT).
Short-term bridge only: Benzodiazepines (caution for dependence); alternative: Buspirone.
Panic Disorder Recurrent unexpected Panic Attacks (abrupt surge of intense fear peaking within 10 minutes) followed by ≥1 month of persistent concern about additional attacks or maladaptive behavioral changes (avoidance). Cardiorespiratory surge: chest pain, palpitations, diaphoresis, dyspnea, choking sensation, fear of dying or losing control. Often associated with Agoraphobia. Acute Attack: Sublingual / oral Benzodiazepines (Alprazolam, Lorazepam).
Maintenance Prophylaxis: SSRIs (Sertraline, Paroxetine) + CBT.

2. Evidence-Based Indications for Cognitive Behavioral Therapy (CBT) 🔥 x20

  • Adjustment Disorder with Depressed Mood: 🔥 x10
    • Emotional or behavioral symptoms developing within 3 months of an identifiable psychosocial stressor (e.g., divorce, marital separation, job loss, medical diagnosis).
    • Symptoms cause marked distress out of proportion to the stressor or significant functional impairment, but do NOT meet full criteria for Major Depressive Disorder (no persistent suicidal ideation). Symptoms resolve within 6 months after the stressor ceases.
    • Treatment of Choice: Cognitive Behavioral Therapy (CBT) / Psychotherapy is the definitive first-line management; pharmacotherapy is generally unnecessary unless symptoms become chronic or severe.
  • Stress-Exacerbated Irritable Bowel Syndrome (IBS): 🔥 x8
    • Patients with chronic IBS who experience severe symptom flare-ups (abdominal cramping, alternating diarrhea/constipation) triggered by psychological stress and anxiety, refractory to standard dietary adjustments (low-FODMAP diet, fiber).
    • Best Non-Pharmacological Strategy: Cognitive Behavioral Therapy (CBT) or gut-directed hypnotherapy → directly modulates the brain-gut-microbiome axis and reduces visceral hypersensitivity.
  • Suicide Risk Assessment & Red Flags:
    • Single Strongest Predictor of Completed Suicide: A History of Prior Suicide Attempt!
    • Other high-risk factors: Male gender, elderly age (>65), living alone/widowed, active alcohol/substance abuse, access to lethal means (firearms), severe hopelessness, psychiatric comorbidity.
    • Action: Direct inquiry about suicidal intent/plan does NOT increase suicide risk. If intent or plan is present → Emergency Psychiatric Evaluation & Constant 1:1 Inpatient Observation.

Topic 2 of 2 • Internal Medicine • Psychiatry

2. Psychopharmacology & Substance Use

🔥 High Yield (28×) 2 Qs

1. Neuroleptic Malignant Syndrome (NMS) vs. Serotonin Syndrome 🔥 x28

Feature Neuroleptic Malignant Syndrome (NMS) Serotonin Syndrome (SS)
Causative Medications Dopamine (D₂) Antagonists:
• Typical antipsychotics: Haloperidol, Fluphenazine
• Atypical antipsychotics: High-dose Olanzapine (e.g., 30 mg/day), Risperidone
• Antiemetics: Metoclopramide, Promethazine
• Acute withdrawal of levodopa/dopamine agonists in Parkinson patients.		 Serotonergic Agents (excess 5-HT):
• Combinations of SSRIs, SNRIs, MAOIs (Phenelzine), TCAs
• Analgesics: Tramadol, Meperidine, Fentanyl
• Triptans, Linezolid, MDMA (Ecstasy), St. John\'s wort.
Onset & Time Course Evolves slowly over 1 to 3 days to weeks after starting or escalating neuroleptic dosage. Rapid onset within 6 to 24 hours of initiating or increasing serotonergic medication.
Neuromuscular Hallmark "LEAD-PIPE" GENERALIZED MUSCLE RIGIDITY
Extreme resistance to passive movement in all muscle groups; Hyporeflexia; NO clonus.		 NEUROMUSCULAR HYPEREXCITABILITY:
Hyperreflexia, spontaneous or inducible Clonus (ocular clonus, ankle clonus), tremors. Lower extremities > upper extremities.
Autonomic & Systemic Signs Extreme Hyperthermia (38.5°C–41°C), autonomic instability (labile blood pressure, tachycardia, severe diaphoresis), tachypnea, altered mental status (agitation to stupor). Hyperthermia (less severe unless extreme), autonomic instability, diaphoresis, mydriasis (dilated pupils), hyperactive bowel sounds & diarrhea.
Key Diagnostic Labs Markedly Elevated Creatine Kinase (CK >1,000–50,000 U/L) (due to rhabdomyolysis → risk of acute renal failure), marked leukocytosis (WBC 15,000–30,000/µL), elevated transaminases. Mildly elevated CK, normal or mildly elevated WBC. Normal transaminases.
Immediate Management 1. IMMEDIATELY DISCONTINUE the offending antipsychotic.
2. Intensive ICU supportive care, aggressive external cooling, IV fluid resuscitation.
3. Specific pharmacotherapy: Dantrolene (direct skeletal muscle relaxant) ± Bromocriptine (dopamine agonist) or Amantadine.		 1. IMMEDIATELY DISCONTINUE all serotonergic agents.
2. Supportive care, IV fluids, cooling.
3. Sedation with IV Benzodiazepines (Lorazepam).
4. Specific 5-HT antagonist: Cyproheptadine.

2. Benzodiazepines: Tolerance, Dependence & Withdrawal Syndromes 🔥 x22

  • Pharmacologic Mechanism: Allosteric modulation of GABAA Receptors → increases the frequency of chloride channel opening → membrane hyperpolarization and CNS inhibition.
  • Tolerance vs. Physiological Dependence: 🔥 x10
    • Tolerance: Downregulation and desensitization of GABA receptors requiring progressively escalating doses to achieve the same hypnotic or anxiolytic effect.
    • Physiological Dependence: Neuroadaptation characterized by rebound symptoms upon wearing off or drug cessation (daytime rebound anxiety, severe tremors, diaphoresis, insomnia, tachycardia, and sensory hypersensitivity).
    • Life-Threatening Withdrawal: Abrupt cessation of high-dose short-acting benzodiazepines (Alprazolam) causes severe delirium, hallucinations, autonomic storm, and generalized status epilepticus!
    • Safe Tapering Protocol: Switch patient to an equivalent dose of a long-acting benzodiazepine (Diazepam or Chlordiazepoxide) and gradually taper by 10–25% every 1–2 weeks over several months.
  • Acute Benzodiazepine Toxicity & Antidote:
    • Presents with somnolence, slurred speech, ataxia, and stupor with relatively preserved vital signs (respiratory depression is rare unless co-ingested with alcohol or opioids).
    • Specific Antidote: Flumazenil (competitive GABAA antagonist).
      BLACK BOX WARNING: Routine use of Flumazenil in comatose patients is strictly avoided because it can precipitate severe, refractory, life-threatening seizures and acute withdrawal in chronic benzodiazepine users!

2 Topics 13 QBank Qs 📄 Continuous Flow (Protected Tables)
Chapter Contents Index (Topics 1–2)
1Dementia & Neurocognitive Disorders 🔥 High Yield (32×) 2Delirium & Pressure Ulcers 🔥 High Yield (30×)
Topic 1 of 2 • Internal Medicine • Geriatrics

1. Dementia & Neurocognitive Disorders

🔥 High Yield (32×) 6 Qs

1. Initial Evaluation of Suspected Cognitive Decline 🔥 x20

Assessment Domain Diagnostic Protocol & First Step Clinical & Practical Rationale
Mandatory 1st Office Step Perform a Detailed Bedside Cognitive Assessment including the Mini-Mental State Examination (MMSE) or Montreal Cognitive Assessment (MoCA). Objective documentation of cognitive deficits (orientation, registration, attention/calculation, recall, language) is mandatory before ordering neuroimaging or starting medications.
Screening for Reversible Causes Order targeted laboratory investigations:
1. TSH (rule out hypothyroidism)
2. Serum Vitamin B12 & Folate levels (rule out deficiency/subacute combined degeneration)
3. Serum Electrolytes & Renal/Hepatic profile
4. RPR / VDRL (if risk factors for neurosyphilis).		 Standard reversible metabolic and endocrine etiologies must be excluded in all older adults with progressive memory loss.
Structural Neuroimaging Non-Contrast Brain MRI (preferred) or Non-Contrast Head CT. Evaluates for regional cerebral atrophy, occult subdural hematoma, normal pressure hydrocephalus, vascular lacunes, or intracranial mass lesions.

2. Major Dementias: Differential Diagnosis & Distinctive Pathologies 🔥 x32

Entity Cognitive & Neurologic Hallmarks Neuroimaging & Neuropathology Management Pearls
Alzheimer Disease (AD)
🔥 x12		 Insidious, progressive decline over months to years in adults >65.
Earliest hallmark: Loss of short-term (recent) episodic memory (misplacing objects, repeating questions), followed by word-finding difficulty, disorientation, and apraxia.		 Brain MRI: Atrophy is most prominent in the Hippocampus & Medial Temporal Lobes.
Pathology: Extracellular beta-amyloid (Aβ) plaques and intracellular neurofibrillary tangles (hyperphosphorylated tau).		 Cholinesterase Inhibitors: Donepezil, Rivastigmine, Galantamine (mild-to-moderate AD).
NMDA Receptor Antagonist: Memantine (moderate-to-severe AD; neuroprotective against glutamate excitotoxicity).
Dementia with Lewy Bodies (DLB)
🔥 x8		 Core Diagnostic Triad:
1. Fluctuating Cognition: Pronounced variations in attention and alertness.
2. Recurrent Visual Hallucinations: Typically well-formed, vivid, detailed (seeing people or animals).
3. Spontaneous Parkinsonism: Resting tremor, rigidity, bradykinesia developing simultaneously or within 1 year of cognitive decline.
• REM Sleep Behavior Disorder (acting out dreams).		 Neuroimaging: Relative preservation of medial temporal lobe volume compared to AD.
Pathology: Cytoplasmic Lewy Bodies (α-synuclein inclusions) in cortical and subcortical neurons.		 Cholinesterase Inhibitors: Donepezil / Rivastigmine.
CRITICAL CONTRAINDICATION: Severe Neuroleptic Sensitivity! Avoid typical antipsychotics (Haloperidol); they trigger fatal rigidity, autonomic collapse, or sudden death. If psychosis requires treatment, use low-dose Quetiapine or Pimavanserin.
Vascular Dementia
🔥 x6		 Stepwise ("staircase") deterioration with abrupt decrements corresponding to past ischemic strokes or lacunar infarcts.
Prominent early executive dysfunction (planning, organization) exceeding memory deficits; focal neurological deficits (hemiparesis, hyperreflexia).		 Brain MRI / CT: Extensive periventricular white matter hyperintensities (leukoaraiosis), multiple cortical infarcts, or strategic lacunar infarctions. Aggressive cardiovascular risk reduction: Antiplatelet therapy (Aspirin), high-intensity statin, optimal BP and glycemic control, smoking cessation.
Frontotemporal Dementia (Pick Disease)
x3		 Younger onset (typically 50–65 years).
Early, profound personality and behavioral changes: Disinhibition, socially inappropriate behavior, apathy, hyperorality (craving sweets), lack of empathy.
Memory and visuospatial orientation are relatively preserved in early stages.		 Brain MRI: Marked, focal atrophy of the Frontal and Anterior Temporal Lobes ("knife-blade" gyral thinning).
Pathology: Round tau-positive cytoplasmic Pick bodies.		 Supportive behavioral therapy, SSRIs (Trazodone, Sertraline) for behavioral symptoms. Cholinesterase inhibitors are ineffective and may worsen agitation.
Normal Pressure Hydrocephalus (NPH)
🔥 x5		 Classic Triad ("Wet, Wobbly, and Weird"):
1. Gait Apraxia ("Wobbly"): Earliest and most responsive sign; broad-based, slow, shuffling, "magnetic" gait (feet appear glued to the floor).
2. Urinary Incontinence ("Wet"): Early urgency progressing to frank incontinence.
3. Cognitive Impairment ("Weird"): Subcortical slowing, apathy, executive dysfunction.		 CT / MRI Brain: Ventriculomegaly (enlarged lateral and 3rd ventricles) out of proportion to sulcal enlargement (Evans index >0.3). Normal CSF opening pressure on LP. Diagnostic Test: High-Volume Lumbar Puncture (Fisher tap test, 30–50 mL CSF) → Transient, objective improvement in gait velocity confirms diagnosis.
Definitive Treatment: Ventriculoperitoneal (VP) Shunting.
↑ Back to Top / جدول المحتويات
Topic 2 of 2 • Internal Medicine • Geriatrics

2. Delirium & Pressure Ulcers

🔥 High Yield (30×) 7 Qs

1. Delirium in the Elderly: Etiologies & Non-Pharmacological Protocols 🔥 x30

Feature Delirium (Acute Encephalopathy) Dementia (Major Neurocognitive Disorder)
Onset & Course Acute onset (hours to days); fluctuating course with diurnal variation ("sundowning"). Insidious, gradual onset (months to years); slowly progressive and irreversible.
Attention & Awareness Markedly impaired attention; fluctuating alertness, distractibility, fragmented thinking. Attention is preserved until advanced stages; alert and aware of surroundings early on.
Reversibility Typically reversible once the underlying medical trigger is identified and corrected. Generally irreversible and progressive.
Precipitating Triggers "I WATCH DEATH":
• Infections: Urinary Tract Infection (UTI), Pneumonia, Sepsis.
• Medications: Anticholinergics, Sedatives/Hypnotics, Opioids, Polypharmacy.
• Metabolic: Hyponatremia, hypoglycemia, dehydration, uremia, hypercalcemia.
• Structural / Organ Failure: Acute urinary retention (full bladder!), constipation / fecal impaction, hypoxia, stroke, pain.		 Primary neurodegenerative proteinopathies (AD, DLB, FTD) or cerebrovascular disease.
First-Line Management 1. Identify & treat the underlying etiology (check vitals, bladder scan, urinalysis, electrolytes, oxygen).
2. Non-pharmacological measures: Frequent reorientation, familiar objects, visible clock/calendar, day-night lighting cycle, family presence, early mobilization, avoid physical restraints.
3. Acute severe agitation threatening safety: Haloperidol (low-dose oral/IV/IM); avoid benzodiazepines (Midazolam) as they paradoxically worsen delirium!		 Cholinesterase inhibitors, NMDA receptor antagonists, cognitive stimulation, caregiver support.

2. Geriatric Syndromes: Polypharmacy & Fall Prevention 🔥 x25

  • Polypharmacy in Older Adults: 🔥 x8
    • Simultaneous use of ≥5 medications. Dramatically increases the risk of adverse drug reactions, drug-drug interactions, cognitive decline, and Orthostatic Hypotension (postural dizziness/lightheadedness upon standing).
    • Mandatory step: Medication reconciliation and deprescribing using the Beers Criteria (avoid sedatives, strong anticholinergics, first-generation antihistamines, long-acting benzodiazepines).
  • Fall Risk & Prevention in the Elderly: 🔥 x10
    • GOLDEN EPIDEMIOLOGIC RULE: A History of a Previous Fall — even if the patient was completely uninjured — is the single strongest clinical predictor of future recurrent falls! ("Patients who fall, even if uninjured, are at risk of another fall").
    • Multifactorial Assessment: Screen for vision impairment, gait/balance deficits, postural vitals, cognitive status, footwear, and home safety hazards.
    • Most Effective Single Intervention: For an elderly patient with slow/shuffling gait and impaired balance, Initiating Physical Therapy focused on balance, strength, and gait training is the most evidence-based intervention to prevent future falls (accompanied by prescription of an assistive walking device [walker/cane] and installing bathroom grab bars).

3. Pressure Ulcers (Decubitus Ulcers): Staging & Management 🔥 x22

NPUAP Stage Anatomical & Clinical Description Management & Dressing Protocols
Stage I Intact skin with non-blanchable erythema of a localized area, usually over a bony prominence (sacrum, greater trochanter, ischium, heel). Area may be painful, firm, soft, warmer, or cooler. Pressure relief, barrier cream, transparent film dressing, frequent repositioning.
Stage II Partial-thickness loss of skin with exposed dermis. The wound bed is viable, pink or red, moist, and may present as an intact or ruptured serum-filled blister. (Subcutaneous adipose is NOT visible). Hydrocolloid or foam dressing to maintain a moist wound environment; protect surrounding skin.
Stage III Full-thickness skin loss. Subcutaneous adipose (fat) is visible within the ulcer, and granulation tissue is often present. Slough and eschar may be visible. Bone, tendon, and muscle are NOT exposed. Undermining and tunneling may occur. Wound debridement of necrotic slough; hydrogel or alginate dressings for exudate absorption; nutritional optimization (protein).
Stage IV Full-thickness skin and tissue loss with exposed or directly palpable fascia, muscle, tendon, ligament, cartilage, or bone. Slough or eschar may be visible. High risk for Osteomyelitis! Surgical sharp debridement, negative pressure wound therapy (wound VAC), reconstructive flap coverage; systemic antibiotics if systemic infection/osteomyelitis.
Unstageable Full-thickness skin and tissue loss in which the actual depth of the ulcer is completely obscured by slough (yellow, tan, gray) or eschar (tan, brown, black). Depth cannot be confirmed until slough/eschar is removed. Debridement is required to expose the wound bed and stage the ulcer, EXCEPT for stable, dry, intact eschar on the heel (acts as a biological cover; do NOT debride!).
Deep Tissue Injury (DTPI) Intact or non-intact skin with localized area of persistent, non-blanchable deep red, maroon, or purple discoloration or epidermal separation revealing a dark wound bed or blood-filled blister. Immediate pressure offloading; monitor closely for rapid evolution into full-thickness ulcer.
  • Core Preventive Rule: Reposition bedbound patients at least every 2 hours; reposition chair-bound patients every 15–30 minutes. Use specialized pressure-redistributing dynamic foam or alternating-air mattresses.

Topic 1 of 4 • Internal Medicine • Rheumatology

1. Rheumatoid Arthritis

🔥 High Yield (32×) 9 Qs

1. Rheumatoid Arthritis (RA): Diagnostic Profile & Disease-Modifying Therapy 🔥 x32

Domain ACR / EULAR Diagnostic Criteria Clinical Relevance & Exam Pearls
Joint Distribution & Duration Persistent (≥6 weeks) symmetrical inflammatory polyarthritis primarily involving small joints: MCP, PIP, and wrists.
GOLDEN RULE: Strictly SPARES the DIP joints and thoracolumbar spine (cervical spine C1–C2 subluxation can occur).		 Morning stiffness lasting >1 hour, which classically improves with physical activity and worsens with rest. Contrast with Osteoarthritis (stiffness <30 min, DIP involvement).
Laboratory Serology • Rheumatoid Factor (RF): IgM antibody against Fc portion of IgG (~70–80% sensitive, non-specific).
• Anti-Citrullinated Protein Antibodies (Anti-CCP): Most specific test (>95% specificity); prognostic marker for aggressive, erosive disease.
• Markedly elevated ESR and CRP.		 Seronegative RA exists (~20%); diagnosis is based on clinical joint score + inflammatory markers + duration ≥6 weeks.
Radiographic Features Hands/feet radiographs show periarticular osteopenia, symmetric joint space narrowing, and progressive marginal bone erosions. Late deformities: Ulnar deviation of fingers, Swan-neck deformity, Boutonnière deformity, Z-deformity of thumb.
Anchor DMARD Pharmacotherapy Methotrexate (MTX) is the anchor first-line synthetic DMARD → initiate immediately upon diagnosis to arrest joint destruction (even before erosions appear on plain X-rays).
Coadministration: Mandatory daily Folic Acid supplementation (reduces GI toxicity, oral ulcers, and hepatotoxicity).		 Monitor CBC (bone marrow suppression), LFTs (hepatotoxicity), and renal function. Baseline chest radiograph (pulmonary fibrosis risk). Teratogenic (strictly contraindicated in pregnancy).
NSAID Gastroprotection NSAIDs (e.g., Naproxen, Ibuprofen) inhibit COX-1 → deplete gastric mucosal Prostaglandin E₂ (PGE₂) → mucosal ischemia and ulceration. Misoprostol (synthetic PGE₁ analog) directly replaces depleted prostaglandins and provides definitive mucosal protection; alternative: Proton Pump Inhibitors (Omeprazole).

2. Acute Complications & Extra-Articular Syndromes in RA 🔥 x26

Complication Clinical Presentation & Diagnostic Clues Emergency Management Protocols
Acute Septic Arthritis in RA
🔥 x8		 Patient with known RA (often on immunosuppression like Methotrexate or biologic agents) presents with a single, acutely hot, red, exquisitely swollen joint (e.g., ankle, knee) + high fever.
Key Distinction: All other baseline RA joints remain quiescent and unchanged.		 Medical Emergency: Perform immediate Urgent Diagnostic Arthrocentesis before antibiotics. Synovial fluid shows turbid/purulent fluid with WBC >50,000–100,000/µL (>90% PMNs). Administer immediate empiric IV Vancomycin + Ceftriaxone and arrange surgical drainage.
Ruptured Baker (Popliteal) Cyst
🔥 x6		 Sudden onset of severe unilateral calf pain, tenderness, erythema, and swelling (pseudothrombophlebitis syndrome) closely mimicking acute DVT. Associated with knee effusion and crescent-shaped ecchymosis below the medial malleolus. Perform Duplex Ultrasound of the Lower Extremity to conclusively rule out Deep Vein Thrombosis (DVT) and confirm synovial fluid extravasation from the popliteal bursa into the gastrocnemius muscle plane.
Felty Syndrome
x3		 Rare, severe triad in chronic, long-standing, seropositive RA:
1. Severe Rheumatoid Arthritis
2. Splenomegaly
3. Neutropenia (Absolute Neutrophil Count <1,500–2,000/µL).		 Dramatically increases susceptibility to recurrent, severe bacterial infections and lower extremity ulcerations. Treat aggressively with Methotrexate ± G-CSF.
Drug-Induced Papilledema in RA
x2		 RA patient on combination DMARDs and chronic Systemic Corticosteroids (Prednisolone) presenting with new-onset headaches, transient visual obscurations, and bilateral optic disc swelling (papilledema). Secondary Pseudotumor Cerebri (Idiopathic Intracranial Hypertension) induced by corticosteroid initiation or rapid dose tapering. Perform urgent fundus examination and brain MRI/MRV to exclude dural venous sinus thrombosis.
⚠️ Model Answer vs. AI Recommended Answer

تنبيه هام للامتحان بخصوص بدء علاج التهاب المفاصل الروماتويدي المبكر (سؤال Q668):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: مريضة تم تشخيصها حديثاً بـ Rheumatoid Arthritis نشط دون وجود تآكلات عظمية ظاهرة بعد على الأشعة السينية (No X-ray erosions). المبادئ التوجيهية العالمية المعتمدة (ACR / EULAR) تنص على البدء الفوري بمثبطات المناعة المعدلة للمرض DMARDs (Methotrexate) مع كورس قصير من الكورتيزون كجسر حتى يبدأ الميثوتريكسات بالعمل Corticosteroids and methotrexate (Option D) لمنع حدوث التآكل المفصلي الدائم مبكراً.

في الـ Model Answer في بنك الأسئلة: تم في بعض النماذج القديمة وضع Option A (NSAID alone) كخيار أولي للأعراض قبل ظهور التآكلات، وهو ما يخالف المعايير العلاجية الحديثة التي تمنع الاكتفاء بمسكنات NSAIDs وحدها عند ثبوت التشخيص.

↑ Back to Top / جدول المحتويات
Topic 2 of 4 • Internal Medicine • Rheumatology

2. Systemic Lupus Erythematosus & Sjögren Syndrome

🔥 High Yield (25×) 7 Qs

1. Systemic Lupus Erythematosus (SLE): Diagnostic Hallmarks & Specific Serologies 🔥 x30

Feature Diagnostic Profile & Clinical Criteria Immunologic & Management Pearls
Demographics & Multisystem Criteria Young females of reproductive age (15–45 years; F:M = 9:1), particularly African-American, Hispanic, and Asian descent.
"SOAP BRAIN MD" Diagnostic Criteria:
• S: Serositis (pleuritis, pericarditis)
• O: Oral/nasopharyngeal painless ulcers
• A: Arthritis (non-erosive, migratory, ≥2 joints)
• P: Photosensitivity (rash after sun exposure)
• B: Blood cytopenias (autoimmune hemolytic anemia, leukopenia <4,000, thrombocytopenia <100,000)
• R: Renal disorder (proteinuria >0.5 g/day, cellular casts)
• A: Antinuclear Antibodies (ANA)
• I: Immunologic disorder (Anti-dsDNA, Anti-Sm, aPL)
• N: Neurologic disorder (seizures, psychosis)
• M: Malar ("butterfly") rash sparing nasolabial folds
• D: Discoid rash (scarring, follicular plugging).		 Serologic Testing Algorithm:
1. Antinuclear Antibody (ANA): Best initial screening test (>98% sensitive; high negative predictive value; non-specific).
2. Anti-dsDNA Antibodies: Highly specific (>95%); titers correlate directly with disease activity and Lupus Nephritis flares; associated with low serum complement (C3, C4).
3. Anti-Smith (Anti-Sm): Most specific antibody for SLE (>99% specific), but persists regardless of disease activity.
4. Antiphospholipid Antibodies (aPL): Lupus anticoagulant, anticardiolipin, anti-β₂-glycoprotein I → risk of arterial/venous thrombosis and recurrent pregnancy loss.
Core Pharmacotherapy • Hydroxychloroquine (HCQ): Mandatory baseline therapy for ALL SLE patients (reduces disease flares, organ damage, and mortality). Requires baseline and annual ophthalmologic screening for retinal toxicity (bull\'s-eye maculopathy).
• Systemic Corticosteroids: For acute disease flares.
• Lupus Nephritis (Class III/IV): High-dose IV Methylprednisolone pulse + Mycophenolate Mofetil or IV Cyclophosphamide.		 Renal biopsy is mandatory in any SLE patient with new proteinuria (>0.5 g/day) or active urinary sediment to guide immunosuppressive therapy.

2. Systemic Sclerosis (Scleroderma) & Scleroderma Renal Crisis 🔥 x25

Subtype Limited Cutaneous SSc (CREST Syndrome) Diffuse Cutaneous SSc
Skin Distribution Skin thickening restricted strictly to face, neck, and distal extremities (distal to elbows and knees). Rapid, widespread skin thickening involving trunk, chest, abdomen, and proximal extremities.
Clinical Features CREST Pentad:
• C: Calcinosis cutis (painful calcium nodules)
• R: Raynaud phenomenon (triphasic: white → blue → red)
• E: Esophageal dysmotility (heartburn, dysphagia)
• S: Sclerodactyly (tight, shiny skin over fingers)
• T: Telangiectasias (face, lips, hands).		 Early prominent visceral involvement:
• Interstitial Lung Disease (Pulmonary Fibrosis): Leading cause of death in diffuse SSc.
• Scleroderma Renal Crisis (SRC): Abrupt severe hypertension + acute oliguric renal failure.
Autoantibody Profile Anti-Centromere Antibodies (highly specific; associated with isolated Pulmonary Arterial Hypertension [PAH]). • Anti-Scl-70 (Anti-Topoisomerase I): Associated with pulmonary fibrosis.
• Anti-RNA Polymerase III: Strong predictor of Scleroderma Renal Crisis.
Scleroderma Renal Crisis Management Presents with sudden onset of Malignant Hypertension (BP >180/110 mmHg), acute oliguric renal failure, microangiopathic hemolytic anemia, and encephalopathy.
DRUG OF CHOICE: Immediate high-dose ACE Inhibitors (Captopril) → reverses renal vasoconstriction.
CONTRAINDICATION: Systemic Corticosteroids (Prednisone >15 mg/day) are strictly avoided as they directly trigger renal crisis!
