Topic 3 of 4 • Internal Medicine • Rheumatology

3. Crystal Arthropathies & Familial Mediterranean Fever

🔥 High Yield (28×) 5 Qs

1. Gout vs. Pseudogout (CPPD): Synovial Fluid & Crystal Analysis 🔥 x35

Feature Gout (Monosodium Urate) Pseudogout (Calcium Pyrophosphate Dihydrate)
Synovial Fluid Microscopy NEEDLE-SHAPED, STRONGLY NEGATIVELY BIREFRINGENT crystals.
Under polarized light with red compensator: Yellow when parallel to the axis of polarization; blue when perpendicular.		 RHOMBOID-SHAPED, WEAKLY POSITIVELY BIREFRINGENT crystals.
Under polarized light: Blue when parallel to the axis of polarization; yellow when perpendicular.
Typical Joint Distribution First Metatarsophalangeal (MTP) Joint (Podagra) in >50% of initial attacks; also ankles, midfoot, knees. Typically monoarticular. Large joints: Knee (>50%), wrist, shoulders, hips. Typically monoarticular or oligoarticular.
Pathophysiology & Triggers Hyperuricemia (>6.8 mg/dL) due to renal underexcretion (90%) or overproduction (10%). Triggers: Red meat, seafood, alcohol (beer), thiazide/loop diuretics, trauma, surgery. Deposition of CPPD crystals in articular cartilage. Strongly associated with Hemochromatosis, Primary Hyperparathyroidism, and Hypomagnesemia.
Radiographic Hallmarks Punched-out, well-demarcated "rat-bite" marginal bone erosions with overhanging sclerotic cortical rims; soft tissue tophi. Normal joint space early. Chondrocalcinosis: Linear punctate radiodense calcification of articular cartilage or fibrocartilage (menisci of knee, triangular fibrocartilage of wrist).
Acute Attack Treatment 1. NSAIDs (Indomethacin, Naproxen): 1st-line in patients without renal/GI disease.
2. Colchicine: (inhibits microtubule polymerization); best within 24–36h.
3. Corticosteroids (oral or intra-articular): Preferred in renal insufficiency, heart failure, or anticoagulation.		 Identical acute management: Intra-articular corticosteroid injection, oral NSAIDs, or Colchicine.
Chronic / Preventative Therapy Urate-Lowering Therapy (Target <6.0 mg/dL):
• Allopurinol / Febuxostat: Xanthine Oxidase Inhibitors (1st-line).
GOLDEN RULE: Do NOT initiate or discontinue allopurinol during an acute flare! Start 2–4 weeks after complete resolution with Colchicine/NSAID flare prophylaxis.		 No definitive disease-modifying therapy; manage underlying metabolic diseases (hemochromatosis, hyperparathyroidism). Chronic prophylactic low-dose colchicine for frequent flares.

2. Osteoarthritis (OA): Degenerative Pathology & Hand Nodal Signs 🔥 x22

  • Clinical Hallmark: Progressive loss of articular cartilage and secondary bone formation in weight-bearing joints (knees, hips, cervical/lumbar spine) and hands.
    • Pain Quality: Deep, aching joint pain that worsens with weight-bearing / joint use and improves with rest.
    • Morning Stiffness: Brief, lasting <30 minutes (gel phenomenon).
  • Hand Nodal Physical Exam Findings: 🔥 x8
    • Heberden Nodes: Bony cartilaginous enlargements of the Distal Interphalangeal (DIP) Joints.
    • Bouchard Nodes: Bony cartilaginous enlargements of the Proximal Interphalangeal (PIP) Joints.
    • (First carpometacarpal joint "squaring" of thumb base is also characteristic; MCP joints are characteristically SPARED).
  • Radiographic Cardinal Tetrad: 1. Asymmetric joint space narrowing, 2. Subchondral sclerosis (eburnation), 3. Subchondral bone cysts, and 4. Marginal Osteophytes (bone spurs). Synovial fluid is non-inflammatory (WBC <2,000/µL).
  • Conservative Management: Weight loss, low-impact exercise, physical therapy; Topical NSAIDs (Diclofenac gel) or oral Acetaminophen/NSAIDs for symptom relief. Total joint arthroplasty for end-stage refractory disease.
↑ Back to Top / جدول المحتويات
Topic 4 of 4 • Internal Medicine • Rheumatology

4. Spondyloarthropathies & Acute Rheumatic Fever

🔥 High Yield (24×) 4 Qs

1. Seronegative Spondyloarthropathies: Ankylosing Spondylitis & Psoriatic Arthritis 🔥 x26

Clinical Entity Pathophysiology & Diagnostic Hallmarks Evidence-Based Management
Ankylosing Spondylitis (AS)
🔥 x12		 Young male (20–35 years) presenting with ≥3 months of chronic Inflammatory Low Back Pain:
• Onset: Insidious, before age 45.
• Morning Stiffness: Prolonged (>30–60 min), worse on awakening.
• Response to Exercise: Classically IMPROVES WITH MOVEMENT AND EXERCISE; does NOT improve with rest.
• Physical Exam: Restricted spinal mobility in all planes (positive Schober test <5 cm expansion), reduced chest expansion (<2.5 cm; risk of restrictive lung defect).
• Extra-Articular Signs: Acute Anterior Uveitis (photophobia, red eye), aortitis/aortic regurgitation, apical pulmonary fibrosis.
• Imaging & Genetics: Bilateral Sacroiliitis on CT/MRI/X-ray; late "Bamboo Spine" (marginal syndesmophytes and ligamentous ossification); HLA-B27 positive in >90%.		 1st-Line Pharmacotherapy: Regular, full-dose Oral NSAIDs (Indomethacin, Celecoxib) combined with dedicated physical therapy and extension exercises.
Refractory to NSAIDs: TNF-alpha Inhibitors (Infliximab, Adalimumab, Etanercept) or IL-17 inhibitors (Secukinumab). Systemic corticosteroids are ineffective.
Psoriatic Arthritis & Arthritis Mutilans
🔥 x8		 Occurs in 15–30% of patients with cutaneous psoriasis (extensor plaques on elbows/knees). Characterized by DIP joint involvement, dactylitis ("sausage digits"), and severe nail pitting / onycholysis.
• Arthritis Mutilans (Q672): The most severe, aggressive phenotype characterized by rapid osteolysis and digital telescoping ("pencil-in-cup" radiographic deformity / "opera-glass hand").		 Methotrexate, Leflunomide; for severe or erosive disease: TNF-alpha inhibitors or IL-23 / IL-17 inhibitors.
Reactive Arthritis (Reiter) Triad of Oligoarthritis + Urethritis / Cervicitis + Conjunctivitis ("can\'t see, can\'t pee, can\'t climb a tree") following GI infection (Shigella, Salmonella, Campylobacter, Yersinia) or chlamydial urethritis. Associated with keratoderma blennorrhagica. NSAIDs 1st-line; treat active chlamydial infection with Doxycycline.

2. Acute Rheumatic Fever (ARF): Modified Jones Criteria & Chemoprophylaxis 🔥 x24

  • Etiology & Pathogenesis: Post-infectious autoimmune cross-reactivity (molecular mimicry) following Group A Beta-Hemolytic Streptococcus (Streptococcus pyogenes) PHARYNGITIS (skin impetigo does NOT cause rheumatic fever!).
  • Modified Jones Criteria Diagnostic Rule: Diagnosis requires evidence of preceding GAS infection (elevated ASO titer, anti-DNase B, or positive throat culture) PLUS 2 Major Criteria OR 1 Major + 2 Minor Criteria.
    • 5 Major Criteria (Mnemonic: JONES):
      • J — Joint Involvement: Migratory Polyarthritis (large joints: knees → ankles → wrists; exquisitely tender, transient, responsive to aspirin).
      • O — Carditis / Valvulitis: Pancarditis; most commonly Mitral Regurgitation (apical holosystolic murmur).
      • N — Subcutaneous Nodules: Firm, painless nodules over bony prominences/extensor tendons.
      • E — Erythema Marginatum: Non-pruritic, evanescent, erythematous ring-like rash with central clearing on trunk and extremities.
      • S — Sydenham Chorea ("St. Vitus Dance"): Rapid, irregular, purposeless involuntary movements, emotional lability.
    • 4 Minor Criteria (Mnemonic: CAFE):
      • C — CRP & ESR Elevated: Objective systemic inflammation.
      • A — Arthralgia: Joint pain without objective swelling (cannot count if arthritis is used as a major criterion).
      • F — Fever: Typically ≥38.5°C (Fever = 1 Minor Criterion).
      • E — ECG Prolonged PR Interval: First-degree AV block (cannot count if carditis is used as a major criterion).
    • Classic Exam Vignette (Q669): A 7-year-old child presenting with a tender, swollen right knee joint followed by left wrist arthritis (Migratory Polyarthritis = 1 Major Criterion) and fever (= 1 Minor Criterion) fulfills 1 Major and 1 Minor criterion.
  • Secondary Antibiotic Prophylaxis: To prevent recurrent streptococcal infections and permanent chronic Rheumatic Heart Disease → Intramuscular Benzathine Penicillin G (1.2 million units IM every 3–4 weeks) for a minimum of 5 years or until age 21 (up to age 40 or lifetime if carditis/valve disease is present).
⚠️ Model Answer vs. AI Recommended Answer

تنبيه هام للامتحان بخصوص الفحوصات التشخيصية لالتهاب الفقار اللاصق (سؤال Q628):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: شاب عمره 32 عاماً يعاني من ألم أسفل الظهر مستمر منذ 3 أشهر، يزداد صباحاً عند الاستيقاظ ويتحسن بالحركة والمشي مع محدودية حركة الجذع في كل الاتجاهات (Inflammatory Back Pain). الفحص الجيني HLA-B27 testing (Option C) وتصوير المفاصل الحرقفية العجزية بالرنين المغناطيسي (MRI Sacroiliac joints) يمثلان الركيزة التشخيصية لتأكيد Ankylosing Spondylitis.

في الـ Model Answer في بنك الأسئلة: تم اعتماد Option B (CT scan of lumbar spines and pelvis) كإجابة نموذجية لتقييم التغيرات الهيكلية وتصلب المفاصل الحرقفية في الحوض والفقرات القطنية.

قناة التليجرام: https://t.me/EMLEinNutshell 🌐 منصة EMLE in a Nutshell: https://emle2026.github.io/EMLE-VIP-Notes/

All 7 high-yield Endocrine & Electrolytes topics combined sequentially on a single continuous page for unified study & A4 PDF printing.

7 Topics 88 QBank Qs 📄 Continuous Flow (Protected Tables)
Chapter Contents Index (Topics 1–7)
1Hyperthyroidism & Thyroiditis 🔥 High Yield (28×) 2Hypothyroidism & Myxedema Coma 🔥 High Yield (32×) 3Thyroid Nodules & Thyroid Surgery 🔥 High Yield (28×) 4Diabetes Mellitus 🔥 High Yield (32×) 5DKA, HHS & Hypoglycemia 🔥 High Yield (35×) 6Adrenal & Pituitary Disorders 🔥 High Yield (30×) 7Calcium, Parathyroid & Acid-Base 🔥 High Yield (26×)
Topic 1 of 7 • Internal Medicine • Endocrine & Electrolytes

1. Hyperthyroidism & Thyroiditis

🔥 High Yield (28×) 11 Qs

1. Differential Diagnosis of Thyrotoxicosis 🔥 x28

Feature Graves Disease 🔥 x11 Subacute (de Quervain) Thyroiditis 🔥 x7 Central (Pituitary) Hyperthyroidism x1
Etiology & Pathogenesis Autoimmune: TSH-Receptor Stimulating Antibodies (TSI / TRAb) stimulate thyroid follicular cells. Post-viral granulomatous inflammation (mumps, Coxsackie, adenovirus) causing follicular rupture. TSH-Secreting Pituitary Adenoma (secondary hyperthyroidism).
Thyroid Gland Exam Diffuse, smooth, non-tender goiter ± vascular thrill / systolic bruit (due to hypervascularity). Exquisitely painful, tender, swollen anterior neck; pain radiates to ears or jaw. Diffuse non-tender goiter; signs of mass effect (headache, bitemporal hemianopia).
Extrathyroidal Signs Thyroid Eye Disease (Graves Orbitopathy): Proptosis/exophthalmos, periorbital edema, diplopia.
Dermopathy: Pretibial Myxedema (orange-peel non-pitting induration).		 Recent history of upper respiratory viral infection (1–3 weeks prior) + fever and neck pain. Visual field defects; hypopituitarism of other axes (amenorrhea, decreased libido).
Serum TSH Markedly Suppressed (<0.01–0.1 mU/L) Markedly Suppressed (<0.1 mU/L) (transient thyrotoxic phase → euthyroid → hypothyroid) Elevated or Inappropriately Normal TSH (despite high thyroid hormone levels).
Free T₄ & Free T₃ Markedly Elevated Elevated (leak of preformed colloid) Elevated
ESR & RAIU Scan Normal/mild ESR.
Diffusely Increased Radioactive Iodine Uptake (RAIU 50–80%).		 Markedly Elevated ESR (>50–100 mm/h) & CRP.
Diffusely Decreased / Absent RAIU (<1–3%).		 Order Contrast-Enhanced Pituitary MRI & alpha-subunit level.
First-Line Management 1. Symptomatic: Beta-blockers (Propranolol, Atenolol).
2. Antithyroid drugs (ATDs): Methimazole (1st-line, except 1st trimester of pregnancy → PTU).
3. Definitive: Radioactive Iodine (RAI-131) ablation or total thyroidectomy.		 Self-limiting course. Symptomatic relief: High-dose NSAIDs (Ibuprofen, Aspirin); if severe/refractory pain → Oral Corticosteroids (Prednisone 40 mg/day). (Antithyroid drugs are ineffective!). Transsphenoidal pituitary adenomectomy.

2. Systemic & Cutaneous Manifestations of Hyperthyroidism 🔥 x20

  • Cardiovascular Manifestations: 🔥 x6
    • Young Adults: Sinus Tachycardia (resting heart rate >100 bpm) with widened pulse pressure (e.g., 150/60 mmHg), hyperdynamic apical impulse, systolic flow murmur.
    • Elderly Adults (>60y) / "Apathetic Hyperthyroidism": Present with weight loss, depression, and new-onset Atrial Fibrillation with rapid ventricular response refractory to digoxin.
  • Plummer Nails (Onycholysis): Separation of the distal nail plate from the nail bed with subungual dirt accumulation → specific cutaneous sign of chronic thyrotoxicosis. 🔥 x4
  • Diagnostic Testing: Outpatient vs. Pregnancy:
    • Non-Pregnant Outpatient: Best initial screening test is Serum TSH Level (if abnormal → reflex Free T₄ and Free T₃).
    • Pregnant Patient (e.g., 8 Weeks Gestation): Order Serum Free T₄, Free T₃, and TSH. (Total T₄ and T₃ are falsely elevated due to estrogen-induced doubling of Thyroid-Binding Globulin [TBG]).
      ABSOLUTE CONTRAINDICATION: Radioactive Iodine Uptake (RAIU) scans and RAI ablation are strictly contraindicated in pregnancy and lactation (destroys fetal thyroid gland!).

3. Thyroid Storm (Thyrotoxic Crisis): Stepwise Emergency Protocol 🔥 x22

  • Precipitating Events: Surgery (thyroid or non-thyroid), infection, trauma, labor, DKA, or iodinated radiocontrast in uncontrolled hyperthyroidism.
  • Clinical Hallmark: Extreme hyperpyrexia (High fever 39.5°C–41°C), marked tachycardia (140–180 bpm, atrial fibrillation, high-output heart failure), CNS delirium/psychosis/coma, severe diarrhea, vomiting, jaundice.
  • Stepwise Pharmacologic Protocol (Memorize the Sequence!):
    1. Step 1 — Beta-Blocker: Immediate IV Propranolol (blocks beta-adrenergic hyperstimulation + inhibits peripheral T₄ to T₃ conversion).
    2. Step 2 — Antithyroid Drug: Propylthiouracil (PTU) 200 mg PO/NG q4h (blocks new thyroid hormone synthesis AND peripheral 5'-deiodinase T₄ → T₃ conversion; preferred over Methimazole in storm).
    3. Step 3 — Saturated Solution of Potassium Iodide (SSKI / Lugol Iodine): Administered STRICTLY ≥1 HOUR AFTER PTU (Wolff-Chaikoff effect blocks release of preformed hormone; if given before PTU, iodine acts as substrate to synthesize more hormone!).
    4. Step 4 — Glucocorticoids: IV Hydrocortisone (100 mg q8h) (blocks peripheral T₄ → T₃ conversion and treats relative adrenal insufficiency).
↑ Back to Top / جدول المحتويات
Topic 2 of 7 • Internal Medicine • Endocrine & Electrolytes

2. Hypothyroidism & Myxedema Coma

🔥 High Yield (32×) 14 Qs

1. Hypothyroidism: Primary vs. Secondary vs. Subclinical Profile 🔥 x32

Entity Etiology & Demographics Biochemical Lab Signature Management Pearls
Hashimoto Thyroiditis (Primary Hypothyroidism)
🔥 x14		 Autoimmune destruction of thyroid follicles; most common cause in iodine-sufficient areas. Females > males (F:M = 8:1).
Presents with cold intolerance, fatigue, constipation, dry skin, coarse brittle hair, periorbital puffiness, delayed relaxation of deep tendon reflexes ("hung-up" ankle jerk), and mild weight gain.		 • Serum TSH: Markedly Elevated (>10–50 mU/L).
• Free T₄: Low (<0.8 ng/dL).
• Antibodies: Anti-Thyroid Peroxidase (Anti-TPO) positive in >95% and Anti-Thyroglobulin (Anti-Tg).
• High risk of developing Thyroid Lymphoma (B-cell non-Hodgkin).		 Levothyroxine (T₄) Monotherapy taken orally once daily in the morning on an empty stomach with water, ≥30–60 minutes before breakfast.
Monitor TSH every 6–8 weeks until euthyroid (target TSH 0.5–2.5 mU/L).
Subclinical Hypothyroidism
🔥 x8		 Early / mild thyroid failure. Frequently asymptomatic or mild non-specific fatigue. • Serum TSH: Elevated (typically 5–10 mU/L).
• Free T₄: STRICTLY NORMAL.
• Positive Anti-TPO increases progression risk to overt hypothyroidism by 4–5% per year.		 Indications to Treat with Levothyroxine:
1. TSH ≥10 mU/L (universal treatment threshold).
2. Pregnancy or attempting conception.
3. Symptomatic patient with positive Anti-TPO or goiter.
Secondary (Central) Hypothyroidism
x3		 Pituitary adenoma, Sheehan syndrome, craniopharyngioma, or head trauma causing TSH deficiency. • Serum TSH: Low or Inappropriately Normal.
• Free T₄: Low.
• Requires pituitary MRI and evaluation of adrenal axis (ACTH).		 GOLDEN RULE: Always rule out or treat Adrenal Insufficiency BEFORE starting Levothyroxine (giving T₄ accelerates cortisol clearance and precipitates fatal Addisonian crisis!).

2. Myxedema Coma: Decompensated Hypothyroid Emergency 🔥 x20

  • Clinical Hallmark: Elderly female with long-standing, undiagnosed or untreated hypothyroidism presenting in winter with altered mental status (lethargy, stupor, coma) precipitated by infection (pneumonia, UTI), cold exposure, sedatives, or myocardial infarction.
  • Physical Findings: Severe Hypothermia (<35.5°C, often <32°C), profound Bradycardia, hypotension, Hypoventilation / Hypercapnic Respiratory Failure, generalized non-pitting myxedematous edema (puffy face, periorbital edema, macroglossia).
  • Metabolic Complications: Severe Hyponatremia (due to SIADH-like impaired free water clearance) and Hypoglycemia.
  • Immediate Emergency Resuscitation:
    • 1. Airway & Breathing: Endotracheal intubation and mechanical ventilation for respiratory depression/hypercapnia.
    • 2. Passive Rewarming: Gentle passive rewarming (warm blankets); avoid active aggressive external rewarming (causes peripheral vasodilation → circulatory collapse).
    • 3. Stress-Dose Steroids FIRST: Administer IV Hydrocortisone (100 mg IV q8h) IMMEDIATELY before thyroid hormone to prevent precipitating acute adrenal crisis.
    • 4. IV Thyroid Hormone: IV Levothyroxine (loading dose 200–400 µg IV) ± IV Liothyronine (T₃).

Topic 3 of 7 • Internal Medicine • Endocrine & Electrolytes

3. Thyroid Nodules & Thyroid Surgery

🔥 High Yield (28×) 9 Qs

1. Thyroid Nodule Diagnostic Workup Algorithm 🔥 x28

Diagnostic Step Clinical Action & Thresholds High-Yield Diagnostic Logic
Step 1: Laboratory Screening Order Serum TSH Level • If TSH is Low (Suppressed) → proceed to Radionuclide Thyroid Scintigraphy (I-123 / Tc-99m).
• If TSH is Normal or Elevated → proceed directly to Thyroid Ultrasound.
Step 2: Scintigraphy (if Low TSH) Radioactive Iodine Uptake Scan • "Hot" (Hyperfunctioning) Nodule: Autonomous toxic adenoma; almost never malignant (<1%) → treat hyperthyroidism (ATDs, RAI ablation, surgery).
• "Cold" (Non-functioning) Nodule: 15–20% risk of malignancy → requires Thyroid Ultrasound and FNA.
Step 3: High-Resolution Ultrasound Evaluate nodule size and suspicious features Suspicious Sonographic Features:
• Microcalcifications (psammoma bodies)
• Hypoechogenicity
• Irregular, infiltrative margins
• "Taller-than-wide" shape
• Extrathyroidal extension & suspicious cervical lymph nodes.
Step 4: Fine-Needle Aspiration (FNA) Ultrasound-Guided FNA Biopsy The single most accurate and cost-effective test to differentiate benign from malignant thyroid nodules. Indicated for nodules ≥1 cm with suspicious features or ≥1.5–2 cm if low risk.

2. Thyroid Carcinomas: Comparative Pathology & Surgical Complications 🔥 x26

Cancer Subtype Epidemiology & Key Pathology Metastatic Spread Pattern Surgical & Adjuvant Management
Papillary Thyroid Carcinoma (PTC)
🔥 x10		 Most common thyroid malignancy (>80% of all cases). History of ionizing radiation exposure.
Pathology: "Orphan Annie eyes" nuclei (ground-glass empty nuclei), nuclear grooves, pseudoinclusions, and Psammoma bodies (concentric calcifications).		 Spreads predominantly via Lymphatics to regional cervical lymph nodes. Excellent 10-year prognosis (>95%). Total / near-total thyroidectomy ± central neck lymph node dissection → Postoperative RAI-131 ablation + suppressive Levothyroxine. Monitor Serum Thyroglobulin (Tg) as recurrence marker.
Follicular Thyroid Carcinoma (FTC)
🔥 x6		 Second most common (~10–15%). Common in iodine-deficient areas.
CRITICAL PATHOLOGY RULE: FNA CANNOT differentiate benign follicular adenoma from follicular carcinoma (both show identical follicular cells). Malignancy requires histologic demonstration of Capsular or Vascular Invasion!		 Spreads characteristically via Hematogenous route to bones and lungs. Diagnostic lobectomy → completion total thyroidectomy if invasion demonstrated → RAI-131 ablation.
Medullary Thyroid Carcinoma (MTC)
🔥 x5		 Originates from Parafollicular C-cells (neuroendocrine). Secrete Calcitonin.
Associated with MEN 2A and MEN 2B (germline RET proto-oncogene mutations).
Pathology: Sheets of polygonal cells embedded in an amyloid stroma (stains with Congo red).		 Spreads via both lymphatics and hematogenous routes. Does NOT concentrate radioactive iodine! Total thyroidectomy + bilateral neck dissection. Screen for Pheochromocytoma before surgery! Monitor Serum Calcitonin & CEA.
Anaplastic Thyroid Carcinoma Elderly adults (>65–70y). Rapidly enlarging, rock-hard, painful anterior neck mass causing stridor, dysphagia, and hoarseness. Extensive local tissue invasion and distant metastases at presentation. Extremely poor prognosis (median survival <6 months). Palliative tracheostomy, chemo-radiation.
  • Post-Thyroidectomy Complications:
    • Recurrent Laryngeal Nerve (RLN) Injury: Unilateral damage causes Hoarseness / breathy voice; bilateral injury causes acute upper airway obstruction / stridor requiring emergent tracheostomy.
    • Superior Laryngeal Nerve (External Branch) Injury: Loss of cricothyroid innervation → Loss of high-pitched voice and vocal fatigue.
    • Hypoparathyroidism & Acute Tetany: Accidental devascularization/removal of parathyroid glands → acute hypocalcemia 24–48h postoperatively (perioral numbness, Chvostek/Trousseau signs, total Ca <7.0 mg/dL) → treat with IV Calcium Gluconate.

4. Diabetes Mellitus

🔥 High Yield (32×) 16 Qs

1. Diagnostic Criteria for Diabetes Mellitus & Impaired Fasting Glucose 🔥 x32

Diagnostic Test Normal Prediabetes (Impaired) Diabetes Mellitus (DM)
Fasting Plasma Glucose (FPG)
(No caloric intake for ≥8 hours)		 <100 mg/dL (<5.6 mmol/L) 100–125 mg/dL (5.6–6.9 mmol/L)
(Impaired Fasting Glucose - IFG)		 ≥126 mg/dL (≥7.0 mmol/L)
(Must be confirmed on repeat test)
Hemoglobin A1c (HbA1c)
(NGSP certified, standardized)		 <5.7% 5.7% – 6.4%
(High risk for developing diabetes)		 ≥6.5% (≥48 mmol/mol)
(Must be confirmed on repeat test)
2-Hour Oral Glucose Tolerance (OGTT)
(75-g anhydrous glucose dissolved in water)		 <140 mg/dL (<7.8 mmol/L) 140–199 mg/dL (7.8–11.0 mmol/L)
(Impaired Glucose Tolerance - IGT)		 ≥200 mg/dL (≥11.1 mmol/L)
Random Plasma Glucose
(In symptomatic patient: polyuria, polydipsia)		 <140 mg/dL — ≥200 mg/dL (≥11.1 mmol/L)
(Does NOT require repeat confirmatory test!)

2. Pharmacotherapy in Type 2 Diabetes: First-Line & Step-Up Strategies 🔥 x30

  • Metformin (Biguanide) — The Universal 1st-Line Anchor: 🔥 x15
    • Mechanism: Activates AMP-activated protein kinase (AMPK) → decreases hepatic gluconeogenesis, increases peripheral insulin sensitivity, reduces intestinal glucose absorption. Weight neutral or mild weight loss; zero intrinsic hypoglycemia risk.
    • MANDATORY PRE-PRESCRIPTION STEP: Calculate the Estimated GFR (eGFR)!
    • Renal Thresholds for Metformin:
      • eGFR ≥60 mL/min/1.73m²: Standard full dose (up to 2000–2500 mg/day).
      • eGFR 45–59 mL/min/1.73m²: Safe to continue; monitor renal function every 3–6 months.
      • eGFR 30–44 mL/min/1.73m²: Reduce maximum dose to 1000 mg/day; do NOT initiate new metformin.
      • eGFR <30 mL/min/1.73m²: ABSOLUTELY CONTRAINDICATED (risk of fatal Lactic Acidosis). Switch patient to Insulin Therapy.
    • Temporary Hold Rule: Discontinue metformin at the time of or prior to iodinated radiocontrast procedures in patients with eGFR <60, and withhold for 48 hours until normal renal function is reconfirmed.
  • Second-Line Step-Up Agents: 🔥 x8
    • SGLT2 Inhibitors (Empagliflozin, Dapagliflozin): Drug of choice if established Atherosclerotic Cardiovascular Disease (ASCVD), Heart Failure (HFrEF), or Chronic Kidney Disease (CKD) with albuminuria. Promote glucosuria, lower blood pressure, reduce CV mortality. Risk: Euglycemic DKA, mycotic genital infections.
    • GLP-1 Receptor Agonists (Semaglutide, Liraglutide): Injectable; drug of choice if ASCVD or compelling need for weight loss. Reduces CV events. Risk: GI nausea, contraindicated in personal/family history of Medullary Thyroid Cancer or MEN 2.
    • Sulfonylureas (Glimepiride, Gliclazide): In classic question-bank step-up algorithms where cost is a constraint, adding a Sulfonylurea is the traditional Model Answer. (Caution: Hypoglycemia risk, weight gain).
    • DPP-4 Inhibitors (Sitagliptin): Weight neutral, well tolerated; avoid combining with GLP-1 RAs.
  • Screening & Monitoring Recommendations:
    • General population screening begins at age 35 (or earlier in adults with BMI ≥25 + risk factors); repeat every 3 years.
    • Annual diabetic microvascular screening: Urine Albumin-to-Creatinine Ratio (uACR) for diabetic nephropathy, comprehensive dilated eye exam for diabetic retinopathy, and 10-g monofilament test for diabetic peripheral neuropathy.
⚠️ Model Answer vs. AI Recommended Answer

تنبيه هام للامتحان بخصوص الخيار الثاني بعد الميتفورمين (سؤال Q676):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: المبادئ التوجيهية الحديثة للجمعية الأمريكية للسكري (ADA) تنص على أنه في حال عدم انضباط السكر على Metformin وحده، فإن اختيار العقار الثاني يعتمد على وجود أمراض مصاحبة: حيث يُفضل SGLT2 inhibitors في حالات هبوط القلب وقصور الكلى المزمن، بينما يُفضل GLP-1 receptor agonists في حالات تصلب الشرايين القلبي الوعائي (ASCVD) أو الرغبة في إنقاص الوزن.

في الـ Model Answer في بنك الأسئلة: في الأسئلة التقليدية وبنوك الامتحانات عند عدم ذكر أمراض قلبية أو كلوية صريحة، تم اعتماد Adding a Sulfonylurea (Option B) كخطوة تصعيدية نموذجية لخفض السكر التراكمي بتكلفة اقتصادية منخفضة.

Topic 5 of 7 • Internal Medicine • Endocrine & Electrolytes

5. DKA, HHS & Hypoglycemia

🔥 High Yield (35×) 13 Qs

1. Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) vs. Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic State (HHS) 🔥 x35

Feature Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic State (HHS)
Patient Demographics Primarily Type 1 Diabetes Mellitus (younger, lean); can occur in severe T2DM during acute stress/infection. Primarily Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus (older, nursing home residents, impaired thirst/access to water).
Pathophysiology Absolute insulin deficiency → unrestrained lipolysis → free fatty acids delivered to liver → hepatic ketogenesis (acetoacetate, beta-hydroxybutyrate). Relative insulin deficiency (enough insulin to suppress lipolysis/ketogenesis, but insufficient to control hyperglycemia).
Onset & Symptoms Rapid onset (<24 hours).
Nausea, vomiting, diffuse abdominal pain (simulates acute abdomen), Kussmaul breathing (deep, rapid sighing respiration), fruity acetone breath odor.		 Insidious onset over days to weeks.
Severe progressive dehydration, weakness, polyuria, followed by profound neurological deficits (lethargy, stupor, coma, focal seizures). Minimal abdominal pain.
Serum Glucose Elevated: 250–500 mg/dL (can be lower in euglycemic DKA with SGLT2i). Markedly Elevated: >600–1,000+ mg/dL.
Arterial pH & HCO₃⁻ Severe High Anion Gap Metabolic Acidosis:
• pH <7.30 (often <7.10)
• Serum HCO₃⁻ <18 mEq/L (often <10)
• Anion Gap >12 mEq/L ([Na⁺] − ([Cl⁻] + [HCO₃⁻])).		 • Normal / Mildly decreased pH (>7.30)
• Serum HCO₃⁻ >18 mEq/L
• Anion Gap typically normal (<12 mEq/L).
Serum Osmolality & Ketones Serum osmolality variable (<320 mOsm/kg).
Serum & Urine Ketones STRONGLY POSITIVE (specifically β-hydroxybutyrate).		 Marked Serum Hyperosmolality: >320 mOsm/kg.
Ketones absent or minimal/trace.
Estimated Fluid Deficit Moderate dehydration: ~3 to 6 Liters (~100 mL/kg). Severe Dehydration: ~8 to 10+ Liters (~100–200 mL/kg).

2. Comprehensive DKA Management Protocol: Fluids, Insulin & Potassium Rules 🔥 x28

  • Step 1 — Aggressive Fluid Resuscitation (Priority #1!): 🔥 x10
    • Start immediately with 0.9% Normal Saline at 1,000–1,500 mL/hour for the first 1–2 hours to restore effective circulating volume.
    • Subsequent fluid: Switch to 0.45% NaCl if corrected serum sodium is normal/elevated; continue 0.9% NaCl if hyponatremic.
    • The Glucose Transition Rule: When serum glucose falls to ≤200 mg/dL (in DKA) or ≤300 mg/dL (in HHS), ADD 5% DEXTROSE (D5W or D5 1/2NS) to IV fluids while maintaining insulin infusion! This prevents acute hypoglycemia and cerebral edema while continuing insulin to clear ketoacidosis.
  • Step 2 — Potassium Management Rules (Life-Saving!): 🔥 x12
    • Insulin drives potassium into cells and lowers serum K⁺ rapidly. Total-body potassium is ALWAYS depleted in DKA due to osmotic diuresis.
    • THE K⁺ THRESHOLD RULES:
      • If Serum K⁺ <3.3 mEq/L: HOLD INSULIN IMMEDIATELY! Administer aggressive IV Potassium Chloride (20–40 mEq/hr) until K⁺ >3.3 mEq/L to prevent fatal cardiac arrhythmias and respiratory muscle paralysis.
      • If Serum K⁺ 3.3–5.2 mEq/L: Start / continue insulin infusion, and add 20–30 mEq K⁺ per liter of IV fluid to maintain serum K⁺ between 4.0 and 5.0 mEq/L.
      • If Serum K⁺ >5.2 mEq/L: Start insulin, but do NOT give supplemental potassium; check K⁺ every 1–2 hours.
  • Step 3 — Continuous IV Insulin Therapy: 🔥 x8
    • Regular Insulin continuous IV infusion at 0.1 units/kg/hour (or 0.14 U/kg/hr without bolus). Target rate of glucose decline: 50–75 mg/dL per hour.
    • Resolution Criteria of DKA: Glucose <200 mg/dL PLUS two of the following: Serum HCO₃⁻ ≥15 mEq/L, venous pH >7.30, and normalized Anion Gap (≤12 mEq/L).
    • Subcutaneous Insulin Transition: Administer subcutaneous basal insulin (Glargine / Detemir) 2 hours BEFORE discontinuing IV insulin infusion to prevent rebound ketoacidosis.
  • Acute Hypoglycemia Management (Whipple Triad):
    • Symptoms of hypoglycemia (sweating, tremors, hunger, confusion) + low plasma glucose (<70 mg/dL) + symptom relief with glucose intake.
    • Conscious Patient: "Rule of 15" → 15–20 g of rapid-acting oral carbohydrates (fruit juice, glucose tablets), recheck in 15 minutes.
    • Unconscious / Comatose Patient: IV 50% Dextrose (D50W, 25–50 mL bolus) immediately. If IV access unavailable → IM Glucagon (1 mg).

Topic 6 of 7 • Internal Medicine • Endocrine & Electrolytes

6. Adrenal & Pituitary Disorders

🔥 High Yield (30×) 14 Qs

1. Adrenal Insufficiency: Acute Crisis vs. Chronic Addison Disease 🔥 x30

Feature Primary Adrenal Insufficiency (Addison Disease) Acute Adrenal (Addisonian) Crisis 🔥 x10
Etiology & Pathophysiology Autoimmune adrenalitis (80% in developed countries; 21-hydroxylase antibodies) or Tuberculosis (developing countries). Destruction of all 3 adrenal cortical zones (loss of cortisol, aldosterone, and androgens). Acute decompensation precipitated by severe physiologic stress (infection, trauma, surgery) in a patient with underlying adrenal insufficiency, or abrupt cessation of chronic exogenous corticosteroids, or bilateral adrenal hemorrhage (Waterhouse-Friderichsen in meningococcemia).
Clinical Presentation Chronic fatigue, generalized weakness, anorexia, weight loss, salt craving, postural orthostatic dizziness, and Cutaneous Hyperpigmentation (palmar creases, oral buccal mucosa, friction areas → high ACTH stimulates melanocyte MC1 receptors). Life-Threatening Shock: Severe refractory hypotension, hypovolemic shock unresponsive to vasopressors/fluids.
Acute Abdomen Simulation: Severe nausea, vomiting, and abdominal pain with guarding / rigidity (pseudoperitonitis) simulating acute surgical perforation!
Electrolyte & Metabolic Signature Classic Triad:
• Hyponatremia (<130 mEq/L)
• Hyperkalemia (>5.0 mEq/L)
• Non-anion gap metabolic acidosis (due to aldosterone deficiency).
• Mild fasting hypoglycemia.		 Profound Metabolic Hallmarks:
• Severe Hyponatremia (e.g., Na⁺ 119 mEq/L)
• Severe Hyperkalemia (e.g., K⁺ 6.3 mEq/L)
• Severe Hypoglycemia (e.g., Glucose 50–55 mg/dL) → contributes to altered sensorium.
Diagnostic Confirmation 1. Morning (8 AM) Serum Cortisol & Plasma ACTH.
2. Cosyntropin (Synthetic ACTH) Stimulation Test: Peak cortisol <18 µg/dL confirms diagnosis. High ACTH indicates primary adrenal failure; low ACTH indicates secondary (pituitary) failure.		 Clinical diagnosis! Do NOT delay life-saving treatment for confirmatory cosyntropin testing! Draw random baseline cortisol and ACTH tube, then treat immediately.
Management Protocols Lifelong oral replacement:
• Hydrocortisone (15–25 mg/day in divided doses).
• Fludrocortisone (0.05–0.2 mg/day mineralocorticoid).
Educate on "stress dosing" (double or triple oral dose during febrile illness/minor surgery).		 Emergency Resuscitation Protocol:
1. IV Hydrocortisone 100 mg IV stat bolus, followed by 100 mg IV q8h (at high doses, hydrocortisone provides full mineralocorticoid activity; fludrocortisone is NOT needed acutely).
2. Aggressive volume expansion with IV 0.9% Normal Saline + 5% Dextrose (D5NS) to correct hypotension, hyponatremia, and hypoglycemia.

2. Cushing Syndrome: Diagnostic Flowchart & Diurnal Rhythm 🔥 x26

  • Etiology & Subtypes:
    • Exogenous (Iatrogenic): Most common cause by far (chronic therapeutic glucocorticoid use). Characterized by low endogenous ACTH and bilateral adrenal atrophy.
    • ACTH-Dependent: Cushing Disease (Pituitary ACTH-secreting adenoma, 70%) or Ectopic ACTH secretion (Small cell lung carcinoma, 10%).
    • ACTH-Independent: Adrenal adenoma or carcinoma (20%).
  • Clinical Hallmark: Central / truncal obesity, "moon facies", dorsocervical fat pad ("buffalo hump"), purple cutaneous abdominal striae (>1 cm wide), proximal muscle weakness (difficulty climbing stairs), hypertension, easy bruising, and osteoporosis.
  • Diagnostic Screening Algorithm: 🔥 x8
    • Earliest Physiological Biochemical Abnormality: Loss of Normal Circadian (Diurnal) Cortisol Variation! (In healthy individuals, cortisol falls to an undetectable nadir at midnight; Cushing patients fail to suppress).
    • 1st-Line Confirmatory Screening Tests (Require ≥2 positive tests):
      1. Late-Night Salivary Cortisol (≥2 measurements at 23:00–24:00).
      2. 24-Hour Urinary Free Cortisol (UFC) (levels >3 times upper limit of normal).
      3. Low-Dose Dexamethasone Suppression Test (LDDST): 1 mg dexamethasone at 23:00 → Serum cortisol at 08:00 fails to suppress below 1.8 µg/dL (50 nmol/L).
    • Step 2 — Plasma ACTH Level: If low (<5 pg/mL) → Adrenal CT. If high (>20 pg/mL) → High-Dose Dexamethasone Suppression Test (HDDST) and Pituitary MRI to differentiate Cushing Disease (suppresses >50%) from Ectopic ACTH (fails to suppress).

3. Pituitary Disorders & Hyponatremia Syndromes 🔥 x20

  • Anterior Pituitary Cell Lineages (Mnemonic: "B-FLAT"): x2
    • Basophilic Cells: Synthesize and secrete FSH, LH, ACTH, and TSH.
    • Acidophilic Cells: Synthesize and secrete Growth Hormone (GH) and Prolactin (PRL).
  • Hyperprolactinemia & Drug-Induced Galactorrhea: 🔥 x6
    • Dopamine tonically inhibits prolactin secretion via D₂ receptors on lactotrophs.
    • D₂ Antagonist Medications: Metoclopramide, Phenothiazines, Haloperidol, Risperidone block dopamine receptors → disinhibition of prolactin → Hyperprolactinemia (prolactin 50–150 ng/mL) → clinical triad of Galactorrhea, Oligomenorrhea / Amenorrhea, and Infertility.
    • Prolactinoma (>200 ng/mL): 1st-line treatment is medical therapy with Dopamine Agonists (Cabergoline, Bromocriptine).
  • Syndrome of Inappropriate ADH Secretion (SIADH): 🔥 x6
    • Caused by ectopic ADH secretion from Small Cell Lung Carcinoma (SCLC) (heavy smoker with 50 pack-years), CNS disorders (stroke, trauma), or drugs (SSRIs, carbamazepine).
    • Diagnostic Laboratory Profile:
      • Euvolemic Hyponatremia: Serum Na⁺ <130 mEq/L with normal skin turgor, no edema, no orthostasis.
      • Low Serum Osmolality: <275 mOsm/kg.
      • Low Blood Urea Nitrogen (BUN <10 mg/dL, e.g., 5 mg/dL) and low serum uric acid (due to volume expansion).
      • Inappropriately Concentrated Urine: Urine Osmolality >100 mOsm/kg (typically >300) and Urine Sodium >20–40 mEq/L.
    • Treatment: Fluid restriction (<800–1000 mL/day). For severe acute symptomatic hyponatremia (seizures, coma) → 3% Hypertonic Saline (correct slowly ≤8 mEq/L in 24 hours to prevent Osmotic Demyelination Syndrome / Central Pontine Myelinolysis).
⚠️ Model Answer vs. AI Recommended Answer

تنبيه هام للامتحان بخصوص الفحوصات الأولية لمتلازمة كوشينغ (سؤال Q735):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: التغير الكيميائي الحيوي الفسيولوجي الأبكر والأكثر حساسية في متلازمة كوشينغ هو فقدان التباين النهاري لإفراز الكورتيزول (Loss of normal circadian / diurnal cortisol variation - Option E)، حيث يفشل الكورتيزول في الهبوط إلى مستواه الأدنى عند منتصف الليل، وهو الأساس العلمي لفحص كورتيزول اللعاب الليلي (Late-night salivary cortisol). فحص كورتيزول الصباح (Morning cortisol) يعاني من تداخل كبير مع المستويات الطبيعية للأصحاء صباحاً.

في الـ Model Answer في بنك الأسئلة: تم في بعض النماذج وضع Option C (Morning cortisol) كإجابة نموذجية مختصرة روتينية، ولكن المرجع المعتمد يثبت تفوق فحص منتصف الليل لفقدان التباين اليومي.

7. Calcium, Parathyroid & Acid-Base

🔥 High Yield (26×) 11 Qs

1. Hypocalcemia & Post-Thyroidectomy Tetany 🔥 x26

Clinical Parameter Presentation & Pathophysiology Immediate Clinical Action
Surgical Etiology Inadvertent devascularization, trauma, or excision of parathyroid glands during Total Thyroidectomy or central neck dissection. Typically manifests 24 to 48 hours postoperatively. Anticipate in all total thyroidectomy patients; monitor ionized and total serum calcium postoperatively.
Biochemical Threshold Acute neuromuscular excitability and tetany manifest when Total Serum Calcium <7.0–7.5 mg/dL (or ionized Ca²⁺ <4.0 mg/dL). Always check serum albumin to calculate corrected calcium: Corrected Ca = Total Ca + 0.8 × (4.0 − Albumin).
Neuromuscular Hallmark • Perioral numbness and digital paresthesias (tingling in fingers/toes).
• Chvostek Sign: Tapping facial nerve anterior to ear lobe produces twitching of ipsilateral facial muscles.
• Trousseau Sign: Inflating BP cuff >20 mmHg above systolic pressure for 3 minutes produces painful Carpopedal Spasm (adduction of thumb, flexion of MCP joints, extension of IP joints).
• ECG Finding: Prolonged QT Interval (predisposes to ventricular arrhythmias).		 Check urgent Serum Calcium, Magnesium, and Phosphate. (Hypomagnesemia causes functional hypoparathyroidism refractory to calcium replacement alone).
Emergency Management Acute symptomatic hypocalcemia / tetany is an immediate medical emergency! Administer IV Infusion of Calcium Gluconate (1–2 g in 50–100 mL D5W over 10–20 minutes) under continuous cardiac monitoring. Transition to oral Calcium Carbonate/Citrate + active Calcitriol (1,25-(OH)₂-Vitamin D).

2. Hyperphosphatemia & Secondary Hyperparathyroidism in CKD 🔥 x20

  • Mechanism of Hyperphosphatemia Symptoms: Elevated serum phosphate complexes with ionized calcium → precipitates calcium phosphate crystals into soft tissues → clinical manifestations (neuromuscular tetany) result directly from the acute drop in Serum Free Ionized Calcium. x2
  • Secondary Hyperparathyroidism in Chronic Kidney Disease (CKD): 🔥 x6
    • Pathophysiology: Declining GFR → reduced renal phosphate excretion (Hyperphosphatemia) + loss of renal 1α-hydroxylase (calcitriol deficiency) → hypocalcemia → chronic parathyroid gland hyperplasia → massive compensatory secretion of PTH.
    • Classic Hemodialysis Vignette: Patient on chronic hemodialysis for 5–10 years presenting with diffuse bone pain, fractures, and pruritus (Renal Osteodystrophy / Osteitis fibrosa cystica).
    • Diagnostic Laboratory Triad: Hypocalcemia (e.g., 8.4 mg/dL) + Hyperphosphatemia (e.g., 5.6 mg/dL) + Markedly Elevated Parathyroid Hormone (PTH >150–500 pg/mL).
    • Treatment: Dietary phosphate restriction, Oral Phosphate Binders (Sevelamer, Calcium Acetate) taken with meals, Calcitriol / Vitamin D analogs, and Calcimimetics (Cinacalcet).
  • Hypokalemic Syndromes & Postoperative Adynamic Ileus: 🔥 x8
    • Postoperative Paralytic Ileus: Characterized by diffuse abdominal distension, nausea, vomiting, obstipation, and absent bowel sounds without mechanical obstruction. The most common underlying electrolyte defect is Hypokalemia (potassium is essential for smooth muscle resting membrane potential and bowel peristalsis).
    • Loop Diuretic Toxicity: High-dose Furosemide (80 mg/day) inhibits Na⁺-K⁺-2Cl⁻ cotransporters in the thick ascending limb → urinary wasting of K⁺ and H⁺ → severe hypokalemia causing skeletal muscle weakness, cramps, hyporeflexia, and U-waves on ECG.

3. Arterial Blood Gas (ABG) Metabolic Acid-Base Disorders 🔥 x22

Disorder Arterial pH Serum HCO₃⁻ Arterial PaCO₂ & Compensation
Primary (Partially Compensated) Metabolic Acidosis
🔥 x4		 <7.35 (Severe Acidemia, e.g., 7.10) Markedly Decreased (<22, e.g., 5 mmol/L) Decreased (<35, e.g., 25 mmHg) → Respiratory compensation via Kussmaul hyperventilation. (pH has NOT returned to normal).
Fully Compensated Metabolic Acidosis
🔥 x8		 Normal (7.35 – 7.45, e.g., 7.35) Decreased (<22, e.g., 16 mmol/L)
(Negative Base Excess, e.g., −8 mmol/L)		 Markedly Decreased (e.g., 2.5 kPa ~19 mmHg) → Complete respiratory compensation successfully returns arterial pH to normal range.
Hypochloremic Hypokalemic Metabolic Alkalosis
🔥 x6		 Elevated (>7.45) Markedly Elevated (>28 mEq/L) Elevated (>45 mmHg) (compensatory hypoventilation). Caused by gastric outlet obstruction / severe vomiting (loss of HCl and KCl).
⚠️ Model Answer vs. AI Recommended Answer

تنبيه هام للامتحان بخصوص علاج القلاء الأيضي الناتج عن انسداد مخرج المعدة (سؤال Q917):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: في حالات القيء المستمر أو انسداد بواب المعدة (Pyloric / Gastric Outlet Obstruction) يفقد المريض حمض الهيدروكلوريك والبوتاسيوم مسبباً Hypochloremic Hypokalemic Metabolic Alkalosis مع جفاف شديد. السائل المفضل للإنعاش المبدئي الفوري واستعادة الحجم خارج الخلوي وعجز الكلوريد هو 0.9% Normal Saline (Option A)، ويُضاف إليه كلوريد البوتاسيوم بمجرد التأكد من إخراج البول.

في الـ Model Answer في بنك الأسئلة: تم اعتماد Option A (0.9% Normal Saline) كإجابة نموذجية رسمية، مع التحذير من الخيار القديم غير العلمي (KCl in 5% glucose) لأن إعطاء الجلوكوز دون صوديوم يحفز الإنسولين فيُدخل البوتاسيوم داخل الخلايا ويفاقم نقص البوتاسيوم في الدم.

Topic 1 of 2 • Internal Medicine • Ophthalmology

1. Acute Angle-Closure Glaucoma

🔥 High Yield (26×) Qs

1. Acute Angle-Closure Glaucoma (AACG): Presentation & Emergency Protocols 🔥 x26

Clinical Domain Pathophysiology & Presentation Emergency Management Rationale
Pathophysiology & Precipitants Pupillary block → forward bowing of peripheral iris against trabecular meshwork → sudden complete occlusion of iridocorneal filtration angle → rapid, catastrophic surge in Intraocular Pressure (IOP >40–70 mmHg; normal 10–21 mmHg).
Triggers: Pupillary dilation in dark theaters/dim lighting, emotional stress, anticholinergics (Atropine), sympathomimetics, or mydriatics in anatomically predisposed shallow anterior chambers (elderly, hyperopic females).		 Ophthalmic emergency! Prolonged elevated IOP causes irreversible ischemic necrosis of retinal ganglion cells and optic nerve within hours → permanent blindness.
Cardinal Clinical Presentation • Severe, excruciating unilateral ocular pain and brow ache.
• Marked visual acuity loss and perception of colored "rainbow halos" around lights.
• Systemic Autonomic Symptoms: Severe ipsilateral frontal headache, intense nausea, vomiting, and abdominal pain (frequently misdiagnosed as acute migraine or acute abdomen!).		 Any elderly patient presenting with severe headache, vomiting, and acute red eye MUST have urgent tonometry / eye examination to rule out acute glaucoma!
Physical & Slit-Lamp Exam • Ciliary flush ("red eye" with circumcorneal injection).
• Steamy, hazy, edematous cornea with loss of corneal luster.
• Fixed, mid-dilated, non-reactive pupil (4–6 mm).
• Shallow anterior chamber on slit-lamp.
• "Rock-hard" globe on gentle finger palpation.		 Measure IOP immediately with applanation tonometry (Tono-Pen or Goldmann tonometry).
Stepwise Medical & Laser Protocol Medical Resuscitation (Multi-Drug IOP Lowering):
1. IV Acetazolamide (500 mg IV stat): Carbonic anhydrase inhibitor → reduces aqueous humor production.
2. Topical Beta-Blocker: Timolol 0.5% drops (decreases aqueous humor synthesis).
3. Topical Alpha-2 Agonist: Apraclonidine 1% or Brimonidine.
4. Osmotic Agent: IV Mannitol (1–2 g/kg) if IOP >50 mmHg refractory to drops.
5. Topical Miotic: Pilocarpine 1–2% drops (instill once IOP <40 mmHg to constrict pupil, pull peripheral iris away from angle, and restore outflow).		 Definitive Surgical Cure:
Bilateral Laser Peripheral Iridotomy (LPI) → creates a full-thickness bypass aperture in the peripheral iris to equilibrate pressure between anterior and posterior chambers.
GOLDEN RULE: The contralateral "fellow" eye MUST also receive prophylactic Laser Peripheral Iridotomy (has >50% risk of acute angle closure).

2. Differential Diagnosis of Acute Monocular Vision Loss 🔥 x24

Clinical Condition Pain Status Fundoscopic & Slit-Lamp Findings Distinguishing Features
Acute Angle-Closure Glaucoma
🔥 x6		 EXCRUCIATINGLY PAINFUL Ciliary injection, hazy/cloudy cornea, fixed mid-dilated pupil, shallow chamber, rock-hard globe (IOP >50). Associated with halos around lights, headache, nausea, and vomiting.
Central Retinal Artery Occlusion (CRAO) PAINLESS Diffuse ischemic retinal pallor / cloudy swelling with a pathognomonic "Cherry-Red Spot" at the fovea (thin foveal retina reveals underlying choroid); boxcar segmentation of blood columns. Sudden, profound, catastrophic monocular blindness ("curtain falling"). Caused by embolic occlusion (carotid atheroma, atrial fibrillation). Immediate ocular massage + anterior chamber paracentesis.
Central Retinal Vein Occlusion (CRVO) PAINLESS "Blood and Thunder" Fundus: Widespread diffuse flame-shaped and blot retinal hemorrhages in all 4 quadrants, tortuous engorged veins, severe optic disc edema, and cotton-wool spots. Sudden monocular visual loss; associated with hypertension, diabetes, and hypercoagulable states.
Retinal Detachment (Rhegmatogenous) PAINLESS Elevated, undulating, wrinkled, grayish-white retina ballooning forward into the vitreous cavity with a visible tear/break. Preceded by Photopsia (flashes of light) and sudden shower of floaters, followed by a dark peripheral "curtain / veil pulled across vision".
Vitreous Hemorrhage PAINLESS Loss of red reflex; dark blood obscuring retinal details on direct fundoscopy. Sudden appearance of dark floaters, cobwebs, or reddish haze. Most common cause: Proliferative Diabetic Retinopathy.

Topic 2 of 2 • Internal Medicine • Ophthalmology

2. Mechanical Corneal Abrasion

🔥 High Yield (24×) 2 Qs

1. Corneal Abrasions: Examination & Outpatient Management Rules 🔥 x20

Clinical Aspect Diagnostic Findings & Protocol Strict Clinical Rules & Contraindications
Clinical Presentation Sudden onset of severe, sharp ocular pain, intense foreign body sensation, profuse reflex lacrimation (epiphora), photophobia, and blepharospasm following minor mechanical trauma (fingernail, tree branch, paper edge, contact lens). Always evert the upper and lower eyelids to inspect for and irrigate out trapped subtarsal foreign bodies!
Diagnostic Confirmation Instill Topical Fluorescein Dye and examine under Cobalt-Blue Filter Light (or Wood\'s lamp).
Epithelial basement membrane defect fluoresces bright yellow-green at the abrasion site. (Seidel test is negative → confirms no full-thickness globe penetration).		 Document absence of corneal infiltrate or hypopyon (which would signify secondary infectious bacterial keratitis or corneal ulcer).
First-Line Outpatient Therapy 1. Prophylactic Topical Antibiotics:
• Non-Contact Lens Wearers: Topical Erythromycin 0.5% ointment or Polymyxin B/Trimethoprim drops QID.
• Contact Lens Wearers: Mandatory antipseudomonal coverage → Topical Fluoroquinolones (Ciprofloxacin 0.3% or Ofloxacin) or Aminoglycoside (Tobramycin) drops to cover Pseudomonas aeruginosa.
2. Analgesia: Oral NSAIDs / Acetaminophen; topical cycloplegic (Cyclopentolate) for severe ciliary spasm.		 Follow up in 24 hours to ensure re-epithelialization. Discontinue contact lens wear until cornea is completely healed for ≥1 week.
Strict Contraindications BLACK BOX WARNINGS:
• NEVER PRESCRIBE TOPICAL ANESTHETICS FOR HOME USE: Repeated use of topical Proparacaine / Tetracaine inhibits corneal epithelial migration, causes toxic keratopathy, permanent corneal ulceration, and corneal melting/perforation!
• NEVER PRESCRIBE TOPICAL CORTICOSTEROIDS: Delays epithelial healing, promotes bacterial superinfection, and potentiates fungal or Herpes simplex keratitis.
• AVOID ROUTINE EYE PATCHING: Clinical trials prove patching does NOT decrease pain or accelerate healing, but creates a dark, warm, hypoxic environment that fosters bacterial infection.		 Topical anesthetics are strictly diagnostic in the clinic and must NEVER be dispensed to patients.

2. Intraocular Metallic Foreign Body: Imaging Rules & MRI Contraindication 🔥 x24

  • High-Risk Mechanism: Striking metal on metal (e.g., hammering nails at a construction site, grinding wheel, drilling) without protective eye goggles → high-velocity metallic projectile strikes the eye.
  • Clinical Signs of Penetrating Globe Injury: Peaked / teardrop-shaped pupil pointing toward the entry site, shallow anterior chamber, subconjunctival hemorrhage, hyphema, or positive Seidel Test (streaming of fluorescein diluted by leaking clear aqueous humor under cobalt-blue light).
  • Diagnostic Neuroimaging Selection: 🔥 x10
    • Safest & Most Accurate Modality: Thin-Section Non-Contrast CT Scan of the Orbits / Facial Bones (axial and coronal 1-mm cuts). CT provides precise anatomical localization of the foreign body (intraocular vs. intraorbital vs. scleral) and assesses for associated fractures or vitreous hemorrhage.
    • ABSOLUTE LIFE-AND-SIGHT CONTRAINDICATION: Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) is STRICTLY CONTRAINDICATED in any patient with suspected or potential metallic foreign body! The powerful magnetic field generates magnetic torque and kinetic movement of the ferromagnetic fragment → catastrophic laceration of intraocular tissues, retinal transection, and irreversible blindness!
    • Plain Orbital Radiography (X-ray): Can confirm radiopaque metallic opacity, but lacks precision to distinguish intraocular from extraocular/retrobulbar foreign body.
  • Emergency Stabilization: Place a rigid, non-pressure Protective Eye Shield (Fox shield) resting on orbital bony margins; elevate head of bed 30–45°; administer IV antiemetics (prevent Valsalva and extrusion of intraocular contents); update tetanus vaccination; give IV broad-spectrum antibiotics (Vancomycin + Ceftazidime); emergent Ophthalmology consultation for surgical vitrectomy and foreign body extraction.
⚠️ Model Answer vs. AI Recommended Answer

تنبيه هام للامتحان بخصوص الجسم الغريب المعدني داخل العين (سؤال Q919):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: في حالات إصابات العمل أثناء طرق المسامير بالمطرقة (Hammering nails) والاشتباه في وجود جسم غريب معدني داخل العين (Suspected metallic intraocular foreign body)، الفحص الأكثر دقة وأماناً بلا منازع هو Non-contrast thin-section Orbital CT scan (Option D). بينما يُعتبر الـ MRI (Option C) ممنوعاً منعاً باتاً ومطلقاً (Strictly Contraindicated) لأن المجال المغناطيسي القوي يؤدي إلى تحرك ودوران الشظية المعدنية مما يسبب تهتك أنسجة العين والعمى الدائم الفوري.

في الـ Model Answer في بنك الأسئلة: تم في نموذج الإجابة القديم وضع Option C (MRI) بالخطأ، وهو خطأ جسيم في مفتاح بنك الأسئلة يجب الانتباه له تماماً، حيث أن التطبيق العملي والصحيح طبياً هو اختيار CT scan (Option D).

Topic 1 of 8 • Surgery • General Surgery

1. Abdominal Wall & Groin Hernias

🔥 High Yield (35×) 26 Qs

1. Surgical Anatomy of the Inguinal Canal & Spermatic Cord 🔥 x9

  • Boundaries of the Inguinal Canal ("MALT"): 🔥 x5
    • Roof (Superior): Arching musculoaponeurotic fibers of Internal Oblique & Transversus Abdominis.
    • Anterior Wall: Aponeurosis of the External Oblique (reinforced laterally by internal oblique fibers).
    • Floor (Inferior): Inguinal Ligament (Poupart) and reinforced medially by the lacunar ligament (Gimbernat).
    • Posterior Wall: Transversalis Fascia throughout; reinforced medially by the Conjoint Tendon (falx inguinalis).
    • Strict Exam Rule: Pectineal Ligament of Cooper is NOT a boundary of the inguinal canal (it forms the posterior border of the femoral canal).
  • Contents of the Spermatic Cord: 🔥 x4
    • Strict Cord Nerve: Genital Branch of the Genitofemoral Nerve (travels inside the spermatic cord and innervates the cremaster muscle and scrotal skin). Note: The Ilioinguinal nerve runs on top of the cord through the superficial ring, not inside the internal spermatic fascia.
    • 3 Arteries: Testicular artery, Cremasteric artery, Artery to ductus deferens.
    • 3 Other Structures: Vas deferens, Pampiniform venous plexus, Lymphatics.

2. Groin Hernias Comparison: Indirect vs. Direct vs. Femoral 🔥 x21

Feature Indirect Inguinal Hernia x2 Direct Inguinal Hernia 🔥 x11 Femoral Hernia 🔥 x8
Anatomic Origin & Neck Deep (internal) inguinal ring (LATERAL to inferior epigastric vessels) Hesselbach triangle defect (MEDIAL to inferior epigastric vessels) Femoral ring (BELOW the inguinal ligament)
Relation to Pubic Tubercle Above and medial to pubic tubercle Above and medial to pubic tubercle Inferior & lateral to pubic tubercle
Pathophysiology Congenital: Failure of closure of Processus Vaginalis Acquired: Weakness of transversalis fascia (Hesselbach triangle) Acquired: Widened femoral ring in multiparous females
Scrotal Descent Frequently descends into the scrotum (complete hernia) Rarely enters scrotum (diffuse forward bulge) Never enters scrotum (remains in upper thigh)
Internal Ring Occlusion Test Bulge stays completely controlled on coughing Bulge escapes / reappears medially on coughing Bulge remains visible below inguinal ligament
Strangulation Risk Moderate (highest in neonates/infants) Extremely low (wide, non-rigid neck) Highest risk of strangulation (>45%) → mandates urgent repair!

3. Pediatric Hernias, Obturator Hernia & Rectus Sheath Hematoma 🔥 x34

  • Pediatric Indirect Inguinal Hernia (Patent Processus Vaginalis): 🔥 x27
    • Definitive Surgical Technique: Herniotomy Alone (High Ligation of the Sac) at the internal ring. Posterior wall repair or prosthetic mesh is strictly contraindicated in children!
    • Irreducible Incarcerated Infantile Hernia (9-Month-Old): Immediate Emergency Herniotomy (high risk of testicular infarction and bowel necrosis). 🔥 x20
    • Successfully Reduced Pediatric Groin Hernia (2-Year-Old): Admit and schedule Semi-Elective Herniotomy during the same hospital stay. 🔥 x7
  • Strangulated Obturator Hernia: 🔥 x6
    • Clinical Vignette: Frail, emaciated elderly woman with recent weight loss (loss of protective preperitoneal fat cushion in obturator canal).
    • Pathognomonic Finding: Mechanical small-bowel obstruction + pain radiating down the medial thigh to the knee relieved by thigh flexion (Howship-Romberg Sign) due to obturator nerve compression.
  • Rectus Sheath Hematoma (Appendicitis Mimic): 🔥 x6
    • Mechanism & Presentation: Sudden forceful abdominal wall contraction / twisting (e.g., playing tennis or severe coughing in anticoagulated patient) causing rupture of the inferior epigastric artery.
    • Clinical Hallmark: Acute severe RLQ pain and a tender abdominal wall mass that remains palpable during abdominal wall contraction (Positive Carnett Sign). Diagnosis confirmed on Abdominal Ultrasound / CT; managed non-operatively.
  • Postoperative Complication: Fever & Leukocytosis at 48h Post-Robotic Repair: x3
    • Fever (38.8°C) and WBC 18,500/mm³ 48 hours following robotic ventral hernia repair → order urgent CT Abdomen/Pelvis with IV Contrast to rule out occult bowel injury / mesh infection.

4. Incarcerated vs. Strangulated Hernia Emergencies 🔥 x23

Feature Incarcerated Hernia x1 Strangulated Hernia (Groin or Umbilical) 🔥 x22
Vascular Status Intact mesenteric blood supply (mechanical irreducibility) Arterial occlusion → ischemic gangrene & bowel necrosis
Physical Examination Irreducible swelling; mild localized tenderness; overlying skin is normal and cool Tense, exquisitely tender, no cough impulse, overlying erythema, edema & warmth
Systemic Signs & Labs Afebrile; normal pulse; normal WBC count; no systemic toxicity High fever, tachycardia, marked leukocytosis, elevated lactic acid
Emergency Management Urgent surgical repair during same admission MANUAL REDUCTION IS STRICTLY CONTRAINDICATED (risk of reduction en masse or reducing gangrenous bowel)!
Immediate resuscitation: Early aggressive IV crystalloid fluids, NPO, NG decompression, broad-spectrum IV antibiotics → Emergency Exploratory Laparotomy / Hernia Repair.

Topic 2 of 8 • Surgery • General Surgery

2. Breast Disorders

🔥 High Yield (28×) 18 Qs

1. Benign Breast Pain & Fibrocystic Disease 🔥 x10

Feature Fibrocystic Changes (Fibroadenosis) 🔥 x7 Benign Focal Mastalgia x3
Patient Demographics Premenopausal female (age 25–45 years) Young reproductive female (age 20–30 years)
Clinical Presentation Bilateral, diffuse, lumpy/nodular breast texture that fluctuates with menses (pain and swelling peak premenstrually) Localized, non-cyclical breast ache with completely normal physical exam (no discrete palpable mass)
Diagnostic Rule Ultrasound demonstrates diffuse multiple benign microcysts Diagnostic ultrasound confirms normal breast tissue and absence of space-occupying lesion
Management Protocol Reassurance, well-fitting supportive bra, evening primrose oil, NSAIDs Patient Reassurance & Clinical Follow-Up.
GOLDEN RULE: Surgical intervention is NEVER indicated for non-palpable mastalgia!

2. Lactational Mastitis & Breast Abscess Protocols 🔥 x14

Clinical Parameter Small / Deep Abscess (<5 cm, Intact Skin) 🔥 x6 Large / Pointing Abscess (≥5 cm, Thinned Skin) 🔥 x8
Clinical Presentation Breastfeeding mother presenting with high fever (38.8–39.2°C), chills, localized breast erythema, and tender fluctuant swelling Severe throbbing breast pain, high fever, fluctuant mass with thinned, shiny, necrotic or pointing overlying skin
Microbiology & Nursing Most common pathogen: Staphylococcus aureus.
Continue breastfeeding / breast pumping from the affected breast (prevents milk stasis and accelerates resolution).		 S. aureus (frequently MRSA). Continue milk expression; send aspirate/pus for Gram stain and culture.
Interventional Drainage First-Line: Ultrasound-Guided Fine-Needle Aspiration (FNA) repeated every 48 hours + oral antistaphylococcal antibiotics (Cephalexin or Flucloxacillin). Surgical Incision and Drainage (I&D) with breaking of all internal loculations and packing + systemic IV/oral antibiotics.

3. Breast Carcinoma, Paget Disease, LCIS & Core Biopsy Gold Standard 🔥 x14

Feature Classic LCIS 🔥 x6 Paget Disease of Nipple x2 Locally Advanced Breast Cancer 🔥 x4
Clinical Presentation Incidental histological finding on core needle biopsy for unrelated mammographic microcalcifications (no discrete lump) Unilateral, persistent (≥3 months) eczematoid, scaly, crusted or weeping lesion of the nipple-areolar complex Hard, irregular, painless breast mass >5 cm, tethered/fixed to pectoral fascia or skin, with matted axillary lymph nodes
Pathologic Entity Non-malignant proliferative biomarker conferring equal bilateral risk for future invasive ductal carcinoma (~25–30% lifetime risk) Malignant intraepidermal migration of ductal cells (Paget cells) representing underlying DCIS or invasive adenocarcinoma Invasive ductal carcinoma (NST) with locoregional lymphatic spread
Next Step / Action Close Clinical & Annual Mammographic Surveillance ± chemoprevention (Tamoxifen). Surgical excision is NOT required! Full-Thickness Punch Biopsy of the Nipple ± diagnostic bilateral mammography and breast ultrasound. 1. Initial step: Diagnostic Bilateral Mammography & Ultrasound.
2. Pathological confirmation: Tru-Cut Core Needle Biopsy (Option C, AI Recommended Answer).
  • Hepatic Metastasis from Primary Breast Carcinoma: x2
    • Patient with previous history of treated breast cancer presenting 2 years later with RUQ ache, hepatomegaly, and multiple bilobar hypodense liver lesions on CT → confirms Metastatic Breast Carcinoma.
⚠️ Model Answer vs. AI Recommended Answer

1. تنبيه بخصوص خراج الثدي أثناء الرضاعة (سؤال Q1095):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: سيدة مرضعة عمرها 31 عاماً تعاني من ألم حاد نابض واحمرار وتورم بالثدي مع حرارة عالية (38.8°C) وقشعريرة لمدة يومين فقط. التشخيص الطبي المعتمد والصحيح هو Lactational Breast Abscess / Mastitis (Option A). وفي الـ Model Answer في بنك الأسئلة تم وضع Option B (Inflammatory breast carcinoma) بالخطأ، وهو خطأ واضح لأن الـ Inflammatory Carcinoma مرض ورمي مزمن خبيث لا يحدث في يومين مع حرارة وقشعريرة في أم مرضعة.

2. تنبيه بخصوص الفحص النسيجي لكتلة الثدي الكبيرة (سؤال Q1094):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: الفحص النسيجي المعياري الذهبي لتشخيص أورام الثدي المشتبهة هو Tru-Cut Core Needle Biopsy (Option C) لأنه يوفر عينة نسيجية كافية لتأكيد الغزو النسيجي (Invasive vs. In situ) وفحص المستقبلات الهرمونية (ER, PR, HER2). بينما في نماذج الأسئلة القديمة (Model Answer) تم اختيار FNAC (Option B) والذي يُعتبر فحصاً خلوياً غير كافٍ حديثاً لتحديد خطة العلاج الجراحي.

Topic 3 of 8 • Surgery • General Surgery

3. Esophageal & Gastric Surgery

🔥 High Yield (25×) 14 Qs

1. Esophageal Surgical Disorders Comparison 🔥 x14

Feature Achalasia x1 Esophageal Carcinoma 🔥 x8 Boerhaave Syndrome 🔥 x5
Pathophysiology Autoimmune/idiopathic loss of myenteric (Auerbach) plexus inhibitory neurons Squamous cell (smoker/alcohol, upper/mid) vs. Adenocarcinoma (Barrett, distal) Barogenic full-thickness transmural tear of left posterolateral distal esophagus following violent vomiting
Hallmark Symptoms Simultaneous progressive dysphagia to both SOLIDS and LIQUIDS + regurgitation Progressive dysphagia (SOLIDS FIRST, then liquids) + marked rapid weight loss Mackler Triad: Severe retching/vomiting → agonizing lower chest/epigastric pain → subcutaneous emphysema
Diagnostic Gold Standard High-Resolution Esophageal Manometry: Complete aperistalsis of esophageal body + failure of LES relaxation (residual pressure >15 mmHg) Upper Endoscopy with Multi-Quadrant Biopsy Gastrografin (water-soluble) Esophagogram or Chest CT with oral contrast: Extravasation of contrast + left hydropneumothorax / pleural effusion
Definitive Surgical Protocol Laparoscopic Heller Myotomy with Partial Fundoplication (Dor/Toupet) or Pneumatic Balloon Dilation Oncologic staging (CT + EUS + PET) → Neoadjuvant Chemoradiation → Transhiatal or Transthoracic Esophagectomy Surgical emergency! Resuscitation, broad-spectrum IV antibiotics → Emergency Exploratory Thoracotomy and Primary Esophageal Repair with mediastinal drainage (<24h)

2. Peptic Ulcer Emergencies & Gastric Carcinoma Surgery 🔥 x21

  • Acute Upper Gastrointestinal Bleeding (Hematemesis): x1
    • Immediate first priority: Two large-bore IV lines, IV crystalloid resuscitation, blood type-and-crossmatch → perform emergent Esophagogastroduodenoscopy (EGD) within 24 hours for dual-modality endoscopic hemostasis (hemoclips, electrocoagulation, or epinephrine injection).
  • Perforated Duodenal Ulcer: 🔥 x8
    • Etiology & Risk: Heavy chronic NSAID therapy, smoking, or *H. pylori* infection.
    • Clinical Hallmark: Sudden, catastrophic epigastric pain rapidly becoming generalized + "board-like" abdominal rigidity + free subdiaphragmatic gas (pneumoperitoneum) on upright chest radiograph.
    • Emergency Operative Repair: Primary Simple Closure with an Omental Pedicle Patch (Graham Patch Repair) and thorough peritoneal lavage. x2
  • Gastric Stump (Remnant) Carcinoma: x3
    • Presents classically 15–30 years after partial gastrectomy (Billroth II) for benign peptic ulcer disease due to chronic alkaline duodenogastric bile reflux promoting mucosal atrophy and metaplasia. Suspect in any patient with previous gastrectomy presenting with weight loss, postprandial fullness, and occult bleeding.

3. Gastric Outlet Obstruction (GOO): Benign vs. Malignant 🔥 x9

Clinical Parameter Benign Peptic Pyloric Stenosis 🔥 x4 Malignant Pyloric / Antral Obstruction 🔥 x5
Clinical Presentation Longstanding history of untreated PUD; presents with persistent non-bilious vomiting of undigested food ingested hours/days earlier, profound weight loss, and audible succussion splash >3h postprandially Elderly, cachectic patient with unresectable Stage IV gastric adenocarcinoma or metastatic pancreatic cancer presenting with complete gastric outlet obstruction
Metabolic Derangement Pathognomonic Hypochloremic, Hypokalemic Metabolic Alkalosis with Paradoxical Aciduria (due to profound gastric HCl loss and secondary renal aldosterone activation) Severe malnutrition, dehydration, and metabolic exhaustion
Definitive Management NG tube decompression, IV aggressive resuscitation with 0.9% Normal Saline + Potassium Chloride → Endoscopic balloon dilation or surgical antrectomy/gastrojejunostomy Endoscopic Self-Expanding Metal Stenting (SEMS) provides significantly superior short-term clinical outcomes, rapid resumption of oral solid diet, and lower 30-day morbidity compared to surgical gastrojejunostomy in terminal patients!

Topic 4 of 8 • Surgery • General Surgery

4. Hepatobiliary, Pancreatic & Splenic Surgery

🔥 High Yield (38×) 25 Qs

1. Acute Cholecystitis & Ascending Cholangitis 🔥 x10

Feature Acute Calculous Cholecystitis 🔥 x6 Acute Acalculous Cholecystitis x1 Acute Ascending Cholangitis x3
Etiology & Risk Persistent cystic duct obstruction by an impacted gallstone (>95%) Critically ill, septic, burn, trauma, or prolonged ICU patient (ischemia & bile stasis) Biliary outflow obstruction (choledocholithiasis or stricture) + secondary bacterial infection (*E. coli*, *Klebsiella*)
Clinical Presentation Constant severe RUQ pain radiating to the right infrascapular area (>6h) + low-grade fever + Positive Murphy Sign Unexplained sepsis, fever, and leukocytosis in a ventilated or critically ill patient with RUQ tenderness Charcot Triad: RUQ pain + high spiking fever/rigors + jaundice.
Reynolds Pentad: + Hypotension/shock + altered mental status (toxic cholangitis).
Diagnostic Findings First-Line Test: Abdominal Ultrasound 🔥 x4 (gallstones, thickened wall >4 mm, pericholecystic fluid, sonographic Murphy sign) Ultrasound demonstrates marked gallbladder distention and pericholecystic fluid WITHOUT gallstones Ultrasound/CT shows dilated CBD (>6–8 mm) and choledocholithiasis; elevated total bilirubin, ALP, and leukocytosis
Definitive Protocol IV antibiotics, bowel rest → Early Laparoscopic Cholecystectomy (within 72 hours of admission) x2 Urgent Percutaneous Cholecystostomy Tube Drainage (patient usually too unstable for laparotomy) Aggressive IV crystalloids, broad-spectrum IV antibiotics → Emergent Biliary Decompression via ERCP with sphincterotomy and stent placement
  • Gallstone Ileus (Cholecystoduodenal Fistula): 🔥 x10
    • Pathophysiology: Repeated chronic cholecystitis → adhesion and erosion of a large gallstone into the duodenum via a cholecystoduodenal fistula → stone travels down small bowel and impacts at the narrowest segment: the Ileocecal Valve.
    • Rigler Triad on Plain Abdominal X-Ray / CT: x2
      1. Pneumobilia (air in the biliary tree)
      2. Mechanical Small-Bowel Obstruction (dilated small-bowel loops with multiple air-fluid levels)
      3. Ectopic radiopaque gallstone in the right lower quadrant / pelvis.
    • Definitive Surgical Management: Emergency Enterolithotomy (Ileotomy and Gallstone Extraction) proximal to the obstruction. The cholecystoduodenal fistula and gallbladder are left untouched during the acute emergency! 🔥 x8
  • Fresh Frozen Plasma (FFP) Timing in Cirrhotic Coagulopathy: x1
    • In a cirrhotic patient with elevated INR requiring urgent cholecystectomy, transfuse Fresh Frozen Plasma "On Call to the Operating Room" immediately prior to surgery, because Factor VII has a very short half-life of only 4–6 hours.
  • Hepatic Adenoma Management in Young Females: 🔥 x5
    • Young woman on long-term oral contraceptive pills (OCPs) with a solitary 6-cm (≥5 cm) hepatic adenoma on MRI → Discontinue OCPs + Schedule Definitive Surgical Resection due to substantial risks of spontaneous life-threatening rupture/hemoperitoneum and malignant transformation to hepatocellular carcinoma.

2. Pancreatic Pseudocyst vs. Pancreatic Head Adenocarcinoma 🔥 x34

Feature Mature Pancreatic Pseudocyst 🔥 x18 Pancreatic Head Adenocarcinoma 🔥 x16
Timeline & Patient Develops ≥4–6 weeks following an episode of acute severe pancreatitis Elderly patient (60–75 years) presenting with profound anorexia, asthenia, and progressive weight loss
Clinical Hallmarks Persistent epigastric fullness, early satiety, and a smooth, non-pulsatile, tender epigastric mass 🔥 x12 Painless Obstructive Jaundice + Palpable, Non-Tender Gallbladder (Courvoisier Sign) x3
Diagnostic Characteristics Encapsulated peripancreatic fluid collection with a fibrous non-epithelial wall and exceptionally high amylase content Dual-phase pancreatic CT: Hypodense mass in pancreatic head causing double-duct sign (dilated CBD and pancreatic duct); Elevated Serum CA 19-9 x2
Management Protocols Asymptomatic: Observe.
Symptomatic / Enlarging (>6 cm): Internal Drainage (Endoscopic Cystogastrostomy or Surgical Cystogastrostomy/Cystojejunostomy, Option B, AI Recommended Answer). External percutaneous drainage is avoided because it leads to chronic pancreaticocutaneous fistulae! 🔥 x6		 Resectable: Pancreaticoduodenectomy (Whipple procedure).
Unresectable / Metastatic with pruritus & jaundice: Palliative Endoscopic Biliary Stenting via ERCP 🔥 x11

3. Splenic Trauma & Splenic Abscess Protocols 🔥 x4

Feature Delayed Blunt Splenic Rupture x3 Solitary Splenic Abscess x1
Clinical Presentation Blunt abdominal trauma (e.g., soccer tackle / kick to LUQ) → initial normal vitals (lucid interval) → sudden hemodynamic collapse, hypotension, and left shoulder tip pain (Kehr Sign) hours later Persistent high spiking fever, LUQ tenderness, leukocytosis, and left-sided pleural effusion in a patient with history of infective endocarditis or hematogenous seeding
Diagnostic Findings FAST exam reveals free intra-abdominal fluid; abdominal CT confirms subcapsular hematoma rupture / parenchymal maceration Abdominal Ultrasound / CT confirms a discrete, unilocular hypodense fluid collection with peripheral enhancement in the spleen
Definitive Management Aggressive resuscitation with blood products → Emergency Exploratory Laparotomy and Splenectomy (in unstable patient) Stable patient with solitary accessible abscess: Image-Guided Percutaneous Drainage + Broad-Spectrum IV Antibiotics (Option B, AI Recommended Answer). Note: Model Answer marks Total Splenectomy (Option D).
⚠️ Model Answer vs. AI Recommended Answer

1. تنبيه بخصوص خراج الطحال المنفرد (سؤال Q1050):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: في مريض مستقر ديناميكياً ولديه خراج طحالي منفرد (Solitary accessible splenic abscess)، الخيار الأولي المعتمد للحفاظ على الطحال وتجنب مضاعفات الاستئصال هو Percutaneous image-guided drainage + IV antibiotics (Option B). بينما في نموذج الإجابة القديم (Model Answer) تم وضع Option D (Total splenectomy) وفقاً للجراحة التقليدية القديمة.

2. تنبيه بخصوص الكيس البنكرياسي الكاذب المتكيس (سؤال Q1109):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: الكيس البنكرياسي الكاذب الناضج المتسبب في أعراض هضمية أو كبر الحجم يُعالج نهائياً عبر النزح الداخلي Internal drainage (Endoscopic cystogastrostomy or Laparotomy with internal drainage, Option B) لمنع حدوث الناسور البنكرياسي الجلدي المزمن (Pancreaticocutaneous fistula). بينما في الـ Model Answer في السؤال Q1109 تم اختيار Percutaneous drainage (Option A) بالخطأ.

3. تنبيه بخصوص خيارات سؤال التهاب القنوات الصفراوية الصاعد المتداخلة (سؤال Q887):
السؤال يصف بدقة حالة التهاب القنوات المرارية الصاعد الحاد (Charcot Triad: حرارة ورعشة ويرقان). حدث خطأ مطبعي في بنك الأسئلة الأصلي بنسخ اختيارات سؤال وذمة الساقين بدلاً من خيارات المرارة، والـ Model Answer المسجل في البنك هو Option E (Lymphatic obstruction).
