Topic 5 of 8 • Surgery • General Surgery

5. Appendix, Small Bowel & Colorectal Surgery

🔥 High Yield (45×) 38 Qs

1. Appendicitis, Crohn Ileitis & Appendiceal Carcinoid 🔥 x12

Feature Uncomplicated Acute Appendicitis 🔥 x4 Incidental Crohn Terminal Ileitis 🔥 x6 Appendiceal Tip Carcinoid (≤1 cm) x2
Clinical Presentation Vague periumbilical pain migrating to RLQ (McBurney point) + anorexia + nausea + Rovsing Sign (crossed tenderness in RLQ on pressing LLQ) Young adult with chronic RLQ ache, diarrhea, and weight loss undergoing surgery for presumed appendicitis Asymptomatic, discovered incidentally on final pathology following routine appendectomy
Operative Findings Erythematous, turgid, non-perforated appendix; minimal purulent fluid; WBC mildly elevated (10,000–14,000/mm³) Grossly normal appendix; terminal ileum is edematous, hyperemic with characteristic "creeping fat" wrapping mesenteric border; normal cecal base Solid, well-circumscribed yellow-tan firm nodule <1 cm located at the appendiceal tip
Surgical Protocol Standard Appendectomy (Laparoscopic or Open) x1 Perform Appendectomy & Close Abdomen (do NOT resect the terminal ileum if the cecal base is healthy!). Medical therapy for Crohn disease is started postoperatively. Appendectomy Alone is Curative! No further surgical resection is required. (Right hemicolectomy is indicated only if >2 cm, positive base margins, or mesoappendiceal invasion).

2. Bowel Obstruction, Intussusception & Sigmoid Volvulus 🔥 x29

Feature Adhesive Small-Bowel Obstruction (SBO) 🔥 x13 Pediatric Intussusception 🔥 x4 Sigmoid Volvulus 🔥 x12
Patient Demographics Adult with prior abdominal surgery (e.g., previous appendectomy or gynecologic surgery) Infant / toddler (age 6–36 months), commonly preceded by viral gastroenteritis / adenitis Elderly, debilitated, institutionalized patient, chronic laxative use, or Parkinson disease / neuropsychiatric illness
Cardinal Symptoms & Signs Colicky central abdominal pain, bilious vomiting, obstipation, and High-Pitched Tinkling Bowel Sounds with Visible Peristalsis (Option C, AI Recommended Answer) Intermittent severe screaming/crying spells with knees drawn to chest + palpable sausage-shaped mass + "Red Currant-Jelly" Mucoid Stools Massive, rapid, asymmetric tympanitic abdominal distension, obstipation, and lower abdominal pain 🔥 x9
Diagnostic Workup Abdominal & Pelvic CT with IV Contrast (gold standard to identify transition point, closed-loop obstruction, or strangulation) 🔥 x7 Abdominal Ultrasound: Pathognomonic "Target / Donut Sign" or "Pseudokidney Sign" in transverse/longitudinal views Abdominal X-Ray: Characteristic "Coffee-Bean Sign" / Inverted U-Loop with apex pointing toward RUQ
Management Protocol Uncomplicated: NPO, NG tube suction, IV crystalloid resuscitation.
Strangulated / Peritonitis: Immediate laparotomy & adhesiolysis.		 Non-operative reduction: Air (Pneumatic) or Hydrostatic Saline Enema under fluoroscopic/ultrasound guidance (success >85%). Initial: Emergency Flexible Sigmoidoscopy or Proctosigmoidoscopy with Rectal Tube Decompression x3 → Semi-elective sigmoid resection during same admission to prevent recurrence.

3. Diverticulitis, Peritoneal TB & Colonic Ischemia 🔥 x24

  • Complicated Sigmoid Diverticulitis with Pelvic Abscess (Hinchey II): 🔥 x7
    • Acute Phase: CT-Guided Percutaneous Drainage + IV broad-spectrum antibiotics (prevents emergent colostomy).
    • Interval Phase (6 weeks later): Diagnostic Colonoscopy to exclude occult colonic adenocarcinoma.
    • Definitive Surgical Cure: Elective Sigmoid Colectomy with Primary End-to-End Anastomosis.
    • Hinchey I (Small Pericolic Abscess <3 cm): 🔥 x11 Manage with NPO, IV fluids, and IV broad-spectrum antibiotics alone.
  • Stable Diverticular Lower GI Bleeding: x2
    • Most resolve spontaneously; persistent active bleeding is localized via urgent colonoscopy and treated by Endoscopic Hemoclip Application or Band Ligation.
  • Peritoneal Tuberculosis with Low-SAAG Ascites (<1.1 g/dL): 🔥 x7
    • Young immigrant presenting with low-grade fever, weight loss, abdominal distension, and dense peritoneal adhesions on imaging → definitive gold standard diagnostic test is Diagnostic Laparoscopy with Targeted Peritoneal Biopsies for AFB stain, PCR, and caseating granuloma histology.
  • Asymptomatic Simple Ovarian Cyst in Reproductive Female: 🔥 x8
    • Asymptomatic 4-cm unilocular simple cystic adnexal mass with thin regular walls on ultrasound in a 25-year-old → Expectant Management and Follow-Up Pelvic Ultrasound in 6–8 weeks (most represent benign physiological follicular/corpus luteum cysts that resolve spontaneously).
  • Acute Ischemic Colitis Post-Cardiac Surgery (CABG): 🔥 x7
    • Patient presenting with acute left lower abdominal pain and mild dark bloody diarrhea 24–48 hours after CABG (systemic hypoperfusion) → confirm diagnosis by Flexible Sigmoidoscopy / Colonoscopy (demonstrates pale, edematous, dusky mucosa at the Griffith point / splenic flexure); manage non-gangrenous disease conservatively with IV fluids, bowel rest, and broad-spectrum antibiotics.

4. Colorectal Malignancy Surgery & Synchronous Hepatic Metastasis 🔥 x29

Clinical Scenario Pathology & Anatomical Extent Definitive Surgical Protocol
Gardner Syndrome 🔥 x5 Autosomal dominant *APC* mutation; profuse colonic adenomatous polyposis + skull & mandible osteomas, epidermoid cysts, and abdominal desmoid tumors 100% colorectal cancer risk by age 40 → Prophylactic Restorative Total Proctocolectomy with Ileal Pouch-Anal Anastomosis (IPAA)
Cecal / Right Colon Adenocarcinoma 🔥 x5 75-year-old female presenting with fatigue, profound iron deficiency anemia, and a palpable Right Iliac Fossa mass Standard Oncologic Right Hemicolectomy with high ligation of ileocolic and right colic vascular pedicles
Transverse Colon Adenocarcinoma 🔥 x10 Obstructing mid-transverse colon adenocarcinoma requiring complete regional lymphadenectomy Extended Right Hemicolectomy (Option C, AI Recommended Answer). Complete oncologic lymphadenectomy requires ligating the right colic, middle colic, and right branch of left colic arteries! (Model Answer marks Transverse Colectomy Option E).
Colon Cancer with Solitary Liver Metastasis x3 Primary right colon cancer with a solitary, resectable 4-cm metastatic lesion in the right hepatic lobe (left liver lobe completely disease-free) Synchronous Oncologic Right Hemicolectomy and Right Hepatectomy (Option C) provides long-term curative survival!
Early Low Rectal Tumors x3 • Villous adenoma with Carcinoma in Situ (Tis) x1
• Mobile, well-differentiated Stage I (T1N0) low rectal adenocarcinoma <3 cm, <30% circumference x2		 • Tis: Complete endoscopic or transanal submucosal excision (No further therapy).
• T1N0: Transanal Endoscopic Microsurgery (TEM) / Transanal Full-Thickness Local Excision with clear margins.
  • Colorectal Anastomotic Leak: 🔥 x8
    • Classic timeline: Postoperative Day 5–7 following anterior rectal resection.
    • Patient develops sudden worsening pelvic/abdominal pain, spiking fever (38.8°C), persistent tachycardia (>110 bpm), and leukocytosis (WBC 17,500/mm³).
    • Definitive confirmation: Abdominal/Pelvic CT with Rectal Water-Soluble Contrast → prompt surgical re-exploration, peritoneal washout, and fecal diversion.
⚠️ Model Answer vs. AI Recommended Answer

1. تنبيه بخصوص علامات التهاب الزائدة الدودية البسيطة (سؤال Q1082):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: المريض يعاني من التهاب الزائدة الدودية الحاد البسيط غير المنفجر. العلامة السريرية المميزة الكلاسيكية هي Rovsing sign (Crossed tenderness in RIF on pressing LIF, Option E). بينما في الـ Model Answer تم اختيار Option B (WBC 19,000/mm³)، علماً بأن ارتفاع كرات الدم البيضاء الشديد إلى 19,000 يشير سريرياً إلى حدوث مضاعفات كالانفجار (Perforation) أو الغنغرينا وليس الزائدة البسيطة.

2. تنبيه بخصوص تسمع انسداد الأمعاء الدقيقة الميكانيكي (سؤال Q1060):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: في حالات انسداد الأمعاء الدقيقة الميكانيكي الحاد (Adhesive SBO)، التمعج العنيف ضد الانسداد يولد أصوات أمعاء عالية النبرة رنانة High-Pitched Tinkling Bowel Sounds (Option C). بينما في الـ Model Answer تم وضع Option A (Distention)، علماً بأن الانتفاخ يكون أقل وضوحاً في انسداد الأمعاء الدقيقة العلوي مقارنة بالأصوات الحركية المميزة.

3. تنبيه بخصوص استئصال سرطان القولون المستعرض (سؤال Q1101):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: الاستئصال الجذري للأورام الخبيثة بالقولون المستعرض مع تجريف العقد اللمفاوية بالكامل لأوعية الـ Middle Colic و Right Colic يتطلب إجراء Extended Right Hemicolectomy (Option C). بينما في الـ Model Answer في بنك الأسئلة تم وضع Transverse colectomy (Option E).

4. تنبيه بخصوص استئصال ورم القولون مع ثانوية كبدية وحيدة (سؤال Q1064):
في مريض ورم القولون الأيمن مع بؤرة ثانوية وحيدة قابلة للاستئصال بحجم 4 سم في الفص الأيمن للكبد مع سلامة الفص الأيسر، الإجراء الجراحي الشافي هو Synchronous Right Hemicolectomy and Right Hepatectomy (Option C) (كما تم تأكيده في السؤال المكرر Q1137)، بينما نموذج إجابة Q1064 اختار خطأ إجراء استئصال نصف القولون فقط.

Topic 6 of 8 • Surgery • General Surgery

6. Anorectal & Pilonidal Surgery

🔥 High Yield (24×) 12 Qs

1. Hemorrhoids vs. Anal Fissure Comparison 🔥 x24

Feature Internal Hemorrhoids (Grade I–II) 🔥 x4 Thrombosed External Hemorrhoids x2 Anal Fissure (Acute & Chronic) 🔥 x18
Anatomic Site & Lining Above the Dentate (Pectinate) Line; visceral columnar mucosa (insensate to sharp pain) Below the Dentate Line; somatic squamous anoderm (exquisitely sensitive) Distal anoderm below dentate line; Posterior Midline (90%) due to poor posterior perfusion
Cardinal Clinical Picture PAINLESS, bright red rectal bleeding (coating toilet paper or dripping into bowl) ± spontaneous reducing prolapse Sudden, acute, continuous severe anal pain and a visible, tense, bluish subcutaneous perianal nodule Severe, agonizing "knife-like / tearing" pain during and for hours after defecation + bright red blood on toilet paper 🔥 x11
Physical Examination Non-palpable on DRE unless thrombosed; diagnosed via office Rigid Proctoscopy / Anoscopy x1 Exquisitely tender, firm, bluish-purple clot visible at the anal verge Visible longitudinal linear tear on gentle buttock separation. Triad of chronic fissure: 1) Fissure base, 2) Sentinel skin tag, 3) Hypertrophied anal papilla.
Definitive Management Protocol Conservative First-Line: High-fiber diet (30 g/day), copious water, and stool softeners x3.
Refractory Grade II: Office Rubber Band Ligation (RBL) above dentate line.		 Within 72 hours of onset: Complete Elliptical Excision of the Thrombosed Hemorrhoid under Local Anesthesia.
After 72 hours: Conservative sitz baths and analgesics.		 Medical First-Line: Stool softeners, sitz baths, and Topical 0.2% Glyceryl Trinitrate (GTN) or 2% Diltiazem to reduce resting sphincter tone.
Refractory Chronic: Lateral Internal Sphincterotomy (LIS) 🔥 x7 (gold standard; cures >95%).

2. Perianal Abscess vs. Acute Pilonidal Sinus Abscess 🔥 x10

Clinical Parameter Perianal / Ischiorectal Abscess 🔥 x8 Acute Pilonidal Sinus Abscess x2
Anatomic Location & Origin Anal margin / ischioanal fossa; arises from obstruction and infection of an anal cryptoglandular unit at the dentate line Intergluteal natal cleft; midline skin over the sacrococcyx (4–5 cm superior to anal verge); caused by ingrown hair penetration
Patient Demographics Adult males > females; increased in Crohn disease and diabetes Young, hirsute, sedentary males (age 18–25 years)
Clinical Presentation Constant, throbbing, severe perianal pain aggravated by sitting/straining; fever; tender erythematous induration Tender, warm, erythematous fluctuant mass strictly located in the upper natal cleft over the lower sacrum
Definitive Operative Protocol Emergency Examination Under Anesthesia (EUA) with Wide Incision and Drainage close to the anal verge.
GOLDEN RULE: Never wait for fluctuance to drain a perianal abscess! Antibiotics alone are useless without drainage.		 Incision and Drainage performed strictly through an off-midline lateral incision with curettage of hair nests; elective definitive excision or flap closure scheduled once acute infection resolves.

3. Post-Obstetric Fecal Urge Incontinence x3

  • Clinical Vignette: Multiparous 58-year-old woman presenting with progressive fecal urge incontinence years after sustaining a third-degree obstetric perineal laceration during childbirth.
  • Diagnostic Investigation of Choice: Anorectal Physiology Testing (Endoanal Ultrasound + High-Resolution Anorectal Manometry) to objectively demonstrate external/internal anal sphincter disruption and delineate resting vs. squeeze pressures before scheduling sphincteroplasty. x3

Topic 7 of 8 • Surgery • General Surgery

7. Vascular Surgery

🔥 High Yield (22×) 12 Qs

1. Acute Lower-Limb Ischemia & Reperfusion Compartment Syndrome 🔥 x9

Feature Acute Lower-Limb Ischemia 🔥 x6 Reperfusion Compartment Syndrome x3
Etiology & Pathophysiology Cardiogenic arterial embolism (most commonly secondary to Atrial Fibrillation or post-MI mural thrombus) impacting at the femoral bifurcation Revascularization / embolectomy performed after prolonged, severe ischemia (>6–8 hours) → microvascular hyperpermeability and severe capillary leak into tight fascial compartments
Cardinal Clinical Signs The Classic 6 Ps: 🔥 x5
1. Pain (severe, sudden, distal)
2. Pallor (pale, marble-like, cold skin)
3. Pulselessness (absent distal dorsalis pedis/posterior tibial pulses)
4. Poikilothermia (cold limb equilibrating with room temperature)
5. Paresthesia (sensory loss; indicates threatened nerve tissue)
6. Paralysis (inability to move toes; indicates advanced irreversible muscle necrosis)		 Tense, "wooden" firmness of the calf muscles, disproportionate agonizing pain, and Severe Pain Elicited by Passive Stretching of the Toes / Ankle
Immediate Action Immediate IV Unfractionated Heparin Bolus (prevents distal thrombus propagation) x1 → Emergent surgical balloon catheter embolectomy (Fogarty) or catheter-directed thrombolysis. Surgical emergency! Intracompartmental pressure >30 mmHg (or ΔP <30 mmHg) → Emergency Four-Compartment Lower-Leg Fasciotomy (two-incision technique) to prevent ischemic myonecrosis and amputation!

2. Chronic Lower-Extremity Ulcers: Arterial vs. Venous Stasis 🔥 x18

Feature Arterial (Ischemic) Ulcer 🔥 x14 Chronic Venous Stasis Ulcer 🔥 x4
Typical Anatomical Site Distal extremities: Tips of toes, great toe, interdigital clefts, or over bony pressure points (dorsum of foot, heel) Medial Malleolus (Gaiter Area) of the lower leg (above the medial malleolus)
Ulcer Characteristics Deep, sharply demarcated, "punched-out" edges; pale, dry, gray or necrotic black base with little to no granulation tissue Shallow, large, irregular margins; moist base with copious exudate and florid pink/red granulation tissue
Pain Characteristics Severe, excruciating pain worse at night / elevation (patient hangs leg over bed for gravity-assisted perfusion); accompanied by intermittent claudication Mild, dull, aching discomfort; sensation of heaviness worse at the end of the day and relieved by leg elevation
Surrounding Skin & Pulses Cold, shiny, atrophic skin, hair loss, thickened brittle nails, dependent rubor / pallor on elevation; Absent or severely diminished pedal pulses (ABPI <0.8) Warm, indurated skin with pitting edema, stasis dermatitis, and brownish Hemosiderin Pigmentation; normal peripheral arterial pulses
Definitive Management Arterial Duplex / Angiography → Revascularization (Endovascular Angioplasty/Stenting or Surgical Bypass). Compression therapy is strictly contraindicated! Venous Duplex Ultrasound → Graduated Multi-Layer Compression Bandaging / Stockings + leg elevation + treatment of underlying superficial venous reflux.

3. Saphena Varix, Superficial Thrombophlebitis & Postoperative DVT 🔥 x14

Feature Saphena Varix 🔥 x10 Superficial Thrombophlebitis x2 Postoperative DVT x2
Clinical Scenario & Exam Soft, compressible, bluish swelling in the groin located inferior and lateral to the pubic tubercle at the saphenofemoral junction; completely disappears when patient lies supine; transmits an expansile impulse and audible thrill on coughing Acutely tender, warm, indurated, erythematous palpable linear "cord" along the course of a superficial lower-limb varicose vein Unilateral calf pain, tender swelling, warmth, and calf circumference discrepancy >2 cm developing days after major abdominal / pelvic surgery
Diagnostic Confirmation Venous Duplex Ultrasound confirms aneurysmal dilation of the proximal great saphenous vein with turbulent reflux Clinical diagnosis; perform duplex ultrasound to ensure thrombus does not extend to within 3 cm of the saphenofemoral junction Compression Venous Duplex Ultrasound demonstrating non-compressibility of deep veins and absence of flow
Management Protocol Elective High Saphenofemoral Ligation (Trendelenburg) and Stripping / Endovenous Thermal Ablation Oral NSAIDs, warm compresses, leg elevation, and continued normal ambulation Immediate therapeutic anticoagulation (LMWH bridge to Warfarin or Direct Oral Anticoagulants / DOACs) for 3–6 months
↑ Back to Top / جدول المحتويات
Topic 8 of 8 • Surgery • General Surgery

8. Neck, Soft-Tissue, Trauma & Surgical Safety

🔥 High Yield (36×) 24 Qs

1. Benign Neck & Subcutaneous Masses 🔥 x8

Feature Branchial Cleft Cyst x3 Subcutaneous Lipoma x3 Inguinal Lymphadenopathy x2
Typical Anatomical Site Lateral neck along the upper-to-middle anterior border of the Sternocleidomastoid (SCM) muscle Subcutaneous tissues of the posterior neck, shoulder, trunk, or upper back Groin crease below the inguinal ligament
Physical Exam Characteristics Smooth, painless, fluctuant, non-transilluminating cyst in a young adult (age 20–30 years); does NOT move with swallowing or tongue protrusion Slow-growing, soft, mobile, lobulated subcutaneous mass with characteristic "Slip Sign" (edge slips from examining fingers) Firm, discrete, non-tender, completely irreducible solid nodule without cough impulse or expansile thrill
Definitive Surgical Protocol Complete surgical excision including the tract leading to the pharynx (second branchial cleft remnant) Elective surgical enucleation / excision under local anesthesia if cosmetically bothersome or symptomatic Excisional lymph node biopsy if persistent (>4 weeks) or systemic features present
  • Deep Cheek Knife Laceration (Tragus to Upper Lip): x3
    • A penetrating cheek laceration along a line connecting the tragus of the ear to the midpoint of the upper lip divides the Parotid (Stensen) Duct and the buccal branch of the Facial Nerve (CN VII) → mandates primary ductal repair over a stent and microsurgical nerve re-approximation.

2. Soft-Tissue Infections, Animal/Human Bites, Melanoma & Line Sepsis 🔥 x15

  • Fournier Gangrene (Perineal Necrotizing Fasciitis): 🔥 x5
    • Clinical Vignette: Uncontrolled diabetic presenting 36–48 hours after perianal procedure/trauma with excruciating scrotal and perineal pain, high fever, septic shock, black necrotic skin patches, foul "dishwater" discharge, and palpable subcutaneous crepitus.
    • Definitive Management: Surgical emergency! Immediate aggressive crystalloid resuscitation, triple broad-spectrum IV antibiotics (Vancomycin + Piperacillin-Tazobactam + Clindamycin) → Emergent Radical Surgical Debridement of all necrotic fascia and skin.
  • Human Bite Wound Management (Forearm Laceration): x2
    • Human bites carry virulent oral polymicrobial pathogens (Eikenella corrodens, *Staphylococcus*, *Streptococcus*, and anaerobes).
    • Protocol: Copious Normal Saline Wound Irrigation, Tetanus Prophylaxis, and Oral Amoxicillin-Clavulanate.
      GOLDEN RULE: NEVER suture a human bite primarily! Leave the wound open to heal by Secondary Intention (or delayed primary closure) to avoid catastrophic closed compartment gas-forming infections.
  • Wound Healing by Secondary Intention: x1
    • An infected abscess cavity or debrided contaminated wound packed with gauze and left open heals via granulation tissue deposition, myofibroblastic wound contraction, and epithelialization: Healing by Secondary Intention.
  • Central Venous Catheter (TPN) Line Sepsis: x1
    • A hospitalized patient receiving Total Parenteral Nutrition (TPN) via a central venous line who develops persistent fever, rigors, and leukocytosis without localized infection → Promptly Remove the Central Catheter, Send Catheter Tip & Blood for Culture, and Place a New Line at a Different Anatomic Site.
  • Cutaneous Malignant Melanoma Prognostic Determinant: 🔥 x6
    • The single most critical histological prognostic indicator for localized cutaneous malignant melanoma is the Breslow Depth / Thickness of the Tumor (vertical measurement in millimeters from the granular layer of epidermis to the deepest tumor cell).

3. Trauma Surgical Priorities & WHO Surgical "Time-Out" Checklist 🔥 x17

  • Hemodynamically Unstable Blunt Abdominal Trauma (FAST Positive): 🔥 x5
    • Blunt abdominal injury patient with persistent hypotension (BP 70/30 mmHg after 2 L IV crystalloid / blood products) and positive FAST showing free intraperitoneal fluid → BYPASS CT SCAN! Transfer immediately to the OR for Emergency Exploratory Laparotomy.
  • Penetrating Thigh Gunshot with Hard Vascular Signs: x3
    • Gunshot wound to midthigh with Hard Signs of Arterial Injury (absent distal pulses, active pulsatile bleeding, rapidly expanding hematoma, or palpable thrill/bruit) → take directly to the Operating Room for Surgical Exploration and Vascular Repair without preoperative CTA delay!
  • Traumatic Diaphragmatic Rupture (Left Hemithorax): 🔥 x5
    • Blunt deceleration trauma patient with dyspnea and upright chest X-ray demonstrating the Nasogastric (NG) tube coiled inside the left chest cavity → Emergency Laparotomy (transabdominal repair with non-absorbable sutures).
  • Penetrating Neck Trauma with Hard Signs: x2
    • Penetrating neck gunshot wound with expanding hematoma, stridor, or active arterial bleeding → Proceed Directly to Surgical Exploration and Neck Exploration.
  • Stable Abdominal Gunshot with Grade III Liver Injury: 🔥 x4
    • Hemodynamically stable patient without generalized peritonitis with Grade III liver laceration on abdominal CT → Non-Operative Conservative Management with ICU Hemodynamic Monitoring and serial examinations.
  • Delayed Deep Glass Laceration (>10–12 Hours Old): 🔥 x7
    • Deep contaminated forearm laceration presenting >10 hours after injury → Wound Debridement, Copious Irrigation, and Open Local Wound Care Without Primary Closure.
  • Chronic Paronychia (>6 Weeks Duration): 🔥 x10
    • Chronic erythema, tenderness, and rounding/loss of the nail fold cuticle without discrete pus in a dishwasher or wet-worker → Topical Corticosteroid Creams (chronic irritant dermatitis ± secondary *Candida* colonization).
  • WHO Surgical Safety Checklist — The "Time-Out": 🔥 x9
    • Mandatory safety pause conducted inside the operating room immediately BEFORE skin incision involving the entire surgical team (surgeon, anesthesiologist, and nurses) to aloud verify:
      1. Correct Patient Identity
      2. Correct Surgical Site and Laterality Marking
      3. Correct Procedure to be Performed
      4. Administration of Prophylactic Antibiotics within the preceding 60 minutes
      5. Anticipation of Critical Events & Blood Loss.
⚠️ Model Answer vs. AI Recommended Answer

تنبيه هام للامتحان بخصوص إصابة المرارة في الطعنات النافذة (سؤال Q894):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: مريض تعرض لطعن نافذ بالربع العلوي الأيمن للبطن (RUQ stab wound) ووُجد أثناء الاستكشاف الجراحي تهتك بالمرارة (Gallbladder laceration). الإجراء الطبي الجراحي المعتمد قطيعاً هو استئصال المرارة The gallbladder injury can be treated with cholecystectomy (Option A).

في الـ Model Answer في بنك الأسئلة: تم تسجيل Option B (Isolated gallbladder injuries are uncommon) كإجابة في نموذج التصحيح (كلاهما عبارات صحيحة علمياً حيث أن إصابات المرارة المنعزلة نادرة أيضاً، والسؤال الأصلي كان يسأل عن العبارة الخاطئة)، لذا يجب الانتباه للإجابتين في الامتحان.

Comprehensive High-Yield VIP Reference • Fully Aligned with EMLE Standards

Topic 1 of 4 • Surgery • Orthopedics & Trauma

1. Upper Limb Injuries

🔥 High Yield (20×) 6 Qs

1. Upper Limb Dislocations & Wrist/Carpal Fractures 🔥 x8

Feature Anterior Shoulder Dislocation 🔥 x4 Colles Fracture (Distal Radius) x3 Scaphoid Fracture x1
Mechanism of Injury Fall onto an abducted, externally rotated, and extended arm Fall onto an outstretched hand (FOOSH) with wrist in dorsiflexion (classic in osteoporotic elderly) Fall onto an outstretched hand (FOOSH) with axial load on radially deviated wrist in young adult
Anatomic Site & Displacement Humeral head dislocates anteriorly and inferiorly (>95% of all shoulder dislocations) Extra-articular fracture within 2 cm of distal articular surface with dorsal displacement and dorsal tilt ("Dinner-Fork Deformity") Transverse fracture through the scaphoid waist (70%) or proximal pole
Pathognomonic Physical Sign Loss of normal rounded deltoid contour ("Squared-Off Shoulder"), palpable humeral head anteriorly, patient holds arm in slight abduction Prominent dorsal bump at wrist, volar prominence of distal radius shaft, radial deviation of hand Exquisite point tenderness in the Anatomical Snuffbox (between EPL and EPB/APL tendons) and with axial thumb loading
Initial Diagnostic Radiographs AP and Scapular-Y (or axillary) views: Humeral head displaced anterior to the glenoid fossa ± Hill-Sachs lesion / Bankart lesion PA and lateral wrist X-rays: Distal radial shortening, dorsal comminution, and loss of normal 11° volar tilt PA, lateral, and dedicated Scaphoid views (wrist in ulnar deviation). GOLDEN RULE: Initial X-rays are frequently negative/normal on Day 1!
Associated Nerve Injury / Major Risk Axillary Nerve (check sensation over the "regimental badge" patch of lateral deltoid prior to reduction) Median Nerve compression (acute carpal tunnel syndrome: paresthesias in radial 3.5 digits) Retrograde blood supply from distal radial artery → high risk of Avascular Necrosis (AVN) of proximal pole and fibrous nonunion!
Management Protocol Urgent closed reduction under sedation (Kocher, Hennepin, or Stimson technique) → post-reduction X-ray → sling immobilization for 2–3 weeks Closed reduction under hematoma block → immobilization in below-elbow plaster cast.
Unstable/comminuted: Volar Locking Plate ORIF.		 Tenderness in snuffbox with normal initial X-ray → Immobilize in a Thumb Spica Cast/Splint and Repeat X-Rays in 10–14 Days (or immediate non-contrast MRI).

2. Post-Dislocation Elbow Myositis Ossificans x3

  • Etiology & Precipitating Factors:
    • Follows traumatic elbow dislocation or supracondylar fracture (e.g., 3–4 weeks prior).
    • Iatrogenic Trigger: Premature, overly aggressive passive stretching, forceful manipulation, or deep massage of the stiff elbow joint during early rehabilitation.
  • Clinical Hallmarks: x3
    • Initial post-reduction recovery is followed by a sudden secondary onset of severe local pain, soft tissue swelling, warmth, and progressive loss of elbow range of motion (flexion and extension block).
  • Pathognomonic Imaging:
    • Elbow radiographs show flocculent, "cloud-like" soft tissue calcification and heterotopic woven bone formation anterior to the distal humerus and brachialis muscle: Myositis Ossificans Traumatica.
    • Management: Complete rest, immobilization in functional splint, indomethacin/NSAIDs, and avoidance of all passive stretching; surgical excision only after mature bone formation (>12 months).

3. Hand Flexor Tendon Anatomy: FDS vs. FDP x3

Anatomic Parameter Flexor Digitorum Superficialis (FDS) x3 Flexor Digitorum Profundus (FDP)
Proximal Origin Arises as a single, common broad muscle belly in intermediate anterior forearm, splitting into 4 separate tendons Arises deeply from proximal ulna and interosseous membrane (separate slip to index, common belly to middle/ring/little)
Tendon Decussation Splits into two slips at the level of the proximal phalanx (Camper Chiasm) to allow the FDP tendon to pass through Passes straight through the bifurcation of the FDS tendon toward the distal digit
Distal Insertion Bilateral palmar margins of the Middle Phalanx of the medial four fingers Palmar base of the Distal Phalanx of the medial four fingers
Specific Isolated Action Primary flexor of the Proximal Interphalangeal (PIP) Joint (tested by holding other fingers extended and flexing target PIP) Sole flexor of the Distal Interphalangeal (DIP) Joint (tested by holding PIP extended and flexing DIP joint alone)

Topic 2 of 4 • Surgery • Orthopedics & Trauma

2. Lower Limb Fractures & Neurovascular Injuries

🔥 High Yield (28×) 9 Qs

1. Open Long-Bone Fractures (Femur & Tibia Protocols) 🔥 x17

  • Immediate Emergency Medical Priority in the ED: x1
    • Immediate Administration of IV Broad-Spectrum Antibiotics (e.g., Cefazolin 2 g IV ± Gentamicin for Gustilo-Anderson Grade II/III). Early systemic antibiotic administration within 3 hours is the single most effective intervention to prevent deep surgical site infection and chronic osteomyelitis!
    • Apply sterile, saline-soaked dressing, stabilize in a splint/traction, check and document neurovascular status, and administer tetanus toxoid prophylaxis.
  • Definitive Surgical Management of Open Femur / Tibia Fractures: 🔥 x16
    1. Radical Debridement & Pulsatile Lavage: Emergent transfer to OR for thorough excision of all devitalized tissue, foreign contamination, and bone fragments lacking soft-tissue attachment.
    2. Antimicrobial Therapy: Continuation of weight-based IV antibiotics for 48–72 hours.
    3. Skeletal Stabilization: Rigid Internal (Intramedullary Nail) or External Fixation (depending on soft-tissue contamination grade). Open long-bone fractures are NEVER treated with a plaster cast alone!

2. Closed Tibial Shaft Fracture Management Comparison 🔥 x9

Feature Closed Reduction & Long-Leg Cast x2 Reamed Intramedullary (IM) Nailing x3 Open Plating (ORIF) in Adolescents 🔥 x4
Primary Clinical Indication Pediatric fractures and minimally displaced, stable low-energy spiral/oblique fractures with <1 cm shortening and <5° angulation Displaced, unstable adult tibial shaft fractures (transverse, comminuted, or high-energy motor vehicle / pedestrian impacts) Unstable fractures requiring rigid anatomic reduction when intramedullary nailing is contraindicated
Biological Preservation Completely preserves fracture hematoma, periosteal vascular sleeve, and surrounding soft tissues Load-sharing biomechanical splint inserted without exposing fracture site; preserves periosteal blood supply Extensive soft-tissue stripping, periosteal stripping, and evacuation of the osteogenic fracture hematoma
Clinical Pros & Cons Non-operative; avoids surgical infection, but carries risks of cast disease, joint stiffness, and malunion Gold Standard for adult tibial fractures: High union rates (>98%), early full weight-bearing, low infection rate Produces a significantly higher incidence of delayed union, nonunion, and implant failure in adolescents! 🔥 x4

3. Ankle Dislocation & Lower-Limb Neurovascular Trauma 🔥 x19

  • Ankle Dislocation — Mandatory Immediate Bedside Action: x1
    • Severe gross deformity of the ankle with skin tenting → perform immediate bedside evaluation of Skin Color, Temperature, Distal Arterial Pulsations (Dorsalis Pedis & Posterior Tibial), and Neurologic Function in the Toes before and immediately after emergent closed reduction!
  • Post-Cast Common Peroneal (Fibular) Nerve Compression: 🔥 x9
    • Anatomical Vulnerability: The common peroneal nerve winds superficial to the Neck of the Fibula beneath the skin and deep fascia.
    • Clinical Hallmark: Tight below-knee plaster cast or external leg splint compressing the fibular neck → Complete Foot Drop (loss of ankle and toe dorsiflexion) + loss of foot eversion + sensory numbness over anterolateral lower leg and dorsum of foot.
    • Immediate Action: Bivalve the cast and release all underlying padding immediately!
  • Arterial Trauma in Femoral / Knee Fractures (Hard Signs vs. Palpable Pulses): 🔥 x9
    • Hard Sign of Vascular Injury: High-velocity gunshot femoral fracture or knee dislocation with persistent pulselessness despite fracture reduction → Immediate Surgical Exploration and Vascular Repair. x1
    • CRITICAL EXAM AXIOM: Palpable distal pedal pulses do NOT reliably exclude significant arterial injury! An intimal tear, subcritical flow, or extensive collateral circulation can transmit a palpable pulse despite a transected or thrombosis-prone major artery! 🔥 x8
⚠️ Model Answer vs. AI Recommended Answer

1. تنبيه بخصوص كسر الفخذ المفتوح ومتلازمة الحجرات (سؤال Q964):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: البروتوكول المعتمد لكسر الفخذ المفتوح بدون علامات سريرية لمتلازمة الحجرات هو Early irrigation, debridement, IV antibiotics, and skeletal fixation (Option E). ولا يتم عمل شق حجرات وقائي روتيني إلا إذا وجدت أعراض متلازمة الحجرات (Compartment syndrome). بينما في الـ Model Answer في بنك الأسئلة تم اختيار Option B (والذي يضيف إجراء شق الحجرات روتينياً Compartment decompression).

2. تنبيه بخصوص تناقض نماذج إجابات كسور قصبة الساق (سؤال Q973 وسؤال Q987):

في بنك الأسئلة يوجد تناقض شهير بين سؤالين: في Q973 (كسر حلزوني طفيف في شاب إثر مشاجرة)، الخيار الطبي التحفظي المعتمد هو الجبس Closed manipulation and cast (Option C) بينما اختار نموذج الإجابة Intramedullary nail (Option B). وعلى النقيض تماماً في Q987 (مريض صدمته سيارة بكسر متبدل في قصبة الساق)، المعيار الذهبي المعتمد طبياً للبالغين هو المسمار النخاعي Intramedullary nailing (Option C) بينما اختار نموذج الإجابة Long leg cast (Option A)! يجب الانتباه لهذه التناقضات عند الحل.

↑ Back to Top / جدول المحتويات
Topic 3 of 4 • Surgery • Orthopedics & Trauma

3. Hip, Knee & Spine Disorders

🔥 High Yield (24×) 7 Qs

1. Pediatric Hip Disorders: Legg-Calvé-Perthes vs. Steroid-Induced AVN 🔥 x4

Feature Legg-Calvé-Perthes (LCP) Disease x3 Corticosteroid-Induced Femoral Head AVN x1
Patient Demographics Young boys aged 4–8 years (peak incidence at 6 years); male:female 5:1; idiopathic etiology Adolescent or adult with history of high-dose systemic corticosteroid therapy (e.g., severe asthma exacerbation)
Clinical Presentation Insidious onset of a painless or mildly aching limp worsening at the end of the day; completely afebrile; restricted hip internal rotation and abduction Progressive, dull anterior groin, thigh, or referred knee ache accompanied by an antalgic limp; normal vitals (afebrile)
Laboratory Markers Completely normal ESR, CRP, and WBC count (differentiates strictly from septic arthritis or osteomyelitis) Normal inflammatory markers (normal ESR, CRP, WBC)
Diagnostic Imaging Pelvic Radiographs (AP & Frog-Leg Lateral): Sclerosis, flattening, fragmentation, and collapse of the capital femoral epiphysis with crescent sign Pelvic & Hip MRI (Gold Standard): Detects early osteonecrosis (band-like low signal on T1) long before plain radiographs become abnormal

2. Knee Disorders: Meniscal Tears vs. Primary Knee Osteoarthritis 🔥 x18

Feature Medial Meniscal Tear 🔥 x14 Primary Knee Osteoarthritis (OA) 🔥 x4
Etiology & Patient Young active individual / athlete following a twisting injury on a semi-flexed, weight-bearing knee Older adult (age >55 years); strongly correlated with obesity (BMI ≥35–40 kg/m²) and repetitive wear-and-tear
Mechanism of Injury Combined Knee Flexion and Axial Rotation x3.
The Medial Meniscus is torn 3–5× more frequently than the lateral meniscus because it is firmly anchored to the deep MCL and joint capsule!		 Progressive loss of articular hyaline cartilage, subchondral bone sclerosis, and osteophyte formation
Cardinal Clinical Symptoms Joint-line clicking, delayed effusion (develops over 24h), and True Mechanical Knee Locking (inability to fully extend the knee passively) 🔥 x11 Chronic, insidious bilateral knee pain worse with weight-bearing/walking and relieved by rest; morning stiffness lasting <30 minutes
Physical Exam Signs Well-localized tenderness along the medial joint line; Positive McMurray Test and Apley grind test Bony enlargement, loss of joint range of motion, and palpable Coarse Joint Crepitus ("Grating Sensation") during active flexion
First-Line Management Diagnostic confirmation with Knee MRI → Arthroscopic partial meniscectomy or meniscal repair (if tear is in vascular red zone) Weight reduction, low-impact exercise, and first-line pharmacotherapy with Acetaminophen (Paracetamol) or Oral NSAIDs (Ibuprofen) x3

3. Osteoporotic Vertebral Compression Fractures 🔥 x4

  • Clinical Vignette: Frail elderly female (age 82 years) with severe osteoporosis presenting after a minor slip and fall with acute, localized, persistent midline lower back pain, kyphotic posture deformity, and point spinous tenderness over L1–L2.
  • First-Line Diagnostic Action: 🔥 x4
    • Order Plain Thoracolumbar Spine X-Rays (AP and Lateral Views) to confirm the presence of Vertebral Body Compression Fractures (wedge-shaped anterior height loss) before initiating physical therapy or adjusting antiresorptive medications.
↑ Back to Top / جدول المحتويات
Topic 4 of 4 • Surgery • Orthopedics & Trauma

4. ATLS, Spine, Chest, Pelvic & Visceral Trauma

🔥 High Yield (38×) 21 Qs

1. ATLS Primary Survey, Polytrauma Imaging & Resuscitation 🔥 x24

  • First Uncompromising Priority in Polytrauma (ATLS "A"): x3
    • Unconscious or irregularly breathing high-speed RTA victim with obvious open limb fractures → Establish Definitive Airway with In-Line Manual Cervical Spine Immobilization. Never get distracted by deformities or bleeding limbs until airway and cervical spine are secured!
  • Primary Survey Trauma Radiographic Adjuncts: 🔥 x10
    • Hypotensive Pedestrian (BP 85/55 mmHg) with Severe Open Tibia Fracture: Obtain portable Chest and Pelvic X-Rays First (along with eFAST) to identify or exclude occult, life-threatening cavitary internal hemorrhage before taking limb X-rays! 🔥 x7
    • High-Energy Bus Collision with Pulseless Deformed Arm & Paraplegia: Order Right-Arm X-Ray, Chest X-Ray, Pelvis X-Ray, and Urgent Spinal Cord Imaging (CT Spine). x3
  • Immediate Long Spine Board Removal: x1
    • Remove the Long Spine Board Using the 4-Person Log-Roll Technique at the End of the Primary Survey inside the ED resuscitation bay. Leaving patients on rigid boards >30 minutes causes severe sacral/occipital pressure ulcers and respiratory restriction!
  • Cervical Cord Injury Levels & Blunt Cerebrovascular Injury (BCVI): 🔥 x4
    • Complete C6 Spinal Cord Quadriplegia: Preserves C5/C6 root myotomes (biceps elbow flexion and wrist extension remain intact), but causes Complete Loss of C7 Function (Paralysis of Triceps / Elbow Extension) and all distal motor/sensory function below C6. x1
    • High-Risk Fractures Mandating CT Angiography (CTA) for BCVI: Screen with CTA of the neck in patients with C1–C3 fractures, cervical fractures traversing the transverse foramen, basilar skull fractures, or Le Fort II/III midface fractures to rule out internal carotid/vertebral artery dissection. x3

2. Unstable Pelvic Ring Fractures with Hemorrhagic Shock 🔥 x8

Clinical Parameter Immediate Bedside Stabilization 🔥 x7 Operative Orthopedic Fixation x1
Clinical Scenario & Vitals High-energy polytrauma victim (fall from height / MVC) presenting in profound hemorrhagic shock with an "Open-Book" pelvic fracture on portable pelvic X-ray Severe pelvic disruption; hemodynamically unstable despite blood resuscitation (BP 86/46 mmHg, HR 136 bpm) with a Completely Negative FAST Exam
Anatomic Bleeding Source Disruption of the posterior sacroiliac ligaments and shearing of the Retroperitoneal Pelvic Venous Plexus (85%) and cancellous pelvic fracture surfaces Expanding retroperitoneal pelvic hematoma (negative FAST rules out major intraperitoneal hemoperitoneum)
Next Best Step Immediate Application of a Commercial Pelvic Binder (or Circumferential Bedsheet Wrap) Centered Over the Greater Trochanters to close pelvic volume and tamponade venous hemorrhage! Emergency Pelvic External Fixation (or C-Clamp) ± Preperitoneal Pelvic Packing.
GOLDEN RULE: Laparotomy is strictly contraindicated with a negative FAST! It opens the retroperitoneum and destroys the pelvic tamponade!

3. Thoracic, Pericardial & Abdominal Trauma Protocols 🔥 x10

Feature Isolated Rib Fracture in Elderly x3 Precordial Stab Wound with Clear CXR 🔥 x5 Abdominal / Renal Trauma x2
Clinical Presentation 75-year-old slipping and falling against kitchen counter; focal severe right chest wall tenderness over 7th rib; clear lung fields on CXR Penetrating stab wound to anterior left chest / precordium; profound hypotension (SBP 85 mmHg), neck vein distention, and normal clear CXR • Blunt trauma with persistent shock (BP 70/40) + positive FAST.
• Stable trauma patient with right flank ecchymosis and gross hematuria.
Life-Threatening Risk Severe splinting due to pain → hypoventilation, basal atelectasis, retention of secretions, and lethal secondary pneumonia Acute Cardiac Tamponade (Beck Triad: hypotension, muffled heart tones, JVD). CXR is completely normal because acute tamponade requires only 150–200 mL of pericardial blood! • Unstable: Massive hemoperitoneum (splenic/liver rupture).
• Stable: Renal laceration / vascular injury.
Next Best Action Multimodal Systemic Analgesics (Option C, AI Recommended Answer) ± Intercostal Nerve Block (Option A, Model Answer) to enable deep breathing and coughing.
NEVER BIND OR STRAP THE CHEST!		 Immediate Bedside FAST Examination (Subxiphoid Pericardial Window) → confirms pericardial fluid → emergent transfer to OR for median sternotomy. • Unstable: Immediate Emergency Exploratory Laparotomy.
• Stable with Gross Hematuria: Abdominal/Pelvic CT with IV Contrast and Delayed Nephrogram Phase.

4. Trauma ICU Complications: Abdominal Compartment Syndrome & Enteral Feeding 🔥 x6

  • Abdominal Compartment Syndrome (ACS): x3
    • Trigger: Postoperative Day 2–3 following damage-control laparotomy, massive fluid resuscitation (>10 L), and visceral edema.
    • Pathophysiology: Sustained Intra-Abdominal Pressure (IAP) >20 mmHg (measured via bladder catheter) accompanied by new organ dysfunction.
    • Clinical Manifestations:
      1. Abdomen: Tensely distended, rock-hard abdominal wall.
      2. Renal: Severe Oliguria or Anuria refractory to fluid boluses (renal vein and parenchyma compression).
      3. Pulmonary: Type 2 Hypercapnic Respiratory Failure with sky-rocketing peak airway pressures (diaphragmatic elevation).
      4. Cardiovascular: Progressive hypotension and refractory tachycardia (diminished inferior vena cava venous return).
    • Definitive Surgical Management: Immediate Decompressive Laparotomy and Temporary Abdominal Closure (Bogota Bag / Vacuum-Assisted Wound Closure).
  • Enteral Nutrition in Severe Head & Chest Trauma (GCS 5 + Aspiration Pneumonitis): x3
    • Comatose, mechanically ventilated trauma patient with severe delayed gastric emptying → Postpyloric (Nasojejunal) Tube Feeding is the route of choice to provide early enteral nutrition while preventing recurrent pulmonary aspiration.
⚠️ Model Answer vs. AI Recommended Answer

1. تنبيه بخصوص السوائل الإنعاشية الأولية في الصدمة النزفية (سؤال Q988):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: بروتوكولات الإنعاش الأولية لطب الطوارئ و ATLS تبدأ فورياً بإعطاء محاليل بلورية سريعة دافئة Rapid crystalloid infusion with Normal Saline 0.9% (Option E) ريثما يتم تجهيز ومطابقة أكياس الدم. بينما في الـ Model Answer في بنك الأسئلة تم وضع Option C (Packed red cells) كإجابة معتمدة للتأكيد على أهمية نقل الدم المبكر في الصدمة النزفية الشديدة.

2. تنبيه بخصوص تسكين آلام كسور الضلوع لدى كبار السن (سؤال Q993):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: الخطوة الأولى في تسكين ألم كسر الضلع المعزول تبدأ بـ Systemic Analgesics (Option C) للحفاظ على التنفس العميق ومنع الـ Atelectasis. وفي الـ Model Answer تم اختيار Option A (Intercostal nerve block) كإجراء تفضيلي لدى المسنين (عمر 75 عاماً) لتجنب تثبيط التنفس الناتج عن المسكنات الأفيونية الجهازية.

Topic 1 of 12 • Pediatrics • Pediatrics

1. Neonatal Resuscitation, Birth Injuries & Perinatal Care

🔥 High Yield (30×) 21 Qs

1. Neonatal Resuscitation Program (NRP) Algorithm & Apgar Score 🔥 x22

  • Best Indicator of Successful Neonatal Resuscitation: 🔥 x5
    • Primary Marker: Rising / Improved Heart Rate = #1 marker of effective PPV/resuscitation.
    • Leading Cause of Under-5 & Neonatal Mortality Globally: 🔥 x4 Prematurity (preterm birth complications).
  • Stepwise NRP Heart Rate Thresholds & Compression Ratio:
    • Initial Stabilization (All Newborns): Warm, dry, stimulate, sniffing position, suction mouth then nose.
    • HR <100 bpm, Apnea, or Gasping: x3 Start Positive Pressure Ventilation (PPV) immediately at 40–60 breaths/min.
    • HR <60 bpm Despite 30 Seconds of Effective PPV: 🔥 x12 Chest compressions + PPV at 3:1 ratio (90:30/min).
    • Exam Excel-Format Alert: In the raw EMLE spreadsheet, 3:1 = 0.12569444444444444 — select 3:1 (0.12569444444444444)!
  • Apgar Score Assessment (1 & 5 Minutes): x2
    • Appearance & Pulse: Pink all over = 2, acrocyanosis = 1, blue/pale = 0
    • Heart rate >100 bpm = 2, <100 bpm = 1, absent = 0.
    • Grimace, Activity & Respiration: Cry/cough = 2, grimace = 1, none = 0
    • Active flexion = 2, some flexion = 1, flaccid = 0
    • Strong cry = 2, weak = 1, absent = 0.
    • Vignette Calculation:
      • HR 70 bpm (1 pt)
      • weak grimace (1 pt)
      • flaccid (0)
      • absent respirations (0)
      • pale/cyanotic (0) = Apgar Score 2.

2. Extracranial Birth Injuries: Caput vs. Cephalohematoma vs. Subgaleal Hematoma x3

Feature Caput Succedaneum Cephalohematoma Subgaleal Hematoma x2
Anatomic Plane SC edema above periosteum Subperiosteal hemorrhage beneath periosteum Between periosteum and galea aponeurotica
Relation to Suture Lines Crosses suture lines Does NOT cross suture lines Crosses suture lines diffusely
Onset & Exam Clues Soft, pitting scalp swelling present at birth Firm parietal swelling appearing h after birth Fluctuant boggy mass pushing ears forward after vacuum/obstructed labor
Key Complication None (benign edema) Indirect hyperbilirubinemia (neonatal jaundice) Life-threatening hypovolemic shock & exsanguination
1st-Line Tx Resolves spontaneously in 2–3 days Observation; resolves in 2–6 w Urgent IV fluid/blood resuscitation & serial hematocrit monitoring

3. Brachial Plexus Birth Injuries: Erb-Duchenne vs. Klumpke Palsy 🔥 x5

Feature Erb-Duchenne Palsy 🔥 x5 Klumpke Palsy
Injured Nerve Roots Upper trunk (C5–C6 ± C7) Lower trunk (C8–T1)
Delivery Risk Factor Maternal diabetes, macrosomia, shoulder dystocia, or breech traction Upward hyperabduction traction on the arm during delivery
Classic Limb Posture “Waiter’s tip”: adducted, internally rotated arm, extended elbow, pronated forearm “Claw hand” (intrinsic hand muscle paralysis) ± ipsilateral Horner syndrome (T1)
Moro & Biceps Reflexes Absent on affected side Intact
Palmar Grasp Reflex Intact Absent
1st-Line Tx Gentle passive range-of-motion physiotherapy Gentle passive range-of-motion physiotherapy

4. Congenital Muscular Torticollis, Perinatal Prophylaxis & Trauma Airway 🔥 x16

  • Congenital Muscular Torticollis: 🔥 x4
    • Pathophysiology & Risk:
      • Fibrosis and contracture of the Sternocleidomastoid
      • (SCM) muscle following breech or traumatic delivery.
    • Classic Exam Findings: Firm 1–2 cm lateral neck mass noticed at 2–6 w
    • Head tilted toward the affected SCM and chin rotated away.
    • 1st-Line Tx: Passive neck stretching exercises and physical therapy.
  • Perinatal Hepatitis B & Vitamin K Prophylaxis:
    • Maternal HBsAg-Positive (Acute or Chronic HBV): 🔥 x5
      • Give HBIG AND HBV vaccine at separate IM sites within 12 h of birth, .
    • Vitamin K Deficiency Bleeding: x2 2–7-day-old neonate (no birth Vit K) + bleeding
    • → isolated prolonged Prothrombin Time (PT / INR) with normal platelets.
  • Fontanelle Closure Timeline & Pediatric Trauma Airway:
    • Fontanelle Closure: x3 Posterior fontanelle closes shortly after birth (1–2 m)
    • Anterior fontanelle closes at 9–18 m.
    • Airway Opening in Unconscious Trauma Child: x2
      • Use the Modified Jaw-Thrust maneuver (without head tilt) to protect the cervical spine.
    • Definitive Airway in Severe Head Trauma: x2
      • Perform immediate endotracheal intubation if GCS ≤8 or absent gag reflex with pooling secretions.
⚠️ Model Answer vs. AI Recommended Answer

1. تنبيه بخصوص الخطوة الأولى في إنعاش المولود المتنفس تلقائياً (سؤال Q1563):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: وفقاً للإرشادات المعتمدة لإنعاش حديثي الولادة (NRP)، المولود الذي يتنفس ويبكي تلقائياً بعد الولادة السريعة يتم فوراً تجفيفه وتدفئته تحت المشع الحراري ومسح مجرى الهواء Dry and warm the infant (Option D) مع تقييم معدل ضربات القلب والتنفس. بينما في الـ Model Answer في بنك الأسئلة تم وضع Deliver respirations with bag-valve mask (Option A) بالخطأ، علماً بأن الـ Bag-Valve Mask يُستخدم فقط في حال انقطاع التنفس (Apnea) أو اللهاث (Gasping) أو هبوط النبض دون 100/دقيقة.

2. تنبيه بخصوص نسبة الضغطات إلى التهوية في الإنعاش الوليدي (سؤال Q1168):
النسبة المعتمدة دولياً لإنعاش حديثي الولادة هي 3:1 Compression-to-ventilation ratio (Option B) (أي 90 ضغطة و 30 نفساً في الدقيقة بمجموع 120 دورة/دقيقة).

Topic 2 of 12 • Pediatrics • Pediatrics

2. Neonatal Respiratory Distress & Congenital Anomalies

🔥 High Yield (28×) 17 Qs

1. Neonatal Respiratory Distress: TTN vs. RDS vs. Meconium Aspiration 🔥 x15

Feature Transient Tachypnea of the Newborn (TTN) 🔥 x10 Respiratory Distress Syndrome (RDS) x3 Meconium Aspiration Syndrome (MAS) x2
Typical Gestational Age Term or late preterm (≥37 w) Preterm (<34 w) Post-term (≥42 w)
Classic Risk Factor Elective Cesarean Section without labor Prematurity or maternal diabetes mellitus Post-maturity and intrauterine fetal distress
Pathophysiology Delayed clearance of fetal alveolar lung fluid Surfactant deficiency (L:S ratio <2:1) → alveolar collapse Meconium airway obstruction & chemical pneumonitis
Chest X-Ray Hallmark Hyperinflation, perihilar streaking, fluid in interlobar fissures Low lung volumes, reticulogranular “ground-glass” + air bronchograms Coarse, patchy bilateral infiltrates & flattened diaphragms
1st-Line Tx Supportive O2; resolves spontaneously in 24–72 h Nasal CPAP + endotracheal exogenous surfactant Supportive ventilation, suctioning, and intubation if severe

2. Congenital Diaphragmatic Hernia (CDH) 🔥 x5

  • Anatomic Defect & Pathophysiology:
    • Defect: Posterolateral diaphragmatic defect allowing abdominal viscera to herniate into the thorax.
    • Primary Cause of Mortality:
      • Compression of the developing fetal lungs resulting
      • in severe pulmonary hypoplasia and persistent pulmonary hypertension.
  • Clinical Clues, Diagnosis & Immediate Stabilization:
    • Classic Triad: Severe cyanosis/respiratory distress at birth, scaphoid (concave) abdomen
    • bowel sounds auscultated in the left chest with heart sounds displaced to the right.
    • Chest X-Ray: Air- and fluid-filled bowel loops in the left hemithorax with contralateral mediastinal shift.
    • Immediate 1st Step in Delivery Room:
      • Insert a large orogastric/nasogastric tube
        • for bowel decompression AND perform endotracheal intubation (avoid bag-mask ventilation!).

3. Esophageal Atresia with Tracheoesophageal Fistula (TEF) 🔥 x6

  • Anatomy & Clinical Presentation:
    • Most Common Subtype (Type C, 85%): Blind-ending proximal esophageal pouch with a distal tracheoesophageal fistula.
    • Prenatal & Postnatal Clues:
      • Maternal polyhydramnios + newborn with excessive oral secretions/drooling, choking, coughing
      • cyanosis immediately upon first feeding.
  • Diagnostic Confirmation & Preoperative Care:
    • Diagnostic Test of Choice:
      • Attempt to pass a radiopaque nasogastric tube
      • Chest X-Ray showing the tube coiled in the upper mediastinal pouch.
    • Preoperative Stabilization:
      • Keep strictly NPO, place infant upright, provide continuous suction of the upper pouch to prevent aspiration pneumonia
      • start IV fluids.

4. Congenital Choanal Atresia & Special NICU Protocols 🔥 x7

  • Congenital Choanal Atresia: x3
    • Pathophysiology & Hallmark Sign: Bony/membranous blockage of the posterior nasal passage
    • Because neonates are obligate nasal breathers
    • it causes cyclical cyanosis that worsens during feeding and improves with crying.
    • Initial Bedside Screening Test: Inability to pass a suction catheter through the nares into the oropharynx.
  • Retinopathy of Prematurity (ROP) & NICU Hemodynamics:
    • ROP Screening Schedule: x2
      • Screen all infants ≤1500 g or ≤30 w at chronological age 4 w OR postmenstrual age 31 w
      • whichever is later.
    • Refractory Preterm Hypotension:
      • Extremely low birth weight preterm (<1000 g) with hypotension unresponsive to fluids and vasopressors
      • → administer IV Hydrocortisone for relative adrenal insufficiency.
    • Ductal-Dependent Congenital Heart Defect:
      • Newborn with persistent cyanosis unresponsive to 100% O2 and a heart murmur
      • → start continuous IV Prostaglandin E1 (Alprostadil) infusion.
↑ Back to Top / جدول المحتويات
Topic 3 of 12 • Pediatrics • Pediatrics

3. Neonatal Jaundice, Sepsis & Necrotizing Enterocolitis

🔥 High Yield (26×) 15 Qs

1. Neonatal Jaundice: Pathologic vs. Physiologic vs. Breast Milk Jaundice 🔥 x9

Feature Pathologic Early Jaundice (<24h) 🔥 x6 Physiologic Jaundice x2 Breast Milk Jaundice
Onset Timing Within the first 24 h of life (e.g., 12 or 18 h) Appears on Day 2–3, peaks Day 4–5 Appears late (Day 4–7), peaks in Week 2–3
Primary Mechanism Immune hemolysis (Rh or ABO incompatibility), G6PD, or sepsis High fetal RBC turnover + immature hepatic UGT1A1 High beta-glucuronidase in breast milk deconjugates intestinal bilirubin
Diagnostic Clues Bilirubin rising >5 mg/dL/day, positive Direct Coombs, spherocytes Unconjugated bilirubin <12–15 mg/dL in a thriving term infant Healthy thriving breastfed infant with normal exam
1st-Line Tx Intensive phototherapy ± exchange transfusion if ≥20–25 mg/dL Reassurance and frequent feedings Continue breastfeeding; resolves by 4–12 w
  • Additional Neonatal Jaundice Pearls:
    • Breastfeeding Failure Jaundice (Suboptimal Intake):
      • Occurs in Week 1 due to inadequate milk intake, dehydration
      • delayed meconium passage (↑ enterohepatic circulation)
      • → increase breastfeeding frequency to 8–12 times/day.
    • Gilbert Syndrome Exclusion:
      • Mild hereditary UGT1A1 reduction presenting in adolescence/adulthood during fasting/stress
      • it is NOT a cause of neonatal jaundice at Day 2 of life!

2. Early-Onset Neonatal Sepsis (≤72 Hours / <7 Days) 🔥 x6

  • Microbiology & Risk Factors:
    • Most Common Pathogens: Group B Streptococcus (#1) and Escherichia coli (#2), followed by Listeria monocytogenes.
    • Perinatal Risk Factors:
      • Prolonged rupture of membranes (PROM >
      • 18 h), maternal intrapartum fever/chorioamnionitis
      • prematurity.
  • Clinical Presentation & Empiric Therapy:
    • Non-Specific Clinical Signs:
      • Temperature instability (hypothermia <
      • 36.5°C or fever), lethargy, poor feeding, apnea, grunting
      • bulging fontanelle.
    • Empiric 1st-Line Antibiotic Regimen:
      • IV Ampicillin + Gentamicin (or Ampicillin + Cefotaxime if meningitis is suspected).
    • Absolute Contraindication in Neonates (≤28 Days):
      • Never administer Ceftriaxone to neonates due to
      • albumin-bilirubin displacement (kernicterus) and fatal calcium-ceftriaxone biliary/pulmonary precipitates!

3. Infant & Childhood Bacterial Meningitis (>1–3 Months) 🔥 x5

  • Presentation & Resuscitation Priorities:
    • Clinical Signs:
      • High fever, inconsolable high-pitched crying, vomiting
      • bulging anterior fontanelle, nuchal rigidity, or non-blanching petechial/purpuric rash.
    • Immediate Step in Meningococcal Septic Shock:
      • If the child is hypotensive, tachycardic, and mottled with delayed capillary refill (>3 sec)
      • → secure IV access and administer a 20 mL/kg isotonic saline (0.9% NaCl) bolus immediately!
  • CSF Workup, Abx & Corticosteroids:
    • Diagnostic & Empiric Regimen: Perform lumbar puncture and start empiric IV Cefotaxime or Ceftriaxone (+ Vancomycin).
    • Adjunctive Dexamethasone:
      • Administer IV Dexamethasone shortly before or with the first
      • antibiotic dose to prevent sensorineural hearing loss (especially from H
      • influenzae type b and S
      • pneumoniae).

4. Necrotizing Enterocolitis (NEC) 🔥 x6

  • Risk Factors & Clinical Triad:
    • Classic Patient:
      • Premature infant (<
      • 32 w) in the NICU started on formula feedings (exclusive human breast milk is protective).
    • Clinical Clues:
      • Feeding intolerance, bilious gastric residuals/vomiting, abdominal distension, bloody stools (hematochezia), lethargy
      • hypothermia.
  • Radiographic Hallmark & Management:
    • Pathognomonic Abdominal X-Ray: Pneumatosis intestinalis ± portal venous gas.
    • Medical vs. Surgical Treatment: Medical = strictly NPO, NG decompression, IV fluids, and broad-spectrum IV Abx
    • Surgical = immediate laparotomy if pneumoperitoneum (free air) develops.
↑ Back to Top / جدول المحتويات
Topic 4 of 12 • Pediatrics • Pediatrics

4. Growth, Development, Vaccinations, Autism & ADHD

🔥 High Yield (42×) 35 Qs

1. Infant Weight Rules, BMI Screening & Short Stature Comparison 🔥 x9

  • Normal Infant Growth & Feeding Rules:
    • Weight Milestones: 🔥 x4 Birth weight doubles by 4–5 m, triples by 12 m (e.g., 3.2 kg
    • → 9.6 kg at 1 year), and quadruples by 2 years.
    • Breast Milk Adequacy Reassurance:
      • Exclusively breastfed infant gaining ≥20–30 g/day (~0.75–1 kg/month) has adequate milk intake
      • → reassure the mother.
    • Most Common Cause of Failure to Thrive: Inadequate caloric intake (decreased intake / feeding error).
    • Overweight & Obesity Screening Age ≥2 Years: Use BMI-for-age percentile (≥85th = overweight; ≥95th = obesity).
Feature Familial (Genetic) Short Stature x2 Constitutional Delay of Growth & Puberty
Parental Height Short stature in one or both parents Normal parental height (often history of “late bloomer” parent)
Linear Growth Velocity Normal (tracks parallel to and just below 5th percentile) Normal in childhood after initial deceleration in infancy
Bone Age vs. Chronological Age Bone Age = Chronological Age (never delayed or advanced!) Bone Age < Chronological Age (delayed bone age)
Pubertal Timing & Final Height Normal puberty; adult height remains short (matches target height) Delayed puberty & growth spurt; reaches normal adult height

2. High-Yield Developmental Milestones Table (Birth to 4 Years) 🔥 x20

Age Gross Motor Fine Motor Speech & Social
4–6 Months x2 Rolls over; steady head control; Moro reflex disappears by 4–6m Reaches for objects; palmar grasp Laughs aloud, babbles consonants
9 Months 🔥 x6 Sits unsupported; crawls (skipping crawling is normal!); pulls to stand Immature pincer grasp; bangs two cubes together “Mama/Dada” nonspecific; waves “bye-bye”; stranger anxiety
12 Months (1 Year) 🔥 x5 Stands unsupported; walks with 1 hand held Mature 2-finger pincer grasp 1–3 words; drinks from cup; no stranger anxiety is a red flag!
18 Months x2 Walks independently; runs stiffly Tower of 3–4 cubes; feeds self with spoon 10–25 words; points to body parts; kisses parents & helps in house
2 Years (24 Months) x3 Runs well; climbs stairs 2 feet per step; kicks ball Tower of 6–7 cubes; turns pages singly 2-word phrases (≥50 words; only 10 words at 2y = speech delay)
4 Years x2 Descends stairs alternating feet (like adults); hops on 1 foot Copies a cross (+); buttons clothes Tells stories; cooperative play

3. Autism Spectrum Disorder (ASD) 🔥 x14

  • Core DSM-5 Diagnostic Dyad:
    • 1. Social Communication & Interaction Deficits:
      • Lack of eye contact, failure to respond to name by 12 m, absence of pointing/joint attention, solitary play
      • severe speech delay (e.g., saying only “mama/dada” at age 3).
    • 2. Restricted, Repetitive Patterns of Behavior:
      • Stereotyped motor movements (hand flapping, rocking), echolalia, lining up toys
      • fixated interests (e.g., spending h watching a spinning washing machine).
  • Screening vs. Confirmatory Workup & Management:
    • Primary-Care Screening Tool (16–30 Months): M-CHAT (Modified Checklist for Autism in Toddlers).
    • Definitive Confirmatory Diagnosis:
      • Referral to a child psychiatrist/psychologist
      • for a comprehensive multidisciplinary developmental assessment.
    • 1st-Line Tx: Early intensive behavioral and speech-language therapy (Applied Behavior Analysis — ABA).

4. ADHD, Tourette Syndrome & Immunization Rules 🔥 x10

  • Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (ADHD): 🔥 x5
    • Core Symptoms: Persistent inattention and/or hyperactivity-impulsivity lasting ≥6 m with onset before age 12 years.
    • Mandatory Cross-Setting Rule: x3 Symptoms MUST be present in at least two settings (e.g., both at home AND at school).
    • First-Line Treatment (≥6 Years): Behavioral therapy combined with CNS stimulants (Methylphenidate / Amphetamines).
  • Tourette Syndrome: x2
    • Diagnostic Criteria:
      • Both multiple motor tics AND at least one vocal/phonic tic persisting for >
      • 1 year with onset before age 18.
  • Egyptian Compulsory Vaccination Schedule & Maternal Drugs:
    • 6-Month Immunization Visit: x2 Administer Oral Polio Vaccine (OPV) + Pentavalent Vaccine (DPT + Hep B + Hib) + IPV.
    • Drug Contraindicated in Breastfeeding: Cyclosporine (immunosuppressant) is contraindicated during lactation.
⚠️ Model Answer vs. AI Recommended Answer

1. تنبيه بخصوص تقييم فرط الوزن والسمنة عند الأطفال بعمر 5 سنوات (سؤال Q1318):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: المعيار المعتمد عالمياً (CDC/WHO/AAP) لتقييم الوزن لدى الأطفال بعمر عامين فما فوق (وهنا طفلة 5 سنوات) هو مؤشر كتلة الجسم بالنسبة للعمر BMI-for-age percentile (Option A). بينما في الـ Model Answer في بنك الأسئلة تم وضع Option B (Weight for height)، علماً بأن الوزن مقابل الطول يُستخدم فقط للرضع دون عمر السنتين.

2. تنبيه بخصوص تطور الحركة الكبرى عند عمر 9 أشهر (سؤال Q1228):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: المهارة الحركية الكبرى النموذجية لعمر 9 أشهر هي الحبو Crawling (Option E) (أو الجلوس المستقل والوقوف بمساعدة). وفي السؤالين المكررين Q1437 و Q1438 تم تأكيد ذلك، بينما في الـ Model Answer في Q1228 اختار النموذج خطأ Option A (Rolling over) وهو تطور يحدث مبكراً في عمر 4–5 أشهر.

3. تنبيه بخصوص تأكيد تشخيص طيف التوحد (سؤال Q1245):
علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer: طفل عمره 3 سنوات تم تحويله إلى طبيب نفسي للأطفال للاشتباه في التوحد، الفحص المطلوب لتأكيد التشخيص قطيعاً هو Comprehensive multidisciplinary developmental assessment (Option C). بينما الـ Model Answer اختار Option B (M-CHAT)، علماً بأن M-CHAT هو مجرد استبيان مسحي أولي للأهل في الرعاية الأولية بعمر 16–30 شهراً وليس الفحص التأكيدي لدى الطبيب النفسي.

↑ Back to Top / جدول المحتويات
Topic 5 of 12 • Pediatrics • Pediatrics

5. Nutrition, Rickets, Vitamins & Cow's Milk Anemia

🔥 High Yield (32×) 24 Qs

1. Cow’s Milk Iron Deficiency Anemia & Cow’s Milk Protein Allergy 🔥 x16

  • Cow’s Milk-Induced Iron Deficiency Anemia: 🔥 x12
    • Classic Patient & Dietary Trigger:
      • 9–24-month-old toddler consuming excessive unmodified cow’s milk (>
      • 24 oz / day) or exclusively breastfed past 6 m without iron-rich complementary foods.
    • (1) Cow’s milk is poor in bioavailable iron
    • (2) High calcium and casein inhibit non-heme iron absorption, and
    • (3) Subclinical cow’s milk enteropathy causes occult microscopic GI blood loss.
    • Clinical & Lab Profile: Progressive pallor, irritability, pica, tachycardia, systolic flow murmur
    • microcytic hypochromic anemia (low Hb, low MCV, high RDW, low serum ferritin).
    • First Diagnostic & Therapeutic Steps:
      • Ask about the child’s usual daily diet, order iron studies / CBC, start oral elemental Iron (3–6 mg/kg/day)
      • limit cow’s milk to <16–20 oz/day.
  • Cow’s Milk Protein Allergy (Allergic Proctocolitis): 🔥 x4
    • Presentation:
      • 2–8-week-old infant fed cow’s milk formula → postprandial crying/colic, regurgitation, blood- and mucus-streaked stools
      • atopic dermatitis (eczema).
    • Management:
      • Eliminate dairy from the breastfeeding mother’s diet or
      • switch formula-fed infants to an extensively hydrolyzed protein formula
    • Never introduce unmodified cow’s milk before 12 m of age.

2. Nutritional Vitamin D Deficiency Rickets 🔥 x8

  • Etiology & Risk Factors:
    • Primary Cause:
      • Prolonged exclusive breastfeeding without Vitamin D supplementation
      • inadequate sunlight exposure or dark skin pigmentation.
  • Skeletal, Cranial & Neuromuscular Clinical Signs:
    • Skull & Thorax:
      • Craniotabes, frontal bossing, delayed anterior fontanelle closure, delayed dentition, costochondral beading
      • Harrison groove.
    • Extremities & Motor Delay: Widened wrists and ankles, delayed sitting/walking
    • progressive genu varum (bowed legs) once weight-bearing.
    • Hypocalcemic Tetany / Seizures:
      • Infant with generalized tonic-clonic convulsions or carpopedal spasm + rachitic signs
      • → check urgent Serum Calcium!
  • Radiographic, Biochemical & Preventive Rules:
    • Wrist X-Ray Hallmarks: Metaphyseal cupping, fraying, and splaying with widened epiphyseal growth plate.
    • Key Biochemical Marker:
      • Markedly elevated Serum Alkaline Phosphatase (ALP), elevated PTH, low serum phosphorus
      • low-to-normal calcium.
    • Universal Prophylaxis:
      • All exclusively breastfed infants require 400 IU/day
      • of oral Vitamin D starting shortly after birth.

3. Scurvy (Vitamin C), Vitamin A & Vitamin B12 Deficiencies x3

Feature Scurvy (Vitamin C) x1 Vitamin A Deficiency x1 Vitamin B12 Deficiency x1
Dietary Risk Factor Diet restricted to bread/water or boiled milk without citrus/fruits Severe malnutrition or fat malabsorption Infant exclusively breastfed by a strict vegan mother
Pathophysiology Defective procollagen hydroxylation → capillary & periosteal fragility Impaired rhodopsin regeneration & squamous metaplasia of conjunctiva Impaired DNA synthesis (folate trap) & CNS demyelination
Classic Clinical Clues Swollen bleeding gums, perifollicular petechiae, corkscrew hairs, subperiosteal bleeding Night blindness, dry cornea (xerophthalmia), and foamy white conjunctival plaques Pallor, hypotonia, lethargy, developmental regression, and macrocytic megaloblastic anemia
First-Line Treatment Oral Ascorbic Acid (Vitamin C) High-dose oral Vitamin A Parenteral or oral Vitamin B12 (Cyanocobalamin)

4. Protein-Energy Malnutrition: Marasmus vs. Kwashiorkor x2

Feature Marasmus x2 Kwashiorkor
Primary Nutritional Deficit Severe deprivation of total calories (all macronutrients) Severe deprivation of dietary protein despite starchy caloric intake
Wellcome Weight-for-Age <60% of expected weight-for-age 60–80% of expected weight-for-age (<60% with edema = Marasmic Kwashiorkor)
Pitting Edema Absent Present (hypoalbuminemic dependent & periorbital edema + ascites)
SC Fat & Exam Severe muscle & fat wasting; fat is lost 1st from Abdomen/Trunk → Limbs → Buccal pad last SC fat preserved; fatty hepatomegaly, “flaky-paint” dermatitis, and reddish-orange hair

Topic 6 of 12 • Pediatrics • Pediatrics

6. Genetics, Metabolic & Pediatric Endocrine Disorders

🔥 High Yield (40×) 34 Qs

1. Chromosomal Syndromes: Down vs. Turner vs. Klinefelter 🔥 x14

Feature Down Syndrome (Trisomy 21) 🔥 x7 Turner Syndrome (45,X) 🔥 x5 Klinefelter Syndrome (47,XXY) x2
Karyotype & Genetics 95% nondisjunction; 4% Robertsonian translocation 45,X (complete or partial monosomy X in females) 47,XXY (male with extra X chromosome / Barr body)
Craniofacial & Neck Clues Upslanting palpebral fissures, epicanthal folds, Brushfield spots, flat nasal bridge Webbed neck (pterygium colli), low posterior hairline, cystic hygroma Sparse facial hair; normal facial features
Stature & Habitual Clues Short stature, hypotonia, single palmar (simian) crease, wide gap between 1st & 2nd toes Short stature, broad shield chest with widely spaced nipples, cubitus valgus Tall eunuchoid stature (long legs), gynecomastia, small firm testes
Associated Organ Anomalies Common AV canal (AVSD), duodenal atresia, hypothyroidism, acute leukemia (order CBC!) Streak ovaries (primary amenorrhea), bicuspid aortic valve, coarctation of aorta Hypergonadotropic hypogonadism (azoospermia, infertility)
Management Karyotyping, neonatal echocardiogram, and thyroid screening Recombinant Growth Hormone therapy + estrogen replacement at puberty Testosterone replacement therapy starting at puberty

2. Pediatric Endocrine Emergencies: Type 1 DM, DKA & Central DI 🔥 x11

  • Type 1 Diabetes Mellitus & Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA): 🔥 x10
    • Clinical Triad: Polyuria, polydipsia, weight loss, abdominal pain, vomiting, fruity acetone breath
    • deep rapid Kussmaul respirations.
    • Immediate 1st Step in DKA Management: 🔥 x5
      • Initial volume expansion with IV Isotonic Saline BEFORE starting IV regular insulin infusion.
    • Potassium (K+) Management in DKA (Life-Saving Next Step): 🔥 x3
      • Total body K+ is always profoundly depleted (3–5 mmol/kg). Although serum K+ may initially appear normal (e.g., 4.1 mmol/L), IV insulin rapidly shifts K+ intracellularly.
      • Once urine output is confirmed and K+ < 5.2–5.5 mmol/L, promptly add Potassium (20–40 mEq/L) to IV fluids to prevent lethal hypokalemic arrhythmias.
      • Glucose Threshold vs. Potassium: After hours of insulin when glucose falls from 650 to 450 mg/dL, the next step is Add Potassium to IV fluids; do NOT add dextrose yet (dextrose is only added when glucose drops to ≤200–250 mg/dL).
    • When Blood Glucose Reaches ≤200–250 mg/dL: x2
      • Add 5% Dextrose (glucose) to the IV fluids
        • while continuing the IV insulin infusion until ketosis and acidosis resolve!
    • Most Feared Pediatric DKA Complication:
      • Cerebral Edema (headache, bradycardia, hypertension, altered mental status 4–12h into therapy)
      • → treat immediately with IV Mannitol or 3% Hypertonic Saline.
  • Central Diabetes Insipidus:
    • Distinguishing Profile:
      • Severe polydipsia and hourly polyuria (nocturnal enuresis) with normal blood glucose (90 mg/dL), hypernatremia
      • very low urine specific gravity (<1.005)
      • → treat with intranasal Desmopressin (DDAVP).

3. Congenital Hypothyroidism, CAH & DiGeorge Syndrome 🔥 x11

  • Congenital Hypothyroidism (Cretinism): 🔥 x4
    • Etiology: Thyroid dysgenesis (agenesis, hypoplasia, or lingual ectopy in 85%) or dyshormonogenesis.
    • Clinical Clues:
      • Prolonged neonatal jaundice, constipation, lethargy, poor feeding
      • macroglossia, hoarse cry, umbilical hernia, hypotonia
      • wide fontanelles.
    • Screening & Treatment: Newborn heel-prick screen shows elevated TSH and low Free T4
    • Start immediate oral Levothyroxine to prevent irreversible intellectual disability.
  • Congenital Adrenal Hyperplasia (CAH) & Disorders of Sex Development: x3
    • 21-Hydroxylase Deficiency (90% of CAH): Autosomal recessive deficiency causing decreased cortisol &
    • Aldosterone and excess adrenal androgens.
    • Classic Presentation: 46,XX female newborn with ambiguous genitalia + neonatal salt-wasting crisis at 1–3 w.
    • Diagnosis & Treatment: Markedly elevated serum 17-hydroxyprogesterone
    • Treat with IV 0.9% NaCl + hydrocortisone + fludrocortisone.
    • 46,XY Newborn with Ambiguous Genitalia:
      • Most commonly caused by impaired androgen synthesis/action.
  • DiGeorge Syndrome (22q11.2 Deletion Syndrome): 🔥 x4
    • Embryology: Failed development of the 3rd and 4th pharyngeal pouches.
    • Classic Features (CATCH-22):
      • Conotruncal cardiac anomalies, Thymic aplasia/hypoplasia, Cleft palate
      • Parathyroid hypoplasia causing hypocalcemic tetany/seizures (e.g., 6 days post-cardiac surgery).

4. Precocious Puberty, Inborn Errors of Metabolism & Neck Masses 🔥 x10

  • Central Precocious Puberty vs. McCune-Albright Syndrome: 🔥 x4
    • Central Precocious Puberty:
      • Early activation of pulsatile hypothalamic GnRH causing breast/pubic hair development before
      • age 8 in girls with growth acceleration and advanced bone age
      • → treat with continuous GnRH agonist (Leuprolide).
    • McCune-Albright Syndrome:
      • Somatic activating GNAS mutation causing the classic triad of peripheral precocious puberty
      • irregular café-au-lait macules
      • polyostotic fibrous dysplasia.
  • Phenylketonuria (PKU) & Glycogen Storage Disease:
    • Phenylketonuria (PKU): x2 Autosomal recessive phenylalanine hydroxylase deficiency
    • → fair skin/blond hair, blue eyes, eczema, intellectual disability, seizures, and musty/mousy urine odor
    • Phenylalanine-restricted diet (recurrence risk in AR conditions such as albinism/PKU = 25% or 0.25).
    • Von Gierke Disease (GSD Type I):
      • Glucose-6-phosphatase deficiency presenting at 3–4 m with fasting hypoglycemic seizures, doll-like cheeks
      • massive hepatomegaly, lactic acidosis
      • hyperuricemia.
  • Pediatric Neck Masses:
    • Thyroglossal Duct Cyst:
      • Painless midline neck mass that moves upward with tongue protrusion/swallowing
      • → initial imaging is Neck US.
    • Pediatric Solitary Thyroid Nodule + Cervical Lymphadenopathy: Most likely diagnosis is Papillary Thyroid Carcinoma.
  • Classic Galactosemia (GALT Deficiency): x1
    • 20-day breastfed neonate with jaundice, vomiting, hepatomegaly, pyuria &
    • non-glucose reducing substances in urine.
⚠️ Model Answer vs. AI Recommended Answer

تنبيه هام للامتحان بخصوص بروتوكول الحماض الكيتوني السكري DKA (سؤال Q1422):

علمياً ووفقاً للـ AI Recommended Answer ومذكرة د. أحمد عامر: في علاج الـ DKA، بعد بدء الإنعاش الأولي بالمحاليل الوريدية وحقن الإنسولين، يدخل البوتاسيوم بسرعة فائقة إلى داخل الخلايا مسبباً هبوطاً حاداً ومميتاً في بوتاسيوم الدم. لذا فإن الإجراء الفوري الإلزامي هو إضافة البوتاسيوم إلى المحاليل الوريدية Add potassium to IV solution (Option B) بمجرد أن يصبح البوتاسيوم أقل من 5.2 mEq/L لتجنب توقف القلب.

في الـ Model Answer في بعض النماذج القديمة: كان يشار إلى إضافة الجلوكوز Option A (Add glucose)، علماً بأن الجلوكوز لا يُضاف مطلقاً في البداية، وإنما يُضاف فقط عندما ينخفض سكر الدم إلى 250–300 mg/dL لمنع هبوط السكر والوذمة الدماغية.
